# Netty — technique cards (176)

Each card is exactly 7 lines. Fetch one with: grep -A 6 "^### netty-NNN " netty.md

### netty-001 — Use linear-time search algorithms for delimiters and substrings
Lens: algorithms · Source: Netty
Principle: Naive substring or multi-delimiter search is O(n·m), and degrades further if it restarts on every read. Use a dedicated single-pass scanner for the common case, and KMP, Two-Way, Bitap or Aho-Corasick for general needles.
Why it is faster: Linear-time algorithms look at each haystack byte a bounded number of times however many partial matches occur. Two-Way needs O(1) extra memory. Bitap packs up to 64 needle positions into one machine word. Aho-Corasick finds many needles in one pass. For a single-byte delimiter, a plain `memchr`-style loop beats all of them, so detect that case and route it to the specialised scanner. On short inputs the setup cost of the advanced algorithms can outweigh their benefit.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Multipart boundary search (Boyer-Moore or Two-Way), and WAF or keyword filters matching many patterns at once (Aho-Corasick). • Native / systems: `memchr`/`memmem` for newline and token splitting in log shippers; grep-like tools. • Data / databases: CSV/TSV ingest with multi-character delimiters, and `LIKE '%needle%'` scans on large text columns. • Embedded: Bitap for short fixed patterns: it needs only one word of state and no tables.
Watch out: Benchmark with realistic needle lengths: for 1-3 byte needles a tight byte or SWAR loop usually wins. Automatic substitution must keep the original semantics exactly, including edge cases such as overlapping delimiters.
Seen in Netty: d7d8503f5c 2012-11-25 `DelimiterBasedFrameDecoder` configured with line delimiters had quadratic performance, spending almost 100% of CPU in its `indexOf()`. A new `LineBasedFrameDecoder` scans once, and `isLineBased(delimiters)` switches to it automatically. OpenTSDB reported an order-of-magnitude throughput gain. • abdcf102da 2020-04-15 Added `io.netty.buffer.search` with KMP, Bitap and Aho-Corasick implemented as `ByteProcessor`s, with `SearchBenchmark`/`SearchRealDataBenchmark`. It reported order-of-magnitude gains, especially on composite buffers. • 3273679e5f 2021-06-28 `ByteBufUtil.indexOf(ByteBuf, ByteBuf)` switched to the Two-Way algorithm (linear time, constant space). • 2427a94bfa 2023-06-16 `DelimiterBasedFrameDecoder` dropped its own O(nm) loop in favour of the shared linear search.

### netty-002 — Open-addressing hash tables over primitive arrays, hashed on the full key
Lens: algorithms · Source: Netty
Principle: For integer keys, store keys and values in flat parallel arrays with linear probing and a power-of-two mask. Hash on the whole lookup key, and size the table for a low load factor.
Why it is faster: Generic maps box every primitive key and allocate one node per entry, so each lookup chases pointers across the heap. Flat arrays keep a short probe sequence within a cache line or two and allocate nothing per operation. Masking with `len-1` replaces two modulus operations per probe, and wrap-around becomes branch-free. If you hash only part of a composite key (a header name shared by many values), all those entries land in one bucket and lookup degrades to O(n). The trade-offs: deletion needs compaction or tombstones, and a bad hash produces long clusters.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: File-descriptor or connection-id tables in C reactors (khash, or a plain array when ids are dense). • Web / backend: In JS, an `Int32Array`-backed map for numeric ids instead of a `Map<number, …>` in hot paths. In Java, fastutil/HPPC-style collections. • Data / databases: Hash-join and group-by tables over integer keys use flat open addressing with a low load factor. For a composite index, hash all of its columns. • Embedded: Fixed-capacity open-addressing tables in static RAM, with no per-entry allocation.
Watch out: Linear probing is very sensitive to the hash function and to the load factor: above about 0.7 probe lengths explode. With a power-of-two size, mix the hash well, or keys with the same low bits collide. Removal must compact the cluster, or later lookups miss entries.
Seen in Netty: 02a6dc8ba7 2014-06-10 Added `IntObjectHashMap`. 07024a4e4b 2014-07-19 rewrote it with linear probing, no state array (a sentinel stands for null values), compaction on removal and an iterator that does not allocate an entry per `next()`. 30060b6083 2014-10-20 / f3ef94d35e 2014-10-20 made the index computation branch-free. • 31211487e9 2014-06-25 The epoll transport's fd-to-channel map uses `IntObjectHashMap`, so no `Integer` is boxed per event. • 3354296c9f 2015-04-03 Templates generate the maps for each primitive key type (`IntObjectHashMap`, `CharObjectHashMap`, ...). ab158a6ea4 2015-04-10 added benchmarks. 7aac50a79a 2015-04-13 uses masks instead of two `%` per probe, and c5faa142fb 2016-05-08 makes `probeNext = (index+1) & mask` with no conditionals. • 86730f53ca 2020-11-29 The pooled allocator replaced boxed collections with `LongLongHashMap` (linear probing kept within a couple of cache lines) and `LongPriorityQueue` (a binary heap over `long[]`): about 13% faster allocations that miss the cache. 32627d712a 2020-11-03 had found an unboxed `long` being re-boxed for `PriorityQueue<Long>.remove`. 78cbf3a3de 2023-07-26 narrowed the heap to `int`s, storing only the needed 32 bits. • 0df408ec45 2022-10-14 `HpackEncoder`: entries hashed only by name collapsed into one bucket when many headers shared a name. It now keeps a second table keyed by name+value and raised the default buckets from 16 to 64 (the 4096-byte table holds more than 64 headers, a load factor above 4). • 6af56ffe76 2016-06-23 The HPACK encoder table is forced to a power-of-two size so `&` replaces `%`. 2233eaa7ac 2025-08-09 replaced a binary search over thread ids with a `LongLongHashMap` keyed by `threadId>>>6` holding a 64-bit membership bitmap, giving an O(1) check. • fd0ae840b6 2016-12-01 A concurrent map's value became a singleton `LeakEntry` with identity `equals` and a cached hash, so `remove(key, value)` no longer does `instanceof`, a cast and `Boolean.equals`.

### netty-003 — Design multimaps for their real access pattern (insert, look up a few, iterate once)
Lens: algorithms · Source: Netty
Principle: Small string-keyed multimaps such as protocol headers are filled once, queried a few times, then iterated in insertion order. A fixed bucket array plus an insertion-ordered linked list fits that pattern better than a tree or a resizing map, and immutable flat arrays fit read-only outbound sets best.
Why it is faster: A `TreeMap` with a case-insensitive comparator costs O(log n) full string compares per lookup. A small fixed bucket table with a case-folding hash gives O(1) lookups without resizing, and threading entries on a doubly linked list keeps insertion order with no extra pass. If iteration needs a special order (pseudo-headers first), keep a second chain in that order as you insert, instead of filtering twice. Predicate queries that walk the hash chain directly avoid building a value list or an iterator. The trade-off: each extra chain makes inserts slower.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: HTTP header and query-parameter containers in frameworks. In JS, a flat `[k, v, k, v]` array beats a `Map` for fewer than about 20 entries that are mostly iterated. • Data / databases: Per-row attribute maps and small option bags, plus LRU caches (a hash plus a recency list, the same pattern). • Embedded: A small fixed bucket array with intrusive lists for MQTT or CoAP options, with no rehashing and no heap growth.
Watch out: A fixed bucket count degrades if real inputs are much larger than expected (attacker-controlled header floods), so cap the entry count or randomise the hash. Every added ordering chain taxes inserts, so measure which operation dominates.
Seen in Netty: 4ede085edc 2010-01-08 `HttpHeaders`: a `TreeMap` was replaced by a custom linked hash map with 17 buckets, an ASCII case-folding hash, case-insensitive equality compared from the end, and a sentinel head for the before/after list. 1a04586b54 2010-02-19 removed a branch in the bucket insert, and 5fb8e8069b 2009-06-15 sized per-name value lists for one value (`new ArrayList(1)`). • 6fd0a0c55f 2015-04-14 A `TreeMap`-based header implementation saved about 30% memory (up to 1.2 KB per gRPC call), but ba6ce5449e 2015-08-12 restored the bucket array with the `head.before/after` list to recover the speed. 6e108cb96a 2015-10-30 skips name validation and rehashing when copying between headers of the same kind. • 06e7627b5f 2016-11-12 `ReadOnlyHttp2Headers` is backed by a flat `AsciiString[]` of name/value pairs: 20 client headers went from 392.9 to 117.5 ns/op. • 1542b36e80 2013-10-11 / e6eb7fa466 2013-10-11 Header iterators walk the linked entries directly. ff6a06a9be 2013-10-11 `contains(name, value, ignoreCase)` no longer builds a `LinkedList`, a3e41ba6eb 2017-11-20 / 0a47c590fe 2017-11-11 added value iterators so `contains` needs neither `getAll()` nor a regex, and 874c99529e 2026-02-03 added `containsAny(name, arg, BiPredicate)`, which walks the chain with a static, non-capturing predicate, for the keep-alive check on every request. • 414c0f2e50 2022-09-29 `DefaultHttp2Headers` (netty5) threads a second linked list that keeps pseudo-headers first: iteration became more than 700% faster, while add/addAll got 22-44% slower with 30 headers. The commit accepts that trade-off.

### netty-004 — Match the queue to the producer/consumer topology, and avoid O(n) queue queries
Lens: algorithms · Source: Netty
Principle: Choose the queue whose guarantees match exactly what the code needs: MPSC for many producers feeding one event loop, SPSC for a pipe between two threads, MPMC only when several consumers really exist. Never call operations that walk the structure, such as `size()`, on a hot path.
Why it is faster: A general-purpose concurrent or blocking queue pays for guarantees nobody uses: locks, CAS on both ends, and a node allocation per offer. An MPSC queue makes producers do one atomic swap and lets the single consumer use plain reads. An SPSC queue needs no atomic read-modify-write at all. On lock-free linked queues, `size()` and sometimes `isEmpty()` traverse every node, so a "cheap" check becomes O(n). Weakening the guarantees is only safe while the topology stays as assumed.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Vyukov MPSC queues for actor mailboxes and for job submission to a render or I/O thread. SPSC rings between an interrupt or capture thread and a worker. • Web / backend: In Rust, choose the crossbeam or tokio channel flavour (mpsc, oneshot) by topology. In Go, avoid `len(ch)`-driven logic. • Embedded: SPSC ring buffers between an ISR and the main loop need only correctly ordered index stores, with no locks or atomic read-modify-write. • Data / databases: Log-record hand-off from many sessions to one WAL writer.
Watch out: An MPSC queue used by two consumers corrupts silently, so enforce the topology in code. Vendored queue copies age badly: Netty went back and forth several times, so benchmark against the current stdlib before keeping a fork.
Seen in Netty: f6baf157df 2011-11-30 Removed an `isEmpty()` check that "may be really expensive (need to traverse all elements in the queue)". 3379a4c25d 2011-12-06 replaced a backported `LinkedTransferQueue` with the Java 7 one "to avoid high cpu usage". 3ff8cd46c9 2009-12-24 had backported a newer version, and 42afa85e53 2009-02-03 moved its rare synchronized fallback into its own method so the JVM could optimise the hot node class. ba1c7c5c55 2012-07-30 went back to the JDK's `ConcurrentLinkedQueue`/`LinkedBlockingQueue`. • d3ffa1b02b 2014-02-26 Added `MpscLinkedQueue` for the NIO and epoll event loops, producing less garbage than `ConcurrentLinkedQueue`. 09100e5043 2014-08-18 stopped re-reading the volatile head in `peekNode`, cutting volatile accesses. • 81fee66c78 2015-05-19 Forked JCTools' `MpscArrayQueue` so buffers freed on another thread return to the owner's allocation cache. The author noted the same-thread case got slightly slower, an explicit trade-off. • c7aadb5469 2016-02-18 Forked JCTools' `SpscLinkedQueue` for `LocalChannel`, and c3abb9146e 2016-03-27 shaded JCTools. e276cb796c 2015-05-06 made `LocalChannel` use an MPSC queue as its inbound buffer, removing a memory copy. 28c39a3183 2016-12-20 switched a queue to MPMC where several consumers did exist (a correctness fix). • ad85f55793 2025-06-22 `SingleThreadIoEventLoop.newTaskQueue` returns an MPSC queue instead of a `LinkedBlockingQueue`: wait-free producers, no node allocation, and no lock-induced deadlocks under virtual-thread schedulers. 36dbdacbed 2024-11-01 used JCTools' unpadded MPSC queues for many lightly contended caches, where padding would cost more memory than false sharing costs time.

### netty-005 — Make the element its own container node (intrusive structures)
Lens: algorithms · Source: Netty
Principle: Let the object being queued, scheduled or tracked carry its own link fields, so inserting it into a list, queue or timer bucket allocates nothing, and removing it is O(1) without a search.
Why it is faster: A non-intrusive container allocates a wrapper node per insert and must search to remove a given element. With embedded links, enqueueing is a few pointer writes, and the element can unlink itself in O(1) because it knows its neighbours. Locality improves too, because the payload and its links share cache lines. The cost is that an object can usually be in only as many containers as it has link fields, and the links must be cleared on removal.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Linux `list_head`/`llist_node` embedded in work items and timers, and intrusive LRU links in cache entries. • Embedded: RTOS task control blocks carrying their own ready-list and delay-list links, so scheduling needs no allocation. • Web / backend: Request objects that carry their own timeout-list links (an idle-connection list in a proxy). Rust's `intrusive-collections`. • Data / databases: Buffer-pool page descriptors with embedded LRU and dirty-list links.
Watch out: Intrusive nodes tie the element type to the container: a task cannot be in two queues at once. Stale links after removal cause use-after-free bugs in native code and retained garbage in managed runtimes, so null them out.
Seen in Netty: d3ffa1b02b 2014-02-26 `OneTimeTask` is both a `Runnable` and a node of `MpscLinkedQueue`, so submitting it to the event loop allocates no wrapper node. 7bee318fc7 2015-11-19 made `OneTimeTask extends MpscLinkedQueueNode` and switched 20 files from anonymous `Runnable`s to it. • 2f7d60f234 2014-05-02 `HashedWheelTimer` keeps timeouts in intrusive doubly linked bucket lists, so cancellation unlinks in O(1). An empty timer had previously cost about 250 KB. • feb804dca8 2019-11-04 A `ScheduledFutureTask` scheduled from another thread enqueues itself into the task queue, and its `run()` adds or removes it from the scheduled queue; no second `Runnable` is allocated. 19b4adf79c 2019-10-17 stores the user's `Runnable` directly in an `Object` field instead of wrapping it in a `Callable` adapter. • 59f11ed64f 2012-05-25 `DefaultChannelFuture` implements `FlushCheckpoint` itself, so tracking a pending flush allocates no separate entry.

### netty-006 — Exploit closed key sets: perfect hashing, switches and first-byte dispatch
Lens: algorithms · Source: Netty
Principle: When the set of keys is fixed at build time (HTTP methods, static header tables, cipher names, month names), choose a lookup that cannot collide: a perfect hash, a `switch` on length or first character, or a position index. Compare in full only once, against the single candidate.
Why it is faster: A general map on a fixed vocabulary pays for chaining, resizing, boxing and resident memory it never needs. A linear sequence of `equals()` calls does several full string compares. A perfect hash costs one hash, one mask, one compare. A switch on the first character or the length prunes almost every candidate with one branch. If entries with the same name sit next to each other in a static table, you can stop at the first mismatch. The table must be verified when it is built: a new key that collides breaks the scheme, so fail fast at startup.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Routers and HTTP method parsing: switch on the first byte or the length, then do one `memcmp`. In JS, a `switch` beats `Object.keys(...).find`. • Native / systems: Keyword recognition in lexers and config parsers with gperf-generated perfect hashes, and opcode tables in interpreters. • Data / databases: SQL keyword and type-name lookup in the tokenizer, and static catalogs looked up by name. • Embedded: AT-command or CLI command dispatch via a switch on the first character, using no RAM for tables.
Watch out: Perfect hashes are brittle: adding a key can create a collision, so verify the table at startup or in a test. Always do a final full compare. Never return a match on hash equality alone.
Seen in Netty: 2ac79dcb74 2010-04-16 `HttpClientCodec` switches on `method.getName().charAt(0)` ('H' for HEAD, 'C' for CONNECT) before calling `equals`. a979433f91 2009-06-15 uses `switch (line.charAt(0))` instead of `startsWith`, and f059f91ff4 2009-06-15 restructures the header loop the same way. • 93b144b7b4 2017-11-11 `HttpMethod.valueOf` uses a perfect hash (`hashCode() >>> 6` masked to 16 slots): about 10% faster (known methods 29.2 to 31.8 ops/µs, mixed 21.1 to 25.6). b7ceeb1797 2019-01-30 added an `==` fast path in `equals`. 664f20315c 2025-04-02 compares against interned "GET"/"POST" first. • 2e433889b2 2019-02-04 `DateFormatter.tryParseMonth` dispatches on characters directly instead of trying 12 `regionMatches` calls: `parseHttpDate` 3-15% faster (Dec: 3.78M to 4.34M ops/s). • 2ed95c906a 2022-10-03 `HpackStaticTable` uses two perfect-hash tables (name, and name+value): lookupHttp went from 16.0 to 10.5 ns and nameOnlyMatch from 13.7 to 3.2 ns. cce3d5b636 2024-01-31 then dispatches pseudo-headers with `switch(length)` plus a reference compare against the interned constants. • 613cfcb600 2020-11-30 The QPACK static table maps each name to the list of its positions: findFieldNameRefForSingleMatch went from 4.86M to 36.3M ops/s. 85ec20ecbd 2020-12-21 makes the HPACK lookup return on the first name mismatch, because equal names are adjacent. • eec0b5d514 2025-05-21, 0a11fc1fdb 2025-07-04 and 54b8663619 2026-02-05 replaced small resident `Map`s and factory objects (TLS groups, key material, `HttpContentCompressor` encodings) with a string `switch`, which is faster and keeps no map in memory.

### netty-007 — Use the simplest structure the access pattern needs: arrays over sets and maps
Lens: algorithms · Source: Netty
Principle: If a collection is only appended to and then iterated, or holds a handful of items, use a plain array with an index loop. Hash sets, linked sets and side indexes earn their cost only when you look things up by key.
Why it is faster: A `HashSet` allocates a node per add, hashes each element and needs an iterator object to walk. An array append is one store, and indexed iteration is sequential and allocation-free. For tiny n, a linear scan of a short list beats maintaining a separate hash index, which costs memory on every instance and work on every mutation. Double-buffering two arrays (flipping each round) lets you reuse them without clearing them element by element.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Reuse one `epoll_event[]`/`kevent[]` array per loop. Hold listener lists in a small vector, not `std::set`. • Web / backend: In JS, arrays for event-listener lists and middleware stacks. A linear scan of fewer than about 16 items beats a `Map` lookup. • Embedded: Fixed arrays of handlers scanned linearly, with no allocator. • Data / databases: Small IN-lists evaluated by linear scan below a threshold, and by a hash set only above it.
Watch out: Arrays do not deduplicate. If duplicates are possible and harmful, the set was doing real work. A linear scan becomes a quadratic bug once n grows unexpectedly, so keep an eye on the upper bound.
Seen in Netty: 3ce9ab2e72 2013-06-11 `SelectedSelectionKeySet`: the JDK selector's ready-key `HashSet` was replaced, via reflection, by an add-only pair of arrays flipped each select: "much less garbage", 1-2% faster. 26124f6906 2016-11-23 simplified it to a single array reset before each select, which also tightens the loop bounds. 3c2dbdb5db 2018-09-05 kept the injection working on Java 9+ by writing the fields through `Unsafe`. daef9ba812 2022-09-17 grows the array only when the next add needs it. • 35b9aa9302 2015-04-02 HTTP/2 connection listeners moved from `LinkedHashSet` to `ArrayList`. Iterators had been about 1.6% of heap in a stream-churn test. 8b1f247a1a 2015-04-10 gave `CompositeByteBuf.iterator()` a dedicated iterator instead of copying into a new `ArrayList`. • 2d2e07578a 2015-11-15 Removed the name-to-context `HashMap` from `DefaultChannelPipeline`. Lookups walk the short linked list instead, which saves a map per connection.

### netty-008 — Sorted arrays with binary search, plus a locality cursor where access is sequential
Lens: algorithms · Source: Netty
Principle: To map an offset or key onto ranges (buffer segments, subnet rules, attribute ids), keep the boundaries in a sorted array and binary-search it. If accesses are mostly sequential, check a cached last-hit position first.
Why it is faster: Binary search over a contiguous sorted array is O(log n) with excellent cache behaviour, and it holds no mutable state, so concurrent readers are safe. A "last accessed" cursor turns sequential access into O(1), but it is shared mutable state and degrades on random access. Netty's history shows the trade-off in both directions. For read-mostly data, an immutable sorted array behind one reference, replaced by copy-on-write, gives lock-free reads.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: ACLs, GeoIP ranges and rate-limit tiers as sorted arrays. Read-mostly routing tables swapped atomically on reload. • Data / databases: Segment or extent lookup in log-structured storage and the fence pointers in SSTables, with a cursor for sequential scans. • Native / systems: Rope and piece-table editors (cursor locality plus binary search), and file systems mapping logical offsets to extents. • Embedded: Calibration tables or interrupt-priority ranges in flash, binary-searched with no RAM cost.
Watch out: Copy-on-write is O(n) per write, so it suits only read-mostly data. A shared cursor must tolerate races (benign, never trusted for correctness) or be per-reader.
Seen in Netty: 85712a72b8 2009-10-13 `CompositeChannelBuffer.componentId()` remembers the last component accessed and scans left or right from it, and multi-byte getters take a fast path when the value fits in one component. • f92cfba388 2013-05-17 `DefaultCompositeByteBuf.toComponentIndex` switched to binary search over component offsets: the cursor was stateful (unsafe for concurrent readers) and poor at random access. • 10539f4dc7 2018-11-03 `CompositeByteBuf` combines both: binary search plus a racy (benign) last-accessed cache and a fast path for index 0, with components in a raw array. 1f93bd36b6 2019-04-08 made `discardReadComponents` start at the first component instead of binary-searching. • 5d27a3d0bb 2020-09-01 `IpSubnetFilter` sorts subnet rules once and binary-searches them, instead of testing every rule. • 4624b6309d 2020-10-02 `DefaultAttributeMap` became an immutable sorted array behind a volatile reference: readers binary-search by `key.id()` with a hand-written loop (avoiding megamorphic comparator calls), and writers copy, insert and CAS. Insertion walks backwards because new ids tend to be larger.

### netty-009 — Hunt down hidden quadratic loops and repeated lookups
Lens: algorithms · Source: Netty
Principle: Look for `contains` on a list inside a loop, element-by-element bulk inserts that each shift an array, and a `containsKey` followed by a `get`. Each looks harmless at n = 10 and dominates at n = 10,000.
Why it is faster: Replacing `List.contains` deduplication with a hash set turns O(n²) into O(n). Inserting m items into an array of n with one shift instead of m shifts turns O(m·n) into O(m+n). Fusing a check-then-get into a single lookup halves the hashing and probing. These problems hide because test inputs are small, so they show up in production as event-loop stalls.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: In JS, `array.includes` inside a `map`/`filter` is O(n²), so build a `Set` first. In Python, use `dict.get` instead of `in` plus `[]`. • Data / databases: Nested-loop joins that should be hash joins, and per-row subqueries in application code (N+1). • Native / systems: `std::map::find` instead of `count` followed by `[]`, and `vector::insert(range)` instead of repeated single inserts.
Watch out: A hash set adds memory and loses order unless you use a linked variant. For genuinely tiny n the linear version may still be faster, so check the realistic maximum, not the typical size.
Seen in Netty: 4021eadab2 2026-09-10 DNS search-domain deduplication uses a `LinkedHashSet` instead of `List.contains`: 4,096 domains went from 12,196 to 147 µs. • 10539f4dc7 2018-11-03 `CompositeByteBuf` bulk insert became O(m+n) instead of O(mn). • 5b59250657 2016-04-22 Replaced a `TreeMap` double lookup with a single `get`, and c6a13e28b9 2016-08-02 made `ConstantPool.valueOf` do one `get` plus `putIfAbsent`.

### netty-010 — Allocate identifiers from a free structure, not by probing
Lens: algorithms · Source: Netty
Principle: When ids must be unique among live entries (query ids, channel ids), keep an explicit pool of free ids, or renumber once on wraparound. Random or sequential probing for an unused id gets slower as the space fills.
Why it is faster: Probing costs roughly 1/(1 − load) attempts per allocation, so it explodes as occupancy approaches 100%, often while holding the event loop. A free-list or bucketed pool hands out an id in O(1) whatever the load. If ids grow monotonically and wrap rarely, one O(n) renumbering pass at wrap time is amortised over 2³¹ cheap allocations, instead of probing on every allocation after the first wrap.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Source-port allocation in NAT, PID and fd allocators (bitmap plus next-free hint), and transaction ids in RPC clients. • Data / databases: Transaction-id wraparound handling (Postgres freezing is a deferred bulk renumber). Slot allocation in page directories. • Embedded: Message ids for CoAP/MQTT in-flight windows via a small free bitmap.
Watch out: If ids must be unpredictable (DNS, security tokens), a FIFO free list leaks state, so shuffle as Netty does. Renumbering is only possible when nothing outside the process holds the old ids.
Seen in Netty: c66da29644 2015-01-29 `EpollEventLoop` detects channel-id overflow, then scrubs and reassigns the ids of all live channels once, instead of probing for free ids on every later allocation. • af33de629f 2024-01-23 `DnsQueryIdSpace` keeps 4 lazily created buckets of 16k ids with randomised push-back, replacing a random-probe loop over an `IntObjectHashMap` that blocked the event loop when many queries were in flight. Random order is kept for DNS spoofing resistance.

### netty-011 — Detect cycles with O(1) memory instead of a visited set
Lens: algorithms · Source: Netty
Principle: To detect a loop in a chain of lookups (aliases, redirects, linked nodes), advance a second pointer at half speed and check whether the two meet (Floyd's tortoise and hare), instead of recording every visited key.
Why it is faster: A visited set allocates a collection and hashes every step. The two-pointer method needs two variables and one comparison per step, with no allocation, and still terminates within a small constant factor of the cycle length. It only works for deterministic chains, where each element has exactly one successor.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Redirect-chain and alias-resolution loops, and config inheritance chains. • Embedded: Linked-list corruption checks in firmware without heap allocation. • Native / systems: Symlink-loop detection and parent-pointer walks in trees that might be corrupt.
Watch out: It detects a cycle but does not cheaply report which element starts it. Long acyclic chains still need a separate depth limit.
Seen in Netty: bdb5e20d99 2020-05-18 `DnsResolveContext.cnameResolveFromCacheLoop` replaced a `HashSet<String>` of seen CNAMEs with an `advance` flag that moves a trailing pointer every other iteration.

### netty-012 — Run inline when already on the owning thread; enqueue (fused) only when crossing threads
Lens: scheduling · Source: Netty
Principle: Before posting work to a single-threaded executor, check whether the caller already is that executor's thread. If it is, call the code directly. If not, send one combined task that carries everything the operation needs.
Why it is faster: A hand-off to your own thread is pure overhead: a queue offer and poll, a possible task allocation, a wakeup, and a full loop iteration of added latency. The thread check is one comparison. When the hand-off is unavoidable, fusing related steps (write plus flush) into one task halves the queue traffic. Inline execution can deepen the stack and reorder work relative to tasks already queued, so it is only correct where ordering is preserved.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Reactors and actor runtimes: `if (currentThread == loopThread) run(); else post(task)`. An actor `tell` that runs immediately when the actor is idle and already scheduled on this thread. • Native / systems: GUI toolkits checking `isMainThread` before posting to the run loop. Asio's `dispatch` versus `post`. • Embedded: Calling a driver function directly from task context, and deferring to a work queue only from ISR context.
Watch out: Running inline can break FIFO order with respect to tasks already queued by other threads, and can recurse deeply (A calls B calls A). Keep the inline path for operations that are order-safe, and bound reentrancy.

It was reverted in 8e82a1621a 2010-02-03 for instability: crossing threads safely needs the queue. • 25c7a783a7 2013-02-21 Removed a condition that forced `execute()` even on the loop thread ("nice perf boost" and a race fix). 5ef30b6d8b 2013-08-07 `writeAndFlush` invokes write and flush inline, or schedules a single task with `flush=true`. • 54aa4d9b68 2021-07-08 `SslHandler` schedules delegated TLS tasks only when it is off the executor thread.

### netty-013 — Coalesce and skip wakeups of a sleeping loop
Lens: scheduling · Source: Netty
Principle: Waking a thread blocked in `select`/`epoll_wait` costs a syscall plus a context switch. Guard it with one atomic word so many producers cause at most one wakeup per sleep, never wake from the loop's own thread, and skip the wakeup when the loop will wake up early enough anyway.
Why it is faster: Without a guard, N producers issue N eventfd or pipe writes for one sleep. A compare-and-set on a "woken" flag reduces that to one. Encoding "asleep until T" in the same word lets producers skip the wakeup when their work is not due before T. Registering wakeup fds edge-triggered removes the read() needed to drain them each time. The race between "about to sleep" and "work arrived" is subtle, and getting it wrong means lost wakeups and stalls.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: libuv's `uv_async_send` coalescing. In thread pools, unpark only when the worker is actually parked. Tokio/async `Waker` deduplication. • Web / backend: Worker-thread message ports: post a single "wake" message per batch, not one per item. • Embedded: Set an RTOS event-flag bit (idempotent) instead of giving a semaphore per item. Hit a NIC doorbell register once per batch.
Watch out: Lost-wakeup races are the classic bug: the consumer must publish "I am going to sleep" before its final check for work, and producers must check after enqueueing. Netty reverted and re-fixed this ordering several times.
Seen in Netty: edd969b384 2008-08-20 "Fixed slow write performance": an `AtomicBoolean wakenUp` with `compareAndSet(false, true)` before `selector.wakeup()`, cleared in the loop. fd4de999bb 2008-08-30 "Decreased the number of wakeup() calls". ea1a68cb83 2008-11-06 sets the flag only when the selector is really woken, and 883af62c92 2012-08-31 sets it before waking. • a4c66dc282 2013-02-09 `wakeup()` checks `!inEventLoop && wakenUp.compareAndSet(false, true)`: the loop never wakes itself. • 877767da00 2016-05-14 If a task arrived while `wakenUp` was set, the next `epoll_wait` is non-blocking instead of sleeping and waiting to be woken. • 63f7854c1e 2019-05-31 timerfd and eventfd are registered with `EPOLLET`, so they never need to be read to clear them (draining only when `eventfd_write` returns EAGAIN). • f01278616a 2019-06-04 Simplified the wakeup race and stopped self-wakeups: executeMulti went from 408k to 441k ops/s and executeSingle from 157k to 168k. 166caf96ef 2019-10-13 merged `wakenUp` and the previous deadline into one `AtomicLong nextWakeupNanos` (asleep until T, or awake), skipped wakeups when an earlier timer is already armed, and removed the 1 s default timeout that forced a re-arm on every sleep.

### netty-014 — Submit effect-free tasks without waking the loop
Lens: scheduling · Source: Netty
Principle: Some tasks only need to run in order before the next task that has a visible effect. A buffered write that is followed by a flush is one example. Enqueue them without waking the consumer, and they run on its next natural iteration.
Why it is faster: A wakeup per buffered write is wasted: nothing observable happens until the flush, and the flush task will wake the loop anyway. Marking tasks as "lazy" removes most wakeup syscalls and context switches from write-heavy cross-thread traffic. The risk is a lazy task that sits until some unrelated event if no eager task ever follows.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Batching log or metric submissions to an I/O thread that will flush on its own timer anyway. • Data / databases: Write-behind queues that do not wake the flusher for each record, only on commit or when a size threshold is reached. • Native / systems: Deferred work items queued without signalling the worker (Linux's `queue_work` versus delayed or batched variants).
Watch out: Every lazy task needs a guaranteed eager successor, or a bounded maximum delay. Otherwise data sits in the queue when the producer stops.
Seen in Netty: 64c3f58279 2014-01-30 Added `wakesUpForTask(Runnable)`: a plain `WriteTask` does not wake the event loop, and only `WriteAndFlushTask` does. • a18c57ea87 2019-10-31 Added a public `LazyRunnable` marker and `lazyExecute()`, meaning "don't wake up after enqueue". • b413464091 2019-11-23 Non-flush `WriteTask`s go through `lazyExecute()`, and the two pooled task classes merged into one, with the flush flag stored in the sign bit of the size field.

### netty-015 — Hashed timing wheels for huge numbers of mostly-cancelled timeouts
Lens: scheduling · Source: Netty
Principle: When most timeouts are cancelled before they fire (I/O idle or read timeouts), hash them into buckets by tick for O(1) insert and cancel, trading exact timing for tick-level granularity.
Why it is faster: A heap costs O(log n) per insert and per cancel, and timers that are scheduled and then cancelled pay that twice for nothing. A wheel inserts into bucket `deadline mod size` and unlinks in O(1) from intrusive bucket lists. Hand-offs from many threads go through an MPSC queue, so only the wheel's thread touches buckets. Storing the absolute round in which an entry fires (rather than a remaining-rounds counter decremented each tick) means a tick does not update every entry in the bucket. Precision is limited to one tick.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Idle-connection and request timeouts in proxies (HAProxy and Envoy use wheels), and TTL expiry in in-memory caches. • Native / systems: The Linux kernel timer wheel. TCP retransmit and keepalive timers in user-space stacks. • Embedded: A single hardware tick driving a wheel of software timers, giving O(1) work per tick with intrusive lists. • Data / databases: Lock-wait and session timeouts.
Watch out: A wheel is wrong for precise or far-future timers: long delays wrap many rounds, and granularity is one tick. A busy bucket still costs O(entries) per tick. If timers mostly fire instead of being cancelled, a heap may be simpler and just as fast.
Seen in Netty: bcbd0f9238 2009-01-19 Introduced `HashedWheelTimer`. b2d27d3b69 2009-01-20 optimised it: 100 ms × 512 ticks by default, reusable iterators "to avoid Iterator creation overhead", and a per-entry `remainingRounds`. 3e0d1f69b0 2009-01-20 minimised `rewind()` calls. • 2f7d60f234 2014-05-02 Rewrote it: producers push timeouts onto an MPSC queue, and the worker moves them into intrusive doubly linked buckets. The read/write lock and latch are gone, and an empty timer no longer costs about 250 KB. 3d81afb8a5 2014-06-05 added a cancellation queue drained every tick, so cancelled timeouts become garbage immediately instead of after a full rotation. • 85b63a00a6 2022-10-14 Replaced `remainingRounds` with an absolute `execRound` and a global `currRound`. Expiry compares the two and can stop early instead of decrementing every entry. • 7c4311df5c 2024-01-29 Wheel sizing rounds to a power of two with an intrinsic-backed helper instead of a loop.

### netty-016 — Indexed heaps and eager removal for cancellable scheduled tasks
Lens: scheduling · Source: Netty
Principle: If scheduled tasks can be cancelled, store each element's heap index inside the element so removal is O(log n), remove cancelled tasks promptly, and skip cancelled entries before they reach the run queue.
Why it is faster: A standard priority queue's `remove(o)` is an O(n) linear search. Leaving cancelled tasks in place until a periodic purge keeps them (and everything they reference) alive and makes the heap deeper. With an embedded index, removal is a sift from a known position. Filtering cancelled tasks during the transfer from the scheduled queue to the task queue saves a pointless dispatch.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Request-deadline heaps in HTTP servers where most deadlines are cancelled when the response completes. • Native / systems: OS and runtime timer queues. Dijkstra and A* with decrease-key, where an indexed heap is the classic answer. • Data / databases: Priority queues of pending I/O or compaction jobs that are frequently re-prioritised or cancelled.
Watch out: The index field must be maintained on every swap, and cleared on removal so a double cancel is detectable. Removal from another thread must be marshalled to the owning thread, or the heap needs a lock.
Seen in Netty: 05dae57ad7 2015-07-18 `ScheduledFutureTask.cancel` removes the task from the scheduled queue directly when on the event loop, or schedules its removal. The old periodic purge task was deleted. • 1f1a60ae7d 2017-11-11 `AbstractScheduledEventExecutor` uses Netty's `DefaultPriorityQueue`, where each task stores its index, so cancellation is O(log n). In the benchmark, cancelling in order and in reverse order now perform alike at 100k tasks; before, one order was far slower. • 85a663fa52 2019-09-27 A completed task's reference is swapped for a static sentinel so the GC can reclaim the callable. • 2bf7e62254 2025-11-07 Cancelled tasks are skipped when moving due scheduled tasks into the task queue.

### netty-017 — Bound the work any one source can do per loop turn
Lens: scheduling · Source: Netty
Principle: In a shared loop, give each connection or task source a budget per turn (syscalls, messages, delegated tasks) and yield when it is spent. Divide loop time between I/O and queued tasks by ratio.
Why it is faster: The main gain is in tail latency, though throughput gains too. A socket that can always write more, or a task queue that keeps refilling, would otherwise monopolise the thread, starving reads, ACKs and timers on other channels. A spin count or message cap bounds per-source work. A ratio-based time budget for tasks, with the clock checked only every N tasks to keep the check cheap, protects I/O latency. Budgets that are too small add loop iterations and reduce batching.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Per-client fairness in Redis-like servers (a cap on commands per event per client). Tokio's cooperative `coop` budget. Node: break long synchronous loops with `setImmediate`. • Data / databases: Throttle background compaction or vacuum against foreground queries by time ratio. • Embedded: Superloops that give each peripheral handler a maximum number of items per pass. • Native / systems: Game loops with a per-frame time budget, and NAPI-style poll budgets in NIC drivers.
Watch out: Reading the clock per task can cost more than the tasks themselves, so sample it every N tasks. Budgets that are too small break batching (fewer bytes per syscall) and lower throughput, so tune them under realistic load.

### netty-018 — Order work inside an iteration so it frees resources first
Lens: scheduling · Source: Netty
Principle: Within one loop turn, run the step that releases memory or unblocks others before the step that creates more work: I/O before queued tasks, draining writes before reading new input.
Why it is faster: Reading first on a connection that has a backlog of unsent output adds more output, so buffered memory grows. Flushing first releases buffers and lowers peak memory under load. Processing ready I/O before user tasks keeps network latency low when the task queue is long. This is a free change, but it alters fairness between the two kinds of work.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: In C reactors, handle `EPOLLOUT` before `EPOLLIN` for the same fd. Process completions before submitting new I/O. • Data / databases: Replication senders that ship pending WAL before accepting more client writes. • Web / backend: Stream pipelines that drain downstream before pulling more upstream (this is what backpressure does).
Watch out: Strict priority can starve the lower-priority step. Pair it with a budget (see the per-turn budget entry above).
Seen in Netty: a4c66dc282 2013-02-09 `NioEventLoop` runs `processSelectedKeys()` before `runAllTasks()`. • 3cf7ccbd3c 2016-09-29 `processSelectedKey` handles `OP_WRITE` (`forceFlush()`) before reading, so queued outbound buffers drain before more inbound data produces more output.

### netty-019 — Spin (busy-poll) before blocking, as an opt-in latency mode
Lens: scheduling · Source: Netty
Principle: When pending work is likely to appear within microseconds, poll without blocking (with a CPU pause hint) instead of sleeping in the kernel and paying for a wakeup. Make it a pluggable strategy, not the default.
Why it is faster: Blocking and being woken costs a context switch (about 13 µs was quoted) plus the producer's wakeup syscall. Spinning on a zero-timeout poll avoids both, at the cost of a fully busy core. A `pause` instruction lowers the spin's power use and its interference with the sibling hyperthread. If tasks are already queued, a non-blocking `selectNow` is the right default even without spinning.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: DPDK/SPDK poll-mode drivers, Seastar, trading gateways. Adaptive spin-then-park in mutexes (as in glibc and parking_lot). • Embedded: Spinning on a status register for very short device operations instead of sleeping on an interrupt. • Data / databases: Spin briefly on a latch before sleeping on a futex in buffer-pool code.
Watch out: Busy polling burns a full core per loop even when idle, and hurts co-located workloads and cloud bills. Netty itself withdrew the first attempt. Measure with your real connection count, and keep it opt-in.
Seen in Netty: 83f1b5fa35 2012-05-20 / b79639f710 2012-05-20 `SelectorUtil.select()` called `selectNow()` 32 times before blocking, which "seems to boost performance dramatically when the number of connections is very small". It was withdrawn in 06a6b9b3a9 2012-05-25 / 778fbc36a9 2012-05-25 pending more experiments. • 5d76daf33b 2016-03-09 Added a pluggable `SelectStrategy` for NIO (default: `selectNow` if tasks are queued, otherwise a blocking select), and 0c839d9e0a 2016-03-25 added it for epoll. • 3a96e7373b 2018-09-28 Added `SelectStrategy.BUSY_WAIT`: `epollBusyWait0` loops `epoll_wait(..., 0)` with `asm volatile("pause")` when nothing is returned. 2a1596a4e9 2018-09-07 exposed `SO_BUSY_POLL` so the NIC driver busy-polls too.

### netty-020 — Read the clock once per batch, not once per item
Lens: scheduling · Source: Netty
Principle: Take one timestamp per loop iteration or read burst and reuse it for every message, timeout and deadline computed in that batch. Never read the clock twice in a check-then-act pair.
Why it is faster: `nanoTime`/`clock_gettime` is cheap but not free (tens of nanoseconds, more on some virtual machines), and per-message reads multiply it by the message rate. A batch-level "now" is accurate enough for idle and read timeouts. Replacing a `hasScheduledTasks()`-then-`poll` pair with one poll halves the clock reads. The cost is a small staleness, bounded by the batch duration.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: A cached "now" per event-loop tick (nginx `ngx_time_update`, libuv `uv_now`). • Web / backend: Request-logging middleware that stamps a whole batch with one timestamp. Game loops caching the frame time. • Data / databases: One commit timestamp per group commit. Per-batch timestamps in time-series ingest. • Embedded: Read the tick counter once per superloop pass.
Watch out: A long batch makes the cached time stale. If a batch can run for many milliseconds, timeouts fire late, so refresh the clock after expensive steps.
Seen in Netty: 8dcb1387e3 2013-03-14 `runAllTasks(timeout)` checks the deadline only every 64 tasks. • bac2e3a6d2 2015-05-21 `IdleStateHandler` takes `nanoTime` at `channelReadComplete` instead of per message, reuses its write listener, and skips listeners for disabled idle types. 9d675def81 2015-05-22 applies the same to `ReadTimeoutHandler`. • 181c159c24 2016-06-30 `pollScheduledTask` directly instead of `hasScheduledTasks()` plus a poll: two `nanoTime` calls become one. • e47dfcc4d3 2024-07-16 `ScheduledFutureTask` avoids a second clock read and delay computation.

### netty-021 — Schedule a per-key serial runner only on the empty-to-non-empty transition
Lens: scheduling · Source: Netty
Principle: For ordered execution per key (per connection, per entity), give each key a queue. Submit the key's runner to the shared pool only when a task lands in an empty queue, and let the runner drain until the queue is empty.
Why it is faster: One submission covers a whole burst of tasks for that key, instead of one pool submission per task. It also removes the shared "currently running keys" set and its synchronisation: the queue's own empty or non-empty state is the scheduling flag. The check and the add must be atomic, or two runners start for one key.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Per-user or per-session serial queues. Akka mailboxes and GCD serial queues work this way. • Data / databases: Per-partition ordered workers in Kafka consumers, and per-row or per-key apply queues in replication. • Native / systems: Per-socket work items in kernels (re-queue only if not already pending).
Watch out: The runner must re-check emptiness under the same lock before exiting, or a task added at that moment is stranded. A runner that drains forever can monopolise a pool thread, so bound it per turn.
Seen in Netty: 824e0034c4 2008-09-26 `ChildExecutor` dropped the shared `HashSet runningChildren`: under the tasks lock it computes `needsExecution = tasks.isEmpty(); tasks.add(cmd)`, then submits itself only if needed.

### netty-022 — Size and choose event-loop threads from measurement, and let them scale
Lens: scheduling · Source: Netty
Principle: Pick the number of I/O threads by measuring, not by assumption. Share loop groups instead of creating them per component. Spread channels with a cheap chooser, and let the pool grow and shrink with measured utilisation.
Why it is faster: One thread per core leaves cores idle when handlers block briefly. Too many threads add context switches and memory (stacks, caches, buffers). Sharing pools prevents a thread explosion when there are many clients or servers. A power-of-two group size turns the chooser's modulo into a mask. Idle loops still cost memory and wakeups, so suspending them and scaling on measured busy time reclaims those resources. Autoscaling needs anti-flapping logic.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: libuv threadpool size, Node cluster worker count, and app-server worker pools that autoscale on utilisation rather than queue length. • Native / systems: Go `GOMAXPROCS` versus blocking cgo. nginx `worker_processes`. Read `availableProcessors`/cgroup quotas inside containers. • Data / databases: Postgres/MySQL connection and worker counts tuned by benchmark, and background-worker pools that shrink when idle.
Watch out: "2× cores" was a measured result for one workload and can be wrong for yours. Autoscaling that reacts too fast oscillates, and moving channels between loops is expensive or impossible.
Seen in Netty: 69450ee6ec 2010-02-23 Doubled the default number of I/O threads to `availableProcessors() * 2`, which measured as "better performing". • e0e87ce2bc 2012-03-06 / 16c625cfd0 2012-04-11 Added a shared `WorkerPool`, and 15c67f87ff 2012-11-28 a `BossPool`, so factories do not each spawn their own threads. • 722f6c5e1a 2014-03-10 When the executor count is a power of two, `next()` uses `idx & (len-1)` instead of `Math.abs(idx % len)`, via a chooser picked once at construction. • bf73ad7fc2 2024-07-10 Added a suspendable `EventExecutor` that gives its thread back when idle. 881fdc3663 2025-08-27 added `AutoScalingEventExecutorChooserFactory` with a `UtilizationMonitor`, which scales active loops between a minimum and maximum, with anti-flapping.

### netty-023 — Detect a spinning poller and rebuild it
Lens: scheduling · Source: Netty
Principle: If the blocking wait keeps returning immediately with nothing to do, the poller is broken (a JDK epoll bug, or an error storm). Count premature returns against a monotonic clock, and after N of them migrate all registrations to a fresh poller.
Why it is faster: A select that returns 0 instantly in a loop pins a core at 100% and starves other work. Detecting it costs one clock comparison per iteration. Rebuilding (re-registering every key with its interest set and attachment) is expensive but rare, and restores normal blocking. A monotonic clock and a threshold such as 80% of the requested timeout prevent false positives from legitimate early returns.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Watchdogs for busy-looping pollers. Re-create an io_uring ring or epoll fd after an error storm. • Embedded: Guard event loops against interrupt storms by masking and re-arming a source that fires continuously. • Web / backend: Detect hot loops in long-running workers (event-loop lag monitors) and recycle the worker.
Watch out: Rebuilding during heavy load can drop readiness edges if done carelessly. Make the threshold conservative so a legitimately busy loop is not mistaken for a broken one.
Seen in Netty: ff3f2b6361 2012-08-25 `recreateSelector()` re-registers every key's interest ops and attachment on a new `Selector` after 10 immediate returns. f8a99a0108 2012-08-27 switched to `nanoTime` and a minimum of 80% of the select timeout. • 45829ff721 2012-08-31 made it opt-in, 95684d92eb 2012-12-04 let users trigger a rebuild, and 292a186d16 2012-08-27 applied it to the client boss thread.

### netty-024 — Create rarely used members and resources on first use
Lens: laziness · Source: Netty
Principle: Do not allocate helpers, queues, views, native scratch memory or OS handles in a constructor if most instances never use them. Create them the first time they are needed.
Why it is faster: Eager construction makes every instance pay allocation, initialisation and GC or free costs, multiplied by the instance count, for features only a few use. Holding OS resources (file descriptors, native buffers) eagerly also caps how many pending operations you can have. A lazy field adds one null check on access. Under concurrency the lazy initialisation must be safe, or races must be benign (for example, two threads may each create the object, and both results are equivalent).
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Lazily create per-request body buffers, parsed cookies and session objects. In JS, create listener arrays and `Map`s on DOM wrappers only when the first one is added. • Embedded: Initialise peripheral driver state, and allocate DMA buffers from a pool, only when the feature is first enabled. • Native / systems: Per-connection TLS or compression contexts created at handshake, or on the first compressed message. Open file handles only when reading starts. • Data / databases: Sparse per-row metadata, lazily allocated sort buffers, and on-demand mmap of segments.
Watch out: Laziness moves the cost to the first use, which may be latency-critical (the first request). Lazy initialisation shared across threads needs safe publication. If almost every instance uses the member, eager creation is simpler and avoids the per-access check.

These were sized for the theoretical maximum and would otherwise cost every event loop native memory. • 185efa5b7c 2019-01-28 Four rarely needed cached `Runnable` fields moved into a lazily created side object, so the common in-loop case pays for one reference. • 26993b0d9c 2020-08-28 `ByteBufOutputStream` creates its `DataOutputStream` only for `writeUTF`. fedc291450 2021-02-23 QUIC creates its native-order buffer view only when ints or longs are read; it had been about 9% of all allocations in HTTP/3 profiling. 10febb298f 2022-10-19 does not initialise a thread-local just to read its size. • 92a10200c6 2023-09-07 The HPACK table-size-change output buffer is allocated only when a SETTINGS frame needs it, which helps short-lived HTTP/2 connections. 756892ce1a 2024-01-12 The `ChunkedWriteHandler` queue is allocated lazily, so the handler is a cheap pass-through when idle. 03eeaca545 2026-10-20 The WebSocket URI that only a deprecated protocol reads is built lazily. • 2f24b94c13 2025-04-26 `AdaptiveByteBuf` creates its temporary NIO buffer on first use: `allocateReleaseAdaptive` (256 B) went from 90.0M to 246.9M ops/s.

### netty-025 — Specialise for the common cardinality: zero, one, then many
Lens: laziness · Source: Netty
Principle: Most lists of listeners, header values or query parameters hold zero or one element. Store the first element in a field or return a shared empty or singleton collection, and allocate a real growable collection only when a second element arrives.
Why it is faster: A general collection costs a header, a backing array (often 10 slots) and an indirection, even to hold one item. Inline single-element storage costs one field. Shared immutable empty results cost nothing. Code paths that branch on "single or array" stay cheap if the branch is predictable. The many-case becomes slightly more complex, and polymorphic fields can upset JIT type profiles.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Small-vector optimisations (LLVM `SmallVector`, Rust `SmallVec`, `std::string` SSO) and tagged single-or-array pointers. • Web / backend: Event emitters that store one listener as a function and switch to an array on the second (Node's `EventEmitter` does this). Return a frozen shared `[]` for empty results. • Data / databases: Posting lists or multi-value columns with an inline first value, and overflow pages only for many values. • Embedded: One inline callback slot per peripheral, with a static overflow table.
Watch out: A shared immutable empty list must never be handed to code that mutates it. In Netty's later work (2022), an `Object` field holding two possible types caused `instanceof` contention on HotSpot and was split into two typed fields. Keep the representations type-stable.

### netty-026 — Return the input unchanged when no transformation is needed
Lens: laziness · Source: Netty
Principle: Before escaping, decoding, case-converting or encoding, scan for the first character that needs work. If there is none, return the original object with no allocation. Otherwise copy the clean prefix once and transform only the rest.
Why it is faster: Most real inputs (URLs, CSV fields, header values) need no changes. An unconditional builder allocates and copies every character even then. A read-only pre-scan is fast and allocation-free, so the common path is just a scan. When a change is needed, exactly one output buffer is allocated, sized from the scan.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: HTML/JSON escaping in template engines that returns the input string when it is clean. URL normalisation in routers. • Native / systems: UTF-8 validation with an ASCII fast path that returns a borrowed slice (Rust `Cow<str>`). • Data / databases: CSV writers and identifier quoting that only quote when needed. Collation-key generation that skips already normalised strings. • Embedded: Protocol encoders that pass buffers through untouched when no byte stuffing is required.
Watch out: Callers must not rely on getting a fresh copy. Returning the same object is only safe for immutable values or with documented aliasing. For inputs that almost always need transformation, the pre-scan is extra work.

When the string needs to be changed, only one buffer is allocated." • aa38b6a769 2017-06-13 `StringUtil.escapeCsv` is up to 4× faster for strings that need no escaping. ee206b6ba8 2019-12-20 `QueryStringEncoder` appends the whole string when no character needs encoding: longAscii went from 0.44 to 1.41 ops/µs. • 83dc3b503e 2018-10-11 `ByteBufInputStream` creates its `StringBuilder` only when needed. 57948458e7 2017-05-19 `NetUtil` validates IPs without `StringTokenizer` or allocation, 2-10× faster. • ed63318c9a 2024-03-25 `AsciiString.toLowerCase/toUpperCase` first checks (8 bytes at a time) whether any character needs converting, and returns `this` if none does.

### netty-027 — Do not produce completion results nobody will read
Lens: laziness · Source: Netty
Principle: If a caller does not care about the outcome of an operation (fire-and-forget writes), do not allocate a future or promise or register a listener for it. Offer a void or stateless variant, and create listeners only when the operation is still pending.
Why it is faster: A future per write is an allocation plus listener bookkeeping, then garbage once it completes. With a TCP stream, an error closes the channel anyway, so most writers never look at the result. A shared stateless "void" completion removes all of that. When the operation has already completed synchronously, attaching a listener only to have it fire at once is wasted work. Errors still have to reach someone, usually by firing an exception event instead.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Metrics and logging clients with `send()` overloads that do not return a promise. Avoid `async` wrappers for fire-and-forget calls in JS hot paths. • Data / databases: Asynchronous write APIs with a "no acknowledgement" mode, such as unacknowledged writes to a cache or replica. • Native / systems: Completion queues where most operations are posted without completion notification (RDMA unsignaled sends, io_uring with `IOSQE_CQE_SKIP_SUCCESS`).
Watch out: Failures must still surface somewhere (channel close, an exception event, a log), or errors vanish silently. A void future must reject attempts to add listeners, so that misuse is caught.
Seen in Netty: cb718a07c8 2012-05-11 / 175acb7899 2012-05-13 Added a per-channel `VoidChannelFuture` for fire-and-forget writes. 36304e1f05 2018-01-25 reuses one stateless listener in `VoidChannelPromise.unvoid()` instead of creating a new one per call. • c25eec0658 2019-10-17 A listener is created only if the write is not already complete. 70ea670ca5 2020-01-21 `ChunkedWriteHandler` attaches its listener only if the future is not done yet. • 61ab453cc5 2026-09-10 `SslHandler.wrap()` checks `!pendingUnencryptedWrites.isEmpty()` before `newPromise()`, which removed about 79% of sampled `DefaultChannelPromise` allocations. • 0419ec28b9 2026-01-27 Netty 5 replaces `Promise` with a stateless `CompletionHandler` in the outbound methods, so outbound operations allocate nothing.

### netty-028 — Skip validation the context already guarantees, and do expensive variants only where required
Lens: laziness · Source: Netty
Principle: Validate once at a trust boundary, remember that it was done (a flag, or the type), and skip repeated checks downstream. Use slow-but-safe variants (constant-time compare, full validation) only for the data that needs them.
Why it is faster: Per-character header validation, defensive deduplication and constant-time comparison are measurable fractions of hot paths. When data comes from a trusted source, is copied between containers of the same kind, or is a known constant, the check cannot fail, so running it is wasted work. Constant-time comparison gives up the early exit that makes ordinary comparison fast, so it should be used only for secrets.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Validate JSON schemas at the edge, not again in every internal service. Compare session tokens in constant time, but route names and cache keys normally. • Data / databases: Defer constraint checks during bulk loads, and skip re-validating rows copied between tables with identical constraints. • Native / systems: Branded or validated types (Rust newtypes such as `NonZero`, a validated `HeaderName`) so the type system records that validation happened. • Embedded: CRC-check a frame once at reception, then hand validated structs onward.
Watch out: Turning validation off on untrusted input opens request-smuggling and injection holes. Make the default safe, and document the trust assumption wherever the check is skipped.
Seen in Netty: 25f96b1644 2013-07-22 `DefaultChannelPromise` sets a `validated` flag to "reduce overhead during writes because of ChannelPromise validation". • c4130e0cf7 2013-11-13 Added a `validateHeaders` flag to HTTP decoders, messages and headers, reported as a 5k performance improvement when disabled. 8edfde3f26 2014-07-11 added the same for SPDY. • 6e108cb96a 2015-10-30 Copying between headers of the same kind skips name validation and rehashing. • 03ad809a3b 2019-10-23 The HPACK encoder checks whether a header is sensitive first, and uses the fast early-exit comparison for the rest. • 97d66ccf7a 2022-08-31 `DefaultCompositeBuffer.extendWith` trusts the ownership invariant (inputs are distinct and owned), so it drops a duplicate check and a concatenating iterator: extending with 64 buffers went from 16,571 to 34,222 ops/s. • 3b81f2c294 2024-01-12 Known HTTP/2 pseudo-header names are looked up before validation, so they skip it.

### netty-029 — Put the cheapest discriminating test first, and short-circuit empty cases
Lens: laziness · Source: Netty
Principle: Before an expensive operation, test for the trivial answer: same reference, nothing to do, an empty collection, a cached hash mismatch, or a status code that rules the case out.
Why it is faster: Identity equality answers a content compare in one instruction. An empty check avoids creating an iterator or running a loop. Comparing cached hash codes is a fast negative for equality. A status-code check can make a whole header scan unnecessary. The fast checks cost almost nothing when they miss, and save the full cost when they hit, which in real workloads is often.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Check a cache or ETag before rendering, and check `if (!list.length) return` before mapping. Auth middleware: reject on the cheap header-presence check before crypto. • Data / databases: Bloom filters before disk lookups, and min/max zone maps before scanning a block. • Native / systems: Compare pointers before `memcmp` for interned strings, and compare lengths first. • Embedded: Test status flags before reading FIFOs over a slow bus.
Watch out: Each extra check costs a little on the miss path. If the fast case almost never happens, the check is pure overhead. Make sure the short-circuit is semantically identical to the full path (for example, NaN or null handling in equality).
Seen in Netty: f1e00947a6 2012-09-13 `processSelectedKeys` creates an iterator only when there are keys to process, which "cut down unneeded garbage". • 375da788e7 2014-06-17 `addFlush()` skips the mark-uncancellable loop when no new messages were added. • e404690574 2017-09-08 `DefaultChannelId.equals` compares cached hash codes first for a fast negative. • 303baf5c0b 2020-10-21 `WebSocketServerExtensionHandler` checks for a 101 response first and calls `contains()` before iterating. 078cdd2597 2021-05-25 `ByteBufUtil.equals/compare` return immediately when `a == b`. • ec68df4916 2022-03-22 `leastUsedArena` returns immediately when the first arena has no users. 5e23212172 2022-04-05 Bzip2 breaks out of an inner loop early. ec9875d084 2023-08-18 `PoolChunk.allocate` re-checks `head.next == head` to avoid a needless subpage or run allocation. • 6c85b722c5 2026-11-11 `NioSocketChannel` skips rescheduling a flush when the outbound buffer is already empty after the spin loop.

### netty-030 — Track which items have pending work instead of scanning all of them
Lens: laziness · Source: Netty
Principle: Keep a set or list of the entities that actually have something to do (streams with pending writes, dirty pages) and visit only those, instead of iterating every entity on each event.
Why it is faster: Scanning all N streams or channels on every readiness or read-complete event is O(N) even when only one has work. A dirty set makes the cost proportional to the active items. Membership has to be maintained on every state change, and an item must not be added twice.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: UI frameworks with dirty-component lists. WebSocket hubs that flush only clients with queued messages. • Data / databases: Dirty-page lists in buffer pools for checkpointing, and changed-row sets for incremental view maintenance. • Native / systems: Ready lists in schedulers and epoll itself (the kernel keeps a ready list instead of scanning fds). Dirty-rectangle rendering. • Embedded: Pending-interrupt bitmaps scanned with count-trailing-zeros instead of polling every peripheral.
Watch out: A missed "mark dirty" means work is silently never done. Mark at every mutation site and test it. Items removed or closed while in the set must be skipped or unlinked.
Seen in Netty: 6470b95cdd 2020-11-20 `QuicheQuicChannel` tracks streams with pending writes and processes only those when the channel becomes writable. • 15b5f39848 2020-11-13 The QUIC codec stopped iterating over all channels on `channelReadComplete`. • 824fe7a0eb 2021-05-26 HTTP/2 GOAWAY skips `forEachActiveStream` when `lastStreamId == MAX_VALUE`, since no stream can be affected.

### netty-031 — Emit results as they are produced instead of staging them in a collection
Lens: laziness · Source: Netty
Principle: Pass each decoded or produced item downstream immediately, rather than collecting the whole batch in a list and iterating it afterwards.
Why it is faster: A staging list costs an allocation (or a pooled list), one write and one read per element, and a second pass. It also keeps every item, and the buffers they reference, alive until the whole batch is done, which raises peak memory and delays release. Emitting immediately lowers latency for the first item and frees memory sooner. Some batching benefits (one downstream call per batch) are lost, so the downstream should batch where that matters.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Generators and async iterators instead of building arrays. Streaming JSON responses row by row. • Data / databases: Push-based (or Volcano-style) pipelines that pass rows or vectors between operators without materialising intermediate results. • Native / systems: Callback- or iterator-based parsers (SAX rather than DOM) for large inputs.
Watch out: Firing inside the decode loop allows reentrancy: downstream code may close the channel or modify the buffer while the loop is still running. Netty has to re-check state after each fire.
Seen in Netty: 11e8163aa9 2015-10-06 `ByteToMessageDecoder` fires its output list after each `decode` call instead of after the whole loop, lowering latency and releasing buffers sooner. • 74d1006253 2017-07-05 `EpollDatagramChannel` fires datagrams as they are read. 76df5a591d 2017-06-21 `QueryStringEncoder` appends to one `StringBuilder` instead of building a `List<Param>`. • 0e4c073bcf 2019-12-16 (master) removed the `List out` parameter from `ByteToMessageDecoder.decode` across 103 files: decoders call `ctx.fireChannelRead` directly.

### netty-032 — Defer static tables and probes to first use with holder classes
Lens: laziness · Source: Netty
Principle: Move large lookup tables, reflective lookups and capability probes used only by rarely called features into separately initialised holders, so they are built on first use rather than at class or module load. Encode tables so the runtime's default zero-filled memory is already correct.
Why it is faster: Static initialisers run once but at startup, often interpreted before the JIT warms up, and they pay for features the process may never use. A holder class (or a lazy module import) defers that cost and saves memory if the feature is never touched. Storing value+1 so that 0 means "invalid" lets a freshly allocated array serve without an `Arrays.fill(-1)` pass. Caching a probe result (a feature check) avoids repeating it.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Dynamic `import()` of rarely used modules in JS bundles. Python lazy imports for CLI tools. • Native / systems: Function-local statics in C++ and `OnceLock`/`LazyLock` in Rust. • Embedded: Build lookup tables on first use in RAM-constrained firmware, or store them in flash and design them so zero (or erased flash, 0xFF) is the default. • Data / databases: Load collation and timezone tables only when a query first needs them.
Watch out: Lazy initialisation moves the cost onto the first request, which may be latency-sensitive. Warm it explicitly if that matters. Holder idioms rely on the language's lazy class-loading guarantees, which some ahead-of-time compilers change.
Seen in Netty: e5386b05e6 2015-12-10 Hex-dump tables moved from `ByteBufUtil` statics into an inner `HexUtil` class. • 442165ecb8 2024-02-10 CRC32/Adler32 reflective method lookups moved into holders so that using `CRC32C` does not trigger them. 613e0a24b9 2025-06-12 creates the CRC32 in `JdkZlibEncoder` lazily. ec465e7513 2025-07-14 caches the io_uring probe result. • e0d7510483 2026-11-06 `StringUtil` hex tables moved into `Byte2Hex`/`Hex2B` holders, and the 64 Ki-entry decode table stores value+1 so its fill pass disappears (startup work for Quarkus and Leyden).

### netty-033 — Keep startup paths cheap: no expensive probes, regexes or reflection, and overlap slow initialisation
Lens: laziness · Source: Netty
Principle: At startup, decide platform capabilities from cheap metadata before running expensive probes. Avoid regex compilation, `String.format` and reflective setup on init paths. Start unavoidable slow steps (such as gathering entropy) on a background thread as early as possible.
Why it is faster: Startup code runs once, before JIT warm-up, so regex compilation, formatter parsing and reflection are disproportionately expensive there. Operations such as `/dev/random` seeding or enumerating network interfaces can block for hundreds of milliseconds. Cheap checks first (system properties, a version gate) often answer the question. Starting slow work in the background overlaps it with the rest of initialisation. This matters most for serverless, CLI and AOT/CDS deployments.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Serverless cold starts: avoid reflection-heavy dependency injection, precompile regexes at build time, and warm caches asynchronously. • Native / systems: Use CPUID or HWCAP feature flags instead of micro-benchmark probes at boot. Read cgroup limits directly instead of spawning tools. • Embedded: Probe hardware lazily during boot, and replace runtime random sampling with static test vectors. • Data / databases: Defer loading extensions and catalog caches until first query. Start WAL replay in parallel with network setup.
Watch out: Cheap heuristics can mis-detect on unusual platforms, so keep the expensive probe as a fallback. Background initialisation needs a join point before first use, or the first caller sees a half-initialised state.

### netty-034 — Pay synchronisation once per batch: drain, swap or reserve in chunks
Lens: amortization · Source: Netty
Principle: Instead of a lock or CAS per element, move a whole batch at once: swap the producer's buffer for an empty one, drain everything into a private list, or reserve shared capacity in fixed-size chunks. Then process the batch with no synchronisation.
Why it is faster: Per-element synchronisation makes consumer and producers contend on every item, and each contended atomic costs a cache-line transfer. One swap or drain per batch spreads that cost over all the items. Charging a shared counter once per chunk (say 16 items) instead of per item cuts atomic traffic by that factor. Latency for individual items rises slightly, and batch sizes must be bounded so one drain does not monopolise the consumer.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Double-buffered log queues (swap the vector under a lock). Kernel per-CPU lists spliced in one operation (`llist_del_all`). • Web / backend: Read all available items in a Go channel loop with a non-blocking select. In Node workers, transfer batches rather than single messages. • Data / databases: Sequence and ID caches that reserve ranges (Postgres `CACHE`, Hi/Lo generators). Group commit of WAL records. • Embedded: An ISR fills a buffer half, and the main loop swaps halves (ping-pong buffering).
Watch out: Batching increases per-item latency, and chunked reservations can strand capacity in idle threads. Bound batch sizes, and reclaim reservations when threads die.
Seen in Netty: 39086edae6 2008-09-26 "Introduced an intermediary write buffer to reduce the contention": `writeBuffer.drainTo(internalWriteBuffer)`. f2c1734636 2008-09-26 "Reduced contention between I/O thread and writer": `drainAll()` swaps the backing array. • b64124efd6 2008-09-27 generalised this into `FastQueue`, 4d17db6eb1 2008-09-28 found a `LinkedList` "same or better", and dae3b05ebb 2008-10-01 replaced it with `LinkedTransferQueue`, showing how the best choice depends on measurement. • afafadd3d7 2016-07-11 Recycler: cross-thread return queues reserve the shared `availableSharedCapacity` in `LINK_CAPACITY` units (one atomic update per link of handles, not per object). • 8a8337eceb 2023-02-02 Recycler: an unsynchronised claim batch on the owner thread amortises claims from the MPSC queue: recyclerGetAndRecycle +27.4%, producerConsumer +22.5%.

### netty-035 — Shrink state that is multiplied by the connection count
Lens: memory-layout · Source: Netty
Principle: Treat every field of an object that exists once per connection, stream or message as a cost multiplied by the peak instance count. Remove fields that can be derived, narrow numeric types, drop hidden pointers, and replace indexes that a short scan can stand in for.
Why it is faster: Smaller objects mean less heap to allocate, zero, trace and copy, and more live objects per cache line and per page. A field is worth deleting when its value is constant for a whole class of instances (an upstream event's future is always "succeeded"), when it can be recomputed cheaply, or when a map exists only to speed up a lookup over a list that is almost always short. Narrowing `long` to `short` or `int` to `byte` only pays when it lets the object fit a smaller allocation bucket, which you have to measure.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Audit per-request and per-socket structs in a Node or Go server holding 100k WebSockets; delete cached-but-derivable fields and side maps. • Embedded: Bit-narrow fields in TCP control blocks and session tables so more connections fit in fixed SRAM. • Native / systems: Reorder and narrow struct members to drop padding (`pahole`), and prefer static/free functions to closures that capture a parent pointer. • Data / databases: Shrink tuple and lock-table headers that exist once per row or per held lock.
Watch out: Replacing a map with a list scan is only safe while the list stays short. Narrowed types can overflow silently, and the saving disappears if the runtime pads the object to the same size anyway.
Seen in Netty: 8f92cfb80a 2009-02-05 Upstream event classes lost their future field: `getFuture()` returns a shared succeeded future, because upstream events never fail. • 1bb003d9ae 2013-01-30 Four `AtomicReference` fields in the handler context became volatile fields with static updaters. The commit estimates 2 GB saved at 10 contexts × 1M connections. • 14158070bf 2013-05-28 The Netty 4 API dropped per-handler buffers to "reduce memory footprint" for many idle connections. • d0a3c2d95e 2013-02-13 334b6c3129 2013-02-26 `DefaultChannelPromise` merged its listener fields, used a `SUCCESS` sentinel instead of a boolean, and dropped an int from its listener-array holder. c406647bb2 2013-05-09 `waiters` went from `long` to `short`. • ad1389be9d 2014-07-19 `CompositeByteBuf.Component` became a static nested class, removing the hidden outer-instance pointer (also 35db3c6710 2014-11-09). • 2d2e07578a 2015-11-15 `DefaultChannelPipeline` dropped its name-to-context `HashMap`; lookups walk the short linked list instead. 0b5e75a614 2016-09-13 `IdleStateHandler` state shrank from int to byte. • 41a75370ca 2024-02-03 QUIC stream arrays grow on demand. 5144215ffc 2024-02-05 One native address instead of two per QUIC connection. 0e436dd450 2024-04-15 One shared `ThreadLocalRandom` implementation. 900ad15179 2026-04-17 `DefaultChannelId` keeps its parts in fields instead of a `byte[]`.

### netty-036 — Inline storage for the common cardinality, overflow for the rest
Lens: memory-layout · Source: Netty
Principle: Most collections attached to an object hold zero or one element. Store the first element (or a few) directly in a field or a packed word, and allocate a real collection only when it overflows.
Why it is faster: An empty `ArrayList` or `LinkedList` costs a header, a backing array and an extra pointer hop before any element is stored. A single field holds the usual one listener or one header value with no allocation and no indirection. A tagged field (either one element or an array) keeps the object small even in the rare multi-element case. The cost is one branch on every access.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Small-vector types (LLVM `SmallVector`, Rust `SmallVec`) for listener and argument lists. • Web / backend: Store a single event handler directly on a JS object and promote it to an array on the second `addEventListener`, as Node's EventEmitter does. • Embedded: A fixed inline slot plus an optional heap extension for per-device callback lists. • Data / databases: Inline short values or a single overflow pointer in index entries.
Watch out: A field that is sometimes a single object and sometimes an array forces a type check on every read. On some runtimes that check is itself a scalability problem, so prefer two typed fields over one polymorphic field.
