### netty-037 — Pack flags, small values and trees into machine words and flat arrays
Lens: memory-layout · Source: Netty
Principle: Encode booleans, small enums, handles and multi-value results as bit fields of an int or long, and store trees as implicit arrays indexed by position instead of linked nodes.
Why it is faster: A packed word is one load, one store and one register, and it moves between functions or across an FFI boundary without allocating a holder object. Set membership becomes an AND instead of a hash lookup. An implicit binary tree in a flat array needs no pointers, so a whole tree level often fits in one cache line. The trade-off is masking code and limited range, and packing is not automatically faster: Netty measured and backed out one packing that gave nothing.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Return `(status, length)` as one `u64` across FFI; tagged pointers that keep flags in alignment bits. • Web / backend: Feature flags and permission sets as bitmasks; NaN-boxing or SMI-style tagging in interpreters. • Embedded: Register-style bit fields for device state; heaps and buddy trees stored as arrays in static RAM. • Data / databases: Bit-packed tuple headers and null bitmaps; implicit B-tree or heap layouts in arrays.
Watch out: Bit packing caps the range of every field and makes code harder to read. Benchmark it, because unpacking can cost more than the memory it saves.

### netty-038 — Move rarely used fields out of the hot object
Lens: memory-layout · Source: Netty
Principle: Keep only commonly used state in the main object. Put rare state in a side object created on demand, or remove a feature whose state taxes every instance.
Why it is faster: Fields that are almost always null still occupy space in every instance and in every derived or wrapped instance. Moving them behind one reference costs a single pointer in the common case and an extra hop only on the rare path. Removing the feature entirely also deletes the code that maintains it on hot paths.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Hot/cold struct splitting in C; box rare enum variants in Rust so the enum stays small. • Data / databases: Store rare attributes out of line from the tuple header. • Web / backend: Keep debug or tracing metadata in a `WeakMap` side table instead of on every request object.
Watch out: If the "rare" path turns out to be common, every access pays an extra dependent load. Removing a public feature breaks users, so it is usually only possible at a major version.
Seen in Netty: 185efa5b7c 2019-01-28 Four cached Runnable fields in `AbstractChannelHandlerContext` moved into a lazily created side object, so the common in-loop case pays one reference. • d9a6cf341c 2018-12-11 `markReaderIndex`/`markWriterIndex` were removed from `ByteBuf` in 5.0 (37 files, -419 lines): every buffer and every derived buffer carried two mark fields that few users needed. • 4ddb81f36f 2016-04-13 Buffers stopped caching a per-buffer byte-order-swapped view.

### netty-039 — Keep fields that are used together in one record
Lens: memory-layout · Source: Netty
Principle: When every access touches all attributes of an item, store the item as one record (array of structs) in a plain array, not as several parallel arrays or a list of wrappers.
Why it is faster: With N parallel arrays, reading one item touches N cache lines and every operation updates N indexes. One array of pre-created records keeps each item's fields on one line and lets entries be reused in place. A raw array instead of a list class removes a layer of indirection and bounds checks. Structure-of-arrays wins only when loops scan a single attribute across many items.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: NIC descriptor rings and io_uring SQEs are arrays of fixed records for this reason. • Web / backend: A queue of request descriptors as an array of objects with stable shapes instead of several parallel JS arrays. • Data / databases: Row-oriented pages for point lookups versus columnar layouts for scans: pick by access pattern.
Watch out: Choose the layout from the access pattern. Records waste bandwidth when a loop reads only one field of millions of items, and pre-created records stay resident even when the queue is empty.
Seen in Netty: a383988cdb 2013-08-13 `ChannelOutboundBuffer` replaced nine parallel arrays (message, promise, size, progress, total, ...) with one circular `Entry[]` plus flushed/unflushed indices. • 10539f4dc7 2018-11-03 `CompositeByteBuf` stores (buffer, offset, adjustment) directly in each component in a raw `Component[]` instead of slice wrappers in an `ArrayList`.

### netty-040 — Keep ASCII protocol text as bytes, not as wide strings
Lens: memory-layout · Source: Netty
Principle: Header names, methods, status lines and pass-through payloads are ASCII on the wire. Store and compare them as byte arrays (one byte per character) and copy them to the output in bulk, instead of decoding to the runtime's string type and encoding back.
Why it is faster: A UTF-16 string doubles the memory, and every round trip pays for two charset transcodings and two allocations. A byte-based string type can be written to a buffer with one memory copy and hashed or compared over raw bytes. It is only a win when the data really is ASCII or opaque, and when most consumers can accept the byte form.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Keep header names as `Buffer`s in Node proxies, and use `latin1` for ASCII instead of UTF-8 decoding. • Native / systems: Use `[]byte`/`&[u8]` keys in Go and Rust protocol code instead of converting to `string`. • Data / databases: Pass column bytes through a wire protocol without converting to host strings.
Watch out: Byte strings cannot represent non-Latin-1 text, and handing them to APIs that need native strings forces the conversion you were avoiding. Reading a runtime's private string internals breaks when the runtime changes its layout.
Seen in Netty: 2b32c3fb59 2012-03-11 Redis status and error replies hold a buffer instead of a `String`, removing a decode and a re-encode. • fbc0ce4784 2015-01-11 HTTP status, method and version use precomputed `AsciiString` forms. • cac51ab8d6 2015-09-02 `ByteBufUtil.writeUsAscii` copies an `AsciiString`'s bytes in bulk. 162e59848a 2020-09-09 `writeAscii` for an `AsciiString` is a straight memory copy. • ca305d86fb 2016-01-19 When encoding, Netty reads a `String`'s backing array directly, adapted to the JDK's compact `byte[]` strings. • 583d838f7c 2018-10-26 `getCharSequence` returns an `AsciiString` for US-ASCII and ISO-8859-1, so no `char[]` is created.

### netty-041 — Size defaults and metadata from the common case and the exact bound
Lens: memory-layout · Source: Netty
Principle: Choose initial capacities, per-connection buffer sizes and allocator metadata from what the typical workload actually uses and from the protocol's exact limits, not from a generous worst case.
Why it is faster: A default capacity is the memory floor of every instance, and structures that never shrink keep it forever. An over-estimated per-record overhead or bitmap size is multiplied by every connection or every chunk. Using the spec's exact bound and the measured common size saves memory without adding work. Undersizing costs regrowth, so the size has to come from measurement.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Per-connection read buffers and header maps in servers with a million idle WebSockets. • Embedded: Static buffers sized from the RFC maximum or the measured maximum, not a round "safe" number. • Native / systems: Allocator metadata sized by actual object count; scratch arrays sized to the real kernel limit. • Data / databases: Per-connection sort and work buffers sized from typical queries, grown on demand.
Watch out: A default that is too small turns into repeated grow-and-copy on every instance. Re-measure when workloads change, and keep the size configurable.
Seen in Netty: 830091c260 2014-07-22 The outbound buffer's circular array keeps one entry object per slot. Lowering its initial capacity from 32 to 4 means "4 times less memory for entry objects". de724063f3 2014-08-12 Header value lists start at 2 instead of 4. • 8066af386a 2017-01-24 OpenSSL record overhead is budgeted at the exact 86 bytes instead of about 2 KB per wrap. ed61ec0f83 2017-01-19 Precise wrap-buffer sizing restored. • d96c02fc68 2019-02-02 One `IovArray` shared across all datagram packets removed 16 MB of direct memory per epoll event loop (the old size was `IOV_MAX` × `UIO_MAXIOV`). 630c82717d 2018-07-29 Those arrays are now created on first use. • 7b4cabc669 2023-07-21 A subpage bitmap was sized `runSize>>>10` (56 longs) when one long covered 64 elements; it is now sized by element count, and locks exist only on head subpages. • f650303911 2022-02-25 Default chunk size cut from 16 MiB to 4 MiB, lowering baseline memory with 2 arenas per core. eb3feb4798 2023-02-28 The size-class table shrank from 19 to 17 groups after a group-count fix.

### netty-042 — Return reference-counted views instead of copies
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: When a consumer needs part of a buffer, give it a view (offset and length over the same memory) and share ownership through a reference count. Copy only when the two lifetimes really have to diverge.
Why it is faster: A copy costs an allocation plus memory bandwidth proportional to the size. A view costs a small object, or nothing if views are pooled. Reference counting tells the system exactly when the last view is gone, so the underlying memory can go back to a pool immediately. The trade-off is that a small view can pin a large buffer, and every holder must release correctly.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `Buffer.subarray` instead of copying slices in Node parsers; Go sub-slices of the read buffer. • Native / systems: Rust `bytes::Bytes::slice`/`split_to`; refcounted mbufs and `sk_buff` clones. • Data / databases: Return tuple pointers into a pinned buffer-pool page instead of copying datums.
Watch out: A 10-byte slice can keep a 64 KB buffer alive. Leaks and double releases become correctness bugs, so pair views with leak detection, and copy when a small piece must outlive its large source.
Seen in Netty: 289e84d69c 2008-08-09 `ChannelBuffer.readSlice()` added in the first days of the project. • 93f9c4a2d6 2010-05-13 `LengthFieldBasedFrameDecoder.extractFrame()` became a hook; `ObjectDecoder` overrides it with `slice()` "to avoid memory copy". 83148869aa 2012-06-28 The HTTP decoder slices content out of its cumulation. 78ccfeedf9 2012-12-19 `writeBytes(buffer.readBytes(n))` became `writeBytes(buffer, n)`, removing an intermediate buffer. • 8d813b127c 2012-07-20 Buffers gained `refCnt` retain/release, the foundation for sharing views safely. • 880acbca72 2014-05-09 Delimiter, fixed-length and line frame decoders switched from `readBytes(n)` to `readSlice(n).retain()`. 891be30a28 2015-04-27 Same for the length-field decoder. d081851156 2016-04-07 Same across all codecs. • 3a9f472161 2016-04-14 `retainedSlice()`/`retainedDuplicate()` return pooled, recycled view objects with their own refcount (131 files converted). • c462ec960b 2019-10-16 When a decoder is removed, its accumulated buffer is forwarded downstream as-is instead of copied. 0ecd0c6ff3 2018-11-29 The HTTP/2 frame reader slices only when needed. • 8fe9c013dd 2020-04-28 The POST decoder retains the buffer when the first chunk is also the last. 72ec704a1a 2022-10-28 The cumulator handles input == cumulation so a receive allocator can reuse the same buffer.

### netty-043 — Reuse per-owner and per-thread scratch storage
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Temporary arrays, builders, iovec blocks and native control buffers that live only for one call can be owned by the handler, the event loop or the thread and reused, instead of allocated per call.
Why it is faster: A scratch buffer per call is an allocation, a zeroing pass and garbage per operation. An object confined to one thread (a handler on its event loop) can simply own a builder. Thread-wide scratch avoids even that. Native scratch memory reused per loop also removes `malloc`/`free` per syscall. Trimming oversized scratch back keeps a single large request from becoming a permanent leak.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Module-level reused `TextEncoder` and scratch typed arrays in JS; per-connection parse buffers. • Native / systems: Per-thread iovec and cmsg arrays in C servers; one big `mmap` sliced into ring entries. • Embedded: Static scratch buffers per task instead of stack or heap allocation per call. • Data / databases: Per-worker formatting and sort buffers reused across rows.
Watch out: Scratch storage must not escape the call or be used re-entrantly. Per-thread scratch multiplies by thread count, so do not use it on unbounded or virtual threads.
Seen in Netty: 1675e61f5b 2013-04-03 All decoders and encoders share one thread-local output buffer. 818bc602ee 2013-11-29 The HTTP decoder owns a `StringBuilder(128)` instead of fetching a thread-local one. • b7415a3307 2016-01-30 `InternalThreadLocalMap.arrayList()` for build-then-`toArray` patterns. 2aef4a504f 2015-10-13 `decodeString` caches a `CharBuffer` per thread. a6637be597 2017-01-03 Fixed a bug that stopped the thread-local list being reused. • 87ec2f882a 2018-10-19 `decodeString` became `new String(bytes, charset)` over a thread-local `byte[]`: about 2-4x faster (US-ASCII 8 B heap 10.8M to 22.4M ops/s). 5954110b9a 2018-11-05 `threadLocalTempArray(int)`. 750bd9f7b7 2019-06-19 A thread-local builder for cookie joining. • b30409890c 2017-05-05 61d3d944e2 2017-08-14 A thread-local `StringBuilder` is trimmed back past a threshold instead of kept large. • 6fdc5c7987 2020-11-07 The QUIC codec reuses its temporary buffers for its lifetime. • 3b9282f2bb 2023-03-13 "Dont malloc all the time": native send takes the address of a control-message block preallocated per event loop instead of `malloc`/`free` per call. • 279cc34a01 2025-04-15 One shared `IovArray` per io_uring handler. 0306ded58e 2026-02-10 Native msghdr blocks are one segment sliced up instead of 1,024×4 separately freed buffers, turning 4,096 arena closes into one.

### netty-044 — Remove hidden allocations from hot loops: iterators, boxing, varargs, temporary collections
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Language conveniences allocate behind your back. Use indexed loops over arrays, primitive types, fixed-arity overloads and direct query methods on hot paths.
Why it is faster: A for-each over a collection allocates an iterator; a `Long` key boxes; varargs build an array; building a list just to test membership allocates a list. Each is small, but on a per-event path they add up to a large share of the allocation rate. Direct queries (`contains(name, value)`) and walks over intrusive links do the same work with no objects.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Indexed `for` loops instead of `for...of`, spread or `forEach` with closures in hot JS; avoid `Map<number, number>` when typed arrays work. • Native / systems: Avoid `dyn` iterator boxing in Rust hot loops; C# struct enumerators. • Data / databases: Primitive column vectors in executors instead of boxed values.
Watch out: Modern JITs often remove iterator and boxing allocations through escape analysis. Profile allocation first and change only the sites that really allocate.
Seen in Netty: f1e00947a6 2012-09-13 The selector loop creates an iterator only when there are selected keys. • ac8ac59148 2014-07-08 Composite buffer deallocation uses an indexed loop. 1542b36e80 2013-10-11 Header iterators walk the linked entries directly. ff6a06a9be 2013-10-11 `HttpHeaders.contains(name, value, ignoreCase)` no longer builds a `LinkedList`. 1547876e97 2013-08-09 `out.size()` cached in loops. • 35b9aa9302 2015-04-02 HTTP/2 connection listeners moved from `LinkedHashSet` to `ArrayList`: iterators had been about 1.6% of heap in a stream-churn test. 8b1f247a1a 2015-04-10 A dedicated composite iterator instead of copying into a new list. • a4d8f930af 2016-08-01 `Float.parseFloat` instead of `Float.valueOf`. 751ed6cc94 2016-01-07 Removed boxing. b880fed195 2026-02-18 No `Long` boxing per TLS handshake. • a3e41ba6eb 2017-11-20 Value iterators on headers so `contains` no longer needs `getAll()`. 620fdc05b5 2022-08-16 netty5 component iteration is always allocation-free for non-composite buffers. 874c99529e 2026-02-03 `DefaultHeaders.containsAny` walks the hash chain with a static predicate for the keep-alive check on every request.

### netty-045 — Allocate the final size once when it can be computed or estimated
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: If the output size is known or boundable before writing (header + payload, compression bound, exact UTF-8 length), allocate that size up front instead of starting small and growing, or reserving a worst case. Process unbounded inputs in fixed-size pieces.
Why it is faster: Starting from a default and growing means several reallocations and copies; reserving the worst case (3 bytes per char, the maximum TLS record) wastes memory on every small message. An exact or tight estimate allocates once. Fixed-size staging chunks keep temporary buffers small enough to come from the pool instead of the general heap.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `Buffer.allocUnsafe(totalLen)`; compute `Content-Length` and write headers and body into one buffer. • Native / systems: `reserve()`, `strings.Builder.Grow`, zlib's `compressBound()`. • Data / databases: Size row and page buffers from schema widths; stream large BLOBs through fixed chunks.
Watch out: A wrong estimate either reintroduces growth or wastes memory. When the input is untrusted, cap the preallocation, since a lying length field could otherwise make you allocate gigabytes.
Seen in Netty: 4116c44c77 2012-01-31 `FixedLengthFrameDecoder` preallocates exactly `frameLength`. • 4d2b78ca3c 2014-06-26 The zlib encoder allocates `ceil(len*1.001)+12` plus the header and drops its 8 KiB member array. 12a3e23e47 2014-06-23 `ioBuffer()` defaulted to capacity 0, so every first write grew. ae61b12b9e 2014-05-18 With OpenSSL, wrap output is `min(pending + overhead, maxPacket)` instead of always the maximum record. 2a4999b6b8 2014-03-24 The varint prepender sizes its header exactly (1-5 bytes). • af4f70ba28 2016-01-25 The memcache encoder allocates 24 + extras + key bytes up front. 2877eef5d5 2020-12-07 STOMP allocates one buffer per frame from a header-size estimate instead of a fixed 256 bytes plus reallocations. 0fdf2e7f3a 2021-04-18 HTTP/2 SETTINGS reuses the already computed payload length. • 11a4b52978 2021-01-25 QUIC sizes datagram buffers from the peer-advertised maximum, so there is no resize path. • ef9a583ee9 2022-07-27 e8c9481d75 2022-07-27 The zlib encoder caps input staging and initial output at 64K and feeds the deflater incrementally. d80a34ecf8 2021-12-08 It stages direct input through a pooled heap buffer instead of a new `byte[]`. • e1cc93f60a 2025-10-24 `copiedBufferUtf8` uses the exact UTF-8 length. 4565f94269 2024-06-04 `SslHandler` allocates the wrap cumulation at its final size. 7893d5f150 2024-01-24 The HTTP encoder sizes the header buffer to also hold small content, so a full message is one buffer and one pipeline traversal.

### netty-046 — Grow geometrically, cap the step, and use the slack you already have
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: When a buffer must grow, double it (up to a threshold, then grow linearly), round to what the allocator will hand out anyway, and grow for free into any headroom inside the current allocation before reallocating.
Why it is faster: Growing by exactly what is needed makes n appends cost O(n²) copying. Doubling makes it amortized O(n), but at large sizes it wastes up to half the memory, so switching to fixed large steps bounds the waste. A pooled buffer often sits inside a bigger block; exposing that headroom lets growth skip reallocation entirely.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Vector growth that consults `malloc_usable_size`; Go's `append` capacity policy. • Embedded: Linear growth above a threshold so a buffer cannot exhaust a small heap in one doubling. • Web / backend: String and array builders in long-running servers. • Data / databases: WAL and sort-buffer growth policies.
Watch out: Doubling can overshoot a maximum or the available memory; always clamp to the cap. Geometric growth leaves capacity unused unless buffers are later trimmed.
Seen in Netty: 5a613f379e 2012-07-19 `calculateNewCapacity()` doubles from 64 bytes up to 4 MiB, then grows in 4 MiB steps up to the maximum. • 575baf5050 2017-06-15 The HPACK Huffman decoder doubles up to 1024 instead of growing by 32 each time. • 4fa7b99da2 2019-05-22 `maxFastWritableBytes()` exposes the slack inside the pooled allocation, and `ensureWritable` uses it. 33d576dbed 2019-12-13 Real reallocations size with `calculateNewCapacity` rather than "just enough". • 23601902ab 2021-09-01 The doubling loop became one `findNextPositivePowerOfTwo` call. c2774f957b 2022-04-20 netty5 `ensureWritable` doubles by default. • 1663eb5965 2020-09-22 The adaptive receive allocator's initial size rose from 1024 to 2048 (at least one 1500-byte MTU), avoiding copies on new connections, especially with TLS.

### netty-047 — Recycle short-lived objects through a bounded thread-local pool
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Objects created at very high rates (write tasks, buffer shells, output lists) can be returned to a per-thread stack and reused instead of becoming garbage. Give the same-thread path no synchronization, route cross-thread returns through a queue, and bound what the pool keeps.
Why it is faster: Reuse removes allocation, zeroing and GC tracing for the pooled type. A plain array stack popped and pushed by its owner costs a few loads and stores. Most objects return on the thread that took them, so that path should not pay for cross-thread machinery; an unsynchronized batch plus a queue for remote returns covers both. Without admission control and capacity limits, a burst fills the pool and the memory stays pinned forever.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Go `sync.Pool` or JS free lists for request/response objects with a high-water mark. • Embedded: Packet-descriptor pools with fixed capacity. • Native / systems: Per-frame object pools in game engines; handle pools at FFI boundaries. • Data / databases: Tuple and row-object reuse in executors.
Watch out: Pooling turns use-after-recycle into silent data corruption. Per-thread pools multiply memory with thread count, and on modern GCs pooling small objects may not beat allocation, so benchmark the specific type.
Seen in Netty: 9449efb9b2 2013-06-10 The Recycler stack became a plain `T[]` (initial 256) instead of an `ArrayDeque`. e57cf9d201 2014-03-12 The double-recycle check runs only with `-ea`; default max capacity 256K. • 030bcaae81 2014-06-13 Rewrite with per-thread weak-order queues for cross-thread recycles: same-thread recycle went from about 116M to about 204-216M ops/s. Users: 6732c6761b 2013-06-10 pooled buffers, de44917e62 2013-08-04 write tasks, 4cd7e62555 2013-07-18 the outbound buffer, 9a88c50ffb 2013-08-11 decoder output lists. • d92c5f5f5b 2016-07-26 Only every 8th never-recycled object is admitted. 445a547265 2016-07-26 Default capacity cut from about 262k to 32k, later 6cd5e8b0ca 2018-02-09 to 4k per thread. afafadd3d7 2016-07-11 Shared capacity reserved per link, not per object. d3dc9c9e74 2016-07-25 Cross-thread queues capped at 2× cores per thread. • e59d0e9efb 2016-05-03 `CodecOutputList` clears by resetting its size and nulls slots only when returned to the pool: 25.2M ops/s vs 17.3M for `ArrayList` with 4 elements. 3b57a73602 2017-12-29 It moved to a 16-entry thread-local array stack, falling back to plain allocation when empty. • d3011d4f5b 2019-11-04 A countdown replaced a mask test for admission. e816018569 2019-10-31 The finalizer was removed. b413464091 2019-11-23 Write and write-and-flush tasks merged into one pooled class. • 28b9834612 2021-11-26 Redesign: weak-order queues gone; each thread has a bounded MPSC queue and remote recycles go straight to the origin. 8a8337eceb 2023-02-02 Unsynchronized claim batch plus owner-thread direct release: get-and-recycle +27.4%, producer/consumer +22.5%. • e778364ac1 2025-10-02 "Unguarded" pools skip the double-recycle CAS, and pools can be pinned to an owner thread. f560b6c611 2025-07-09 Using the Recycler directly instead of through a wrapper means less stack depth and "less work for C2". • a434eef727 2024-11-07 Adaptive buffer shells are pooled in a bounded MPMC queue owned by the magazine. c83c1f5526 2025-04-02 2f6d2af01a 2025-01-13 No thread-local pools on virtual threads or unknown threads.

### netty-048 — Carve large chunks with a pooled size-class allocator
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Allocate memory from the OS in large chunks and serve requests from them with an allocator that rounds sizes to classes and tracks free space in compact, array-encoded structures, so buffers are reused instead of freshly allocated and later reclaimed.
Why it is faster: Fresh large buffers are zeroed by the runtime, and native buffers are expensive to obtain and free. A pool reuses memory that is already mapped. A buddy tree stored in a flat array finds a free run in O(log n) with no pointer chasing, especially when each node records the shallowest free depth below it so the search never backtracks. Power-of-two classes waste up to half of each block; finer geometric classes (several per doubling) cut that waste at the cost of more bookkeeping.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Pool request and response buffers in a proxy instead of allocating per message. • Native / systems: Buddy-plus-slab allocators for GPU, DMA or huge-page memory; size-classed arenas (jemalloc, mimalloc) for services. • Embedded: A buddy allocator over a static region when sizes vary but fragmentation must stay bounded. • Data / databases: Buffer-pool and query-arena allocators; best-fit extent maps in storage engines.
Watch out: Pools retain memory the application no longer needs, and a pooled buffer that is leaked never comes back. Rounding bugs silently double memory use. A pool only pays off when allocations are frequent and sizes repeat.
Seen in Netty: b47fc77522 2012-12-06 jemalloc-style `PoolArena`/`PoolChunk`: 8 KiB pages, 16 MiB chunks, a buddy tree in a flat `int[]`, requests normalized to powers of two, and chunk lists binned by usage (`q050 → q025 → q000 → qInit → q075`) so moderately used chunks fill first and empty ones can be released. • e5ed69241b 2014-05-30 Recursion in `allocateRun()` unrolled (+10% for non-subpage sizes). ea3dac0753 2014-05-30 Random left/right tree descent replaced by a fixed order, same speed with no RNG. • 6bd8c5d4d0 2014-06-18 69a6ad940a 2014-06-18 Each tree node stores the shallowest free depth in its subtree: with the thread cache disabled, 8 KiB allocations went from 215 to 3,656 ops/ms (about 17x) and 64 KiB from 1,221 to 4,058. • a2f232720b 2013-06-20 Size normalization uses a bit smear instead of a doubling loop. 15d11289b0 2014-04-14 A missing decrement made exact powers of two double (1024 → 2048), wasting about half the pool for composites. • 0d701d7c3c 2020-07-16 jemalloc-4-style `SizeClasses` (4 classes per doubling) replaced the buddy allocator, with runs of pages kept in priority queues by size. 221c1a1ed7 2020-11-25 Fixed a thread-cache index regression it introduced. • 86730f53ca 2020-11-29 Primitive `LongLongHashMap` and `LongPriorityQueue` for run tracking: about 13% faster allocations that miss the cache. 32627d712a 2020-11-03 Stopped re-boxing a `long` on removal. • dc69c04434 2020-03-31 Usage-percentage checks on every alloc and free became integer comparisons against free-byte thresholds computed once at construction. • 2dbc1e7c5c 2026-01-12 The adaptive allocator gained a buddy chunk for large buffers (power-of-two sizes dominate), so space is reused before the whole chunk drains. f44a88d340 2026-02-02 Reusable chunks are indexed by free capacity in a concurrent skip list for best fit.

### netty-049 — Put a bounded per-thread cache in front of the shared pool
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Keep a small cache of recently freed blocks per thread and size class so most allocate/free pairs never touch the shared, locked pool. Bound it, trim it when idle, and enable it only for threads that benefit.
Why it is faster: A thread-local hit needs no lock or atomic and returns memory that is probably still in the CPU cache. Returning a block freed on another thread through a lock-free queue to its owner keeps memory from migrating to threads that never allocate. The cost is memory held in every cache, which scales with the number of threads, so caches need limits, trimming and a policy for short-lived or very numerous threads.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: tcmalloc/mimalloc per-thread free lists with remote-free queues; per-CPU magazine caches in kernels. • Web / backend: Per-worker caches of request objects in Go (`sync.Pool` is per-P) or Node worker threads. • Data / databases: Per-worker memory contexts in query executors that return blocks to a shared arena only when full.
Watch out: Memory held in caches grows with the thread count and is invisible to the shared pool. With thousands of threads (or virtual threads) prefer shared striped pools. Producer threads that only free will drain the owners' pools without limits.
Seen in Netty: 8429ecfcc4 2014-03-01 `PoolThreadCache`, modelled on jemalloc's tcache, with tiny/small/normal tiers, only for known event-loop threads. 14600167d6 2013-11-30 No lock when freeing huge unpooled chunks. • 48f1398869 2014-09-02 Buffers record their allocating thread; only a free on that thread goes into its cache. • 81fee66c78 2015-05-19 Cross-thread frees go back to the owner's cache through a JCTools MPSC array queue (the commit notes the same-thread case got slightly slower). • 2b8fd8d43b 2016-11-22 `useCacheForAllThreads=false` limits caches to event-loop threads. 24b39bc287 2016-12-01 No cleanup watcher when caches are disabled. 0be53f296f 2015-08-07 Cache entry objects are actually recycled. • 9681842d54 2019-03-22 `cacheTrimIntervalMillis` and `trimCurrentThreadCache()`. 78c4abbfb7 2019-04-01 Caching for all threads off by default. • 05b7af7ae4 2020-04-06 The arena passes the already-fetched thread cache down the call chain instead of looking it up again: 57.1 to 50.7 ns per get-and-release. • 28fe6a9b91 2024-05-06 In the adaptive allocator, event-loop threads get a private magazine, saving two atomic operations (lock and unlock) per allocation. 7b4fe3d25c 2024-11-11 Their magazines use a thread-local pool and only shared ones pay for MPMC queues. accd981104 2026-01-06 Unguarded recyclers and a local LIFO list cut more atomics. • ee2cb981ce 2026-06-05 Size-classed caches purge by epoch every 16/128 polls; the thread-local cache is a ring split into non-empty and empty zones for an O(1) capacity scan, removing malloc/free churn on 2-core containers.

### netty-050 — Stripe the allocator across independent sub-pools
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Split one locked pool into several independent arenas and spread threads across them, so threads rarely wait for the same lock. Size the stripe count by both cores and memory, and route a thread elsewhere when its preferred stripe is busy.
Why it is faster: A single allocator lock serializes every thread. With N stripes, contention falls to the threads sharing a stripe. Sticky assignment keeps a thread's memory local; picking the least-used stripe avoids two threads colliding after thread churn. A try-lock-and-move-on scheme spreads contention like a striped counter without per-thread memory, which matters when there are huge numbers of threads. Each stripe holds its own chunks, so stripes cost memory.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Per-CPU malloc arenas; sharded free lists chosen by try-lock. • Web / backend: Striped connection or object pools where a busy shard hands the request to the next one. • Data / databases: Buffer pools partitioned by page-id hash; per-partition lock managers.
Watch out: More stripes mean more partially used chunks and higher idle memory. Cap the count by available memory, not just by cores.
Seen in Netty: b47fc77522 2012-12-06 One arena per core; each thread is bound round-robin to an arena on first allocation, with `synchronized` only per arena. • 0da48e7e7f 2013-06-14 Arena count = `min(cores, maxMemory / chunkSize / 2 / 3)`, computed separately for heap and direct memory. • e0ef01cf93 2015-06-17 Default arenas raised to 2× cores to match the default event-loop count. b88a980482 2016-03-14 New thread caches bind to the arena with the fewest users. ec68df4916 2022-03-22 Fast path when the first arena has no users. • 188ead1f63 2024-04-23 `AdaptivePoolingAllocator`: magazines chosen by thread id and guarded by `StampedLock.tryWriteLock`; repeated failures grow the magazine array up to 2× cores. ec41d5fd36 2025-06-30 Start with one magazine and allow more attempts before expanding: about 15% less memory waste. • c29f4a51da 2025-01-24 When every stripe is contended, steal a chunk atomically (`getAndSet` on next-in-line, else poll a central queue) and allocate from it without a lock. caf240ced2 2024-11-26 Freeing old magazines moved out of the expansion lock.

### netty-051 — Hand out the most recently freed item first (LIFO reuse)
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: A free list or object pool should return the item released last, because it is most likely still in the CPU cache.
Why it is faster: A FIFO pool returns the coldest object, whose memory has probably been evicted. LIFO reuse touches the same few cache lines repeatedly, lowering misses and, in pooled-object designs, GC time. LIFO also lets the tail of the pool stay untouched, so it can be trimmed.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Allocator free lists (LIFO by default in tcmalloc and jemalloc). • Web / backend: Hand out the most recently returned connection or worker thread from a pool. • Data / databases: Reuse recently freed buffer frames before cold ones.
Watch out: LIFO starves items at the bottom, which is fine for memory but bad for connections that need keep-alive traffic or fair rotation.

### netty-052 — Accumulate data as a list of segments behind one logical buffer
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Instead of growing one contiguous buffer and copying every new chunk into it, keep the chunks as components of a composite (rope) buffer that presents them as one sequence. Bound the component count or make component lookup cheap.
Why it is faster: Appending by copy is O(total bytes) per append and needs reallocations. Appending a segment is O(1). The cost moves to reads, which must find the right component, so lookups need either a cursor for sequential access or a binary search over offsets. For small pieces a copy is often cheaper than the bookkeeping, so production code mixes both.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Keep incoming body chunks in an array and call `Buffer.concat` only when a contiguous view is really needed. • Native / systems: iovec lists and mbuf chains that go straight to `writev`; ropes and piece tables in editors. • Data / databases: Write batches and WAL readers kept as segment lists; result sets composed of page slices.
Watch out: Random access and per-byte reads get slower as the component count grows, and many consumers (TLS engines, parsers) need contiguous memory anyway. For small chunks, copying into one buffer usually wins.
Seen in Netty: 902ab48820 2009-11-17 A first attempt to cumulate via composites was reverted the same day by 8eeb72cbbf 2009-11-17 because it "might perform very bad as the number of components... increases". • 476cf97b97 2012-04-24 Append in place when the bytes fit, otherwise wrap a composite. 91d5c9a0a6 2012-07-03 Default max components raised to 1000 to save copies. 166d8d9436 2012-07-03 The chunk aggregator composes chunks and copies once past the limit. 5ae0adbfaa 2009-06-15 Consolidate a composite once before writing instead of copying on every partial write. • 85712a72b8 2009-10-13 Component lookup caches the last accessed component and scans from it; multi-byte getters take a fast path when the value sits in one component. • f92cfba388 2013-05-17 Lookup switched to a stateless binary search over component offsets, safe for concurrent readers. cad7c1f1b0 2013-07-04 Multipart data uses unbounded composites to avoid consolidation copies on every add. • 50af9b916c 2014-11-21 `ByteToMessageDecoder` gained a composite cumulator: wrk plaintext went from 3,846,702 to 4,032,584 req/s. 0b1c82b254 2016-02-15 Created with `Integer.MAX_VALUE` components so consolidation never triggers. • 10539f4dc7 2018-11-03 Components stopped being slice wrappers, the list became an array, bulk insert went from O(mn) to O(m+n), and a racy last-component cache returned. 1f93bd36b6 2019-04-08 `addFlattenedComponents` for shallow copies. 0bea8ecf5d 2018-08-07 `nioBuffer()` returns a duplicate when one component covers the range. • 019cdca40b 2019-12-22 The composite cumulator never copies bytes. 33d576dbed 2019-12-13 The merge cumulator appends into a shared cumulation if it fits. d7c977dd71 2017-12-03 TLS write aggregation prefers copying small writes over building a composite. • 97d66ccf7a 2022-08-31 netty5 composite `extendWith` dropped a defensive duplicate check on owned inputs: 64 buffers went from 16,571 to 34,222 ops/s.

### netty-053 — Hand consumers the memory you already have
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: When a codec, checksum or compressor accepts (array, offset, length), a native buffer or an address, pass the existing buffer's memory directly. Likewise let producers write straight into the caller's destination instead of returning a new buffer to be copied.
Why it is faster: The pattern `new byte[len]` + copy + call allocates a temporary and doubles memory traffic for every message. Feeding the backing array or a buffer view removes both. Per-byte fallbacks are far worse: a checksum computed one byte at a time through a native boundary can be tens of times slower than a block API.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Point zlib's `next_in` and OpenSSL BIOs at existing buffers; implement `io.ReaderFrom`/`WriterTo` in Go. • Web / backend: Pass `subarray` views to WebCrypto, WASM codecs and `TextDecoder` instead of sliced copies. • Data / databases: Checksum WAL records and pages in place; let the wire protocol copy column data straight into the output buffer.
Watch out: Check which API variant is really used at run time: a reflective lookup that fails silently can drop you onto a per-byte path. Make sure the consumer does not keep the borrowed memory after the call returns.
Seen in Netty: 4680cd0bb3 2010-08-16 `ProtobufDecoder` calls `mergeFrom(buf.array(), offset, length)` when the buffer has an array, instead of wrapping a stream. 08107683b3 2009-06-12 The OIO datagram path builds the packet over the existing array. • 12898a2ef4 2012-06-10 `ChunkedInput` writes its chunk into a caller-provided buffer instead of returning one, saving a copy in most cases. • 0f7379157a 2014-01-30 `JdkZlibEncoder` calls `deflater.setInput(array, offset, len)`. 65686b6c83 2014-06-25 Same for the SPDY header compressor. 0393ffbfb2 2013-07-14 Fewer copies in the HTTP content decoder. • 87551fc751 2016-07-19 `ByteBufChecksum` uses `Checksum.update(ByteBuffer)` or a byte processor instead of copying. bb3c4a43d8 2016-07-12 LZ4 decoder. 9151739577 2016-07-19 Bzip2 encoder feeds bytes via `forEachByte`. f25f1f255d 2016-07-20 The Snappy stream identifier is compared in place. • 09158a6420 2019-06-18 `Lz4XXHash32` uses xxHash32's ByteBuffer block API, about 25x faster in internal tests than per-byte hashing. db1e662933 2019-06-16 A reflection bug that silently disabled `update(ByteBuffer)` was fixed. • 569ede081f 2021-10-11 zlib compressor and decompressor pass ByteBuffers directly on Java 11+. 60c9cbf46a 2021-07-26 FastLz works on direct buffers. 4bd20067ac 2023-07-07 `SslHandler.wrap` and ae079d1aed 2023-07-20 Brotli/LZ4 use the cached internal NIO view.

### netty-054 — Copy at absolute offsets instead of creating temporary view objects
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Do not create a duplicate, slice or wrapper object just to set a position for one copy or one I/O call. Use absolute-offset APIs, raw address copies, or a view cached on the buffer and reused.
Why it is faster: A `duplicate().position(x)` per copy is one short-lived object per operation on the hottest path. Profiles of busy brokers showed these views as a double-digit share of all allocations. Absolute-offset copies need no object, and when a view is unavoidable one cached instance per buffer suffices. Removing the view can also let the JIT scalarize the remaining cursor objects.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `buf.copy(target, targetStart)` or `TypedArray.set(src, offset)` instead of `slice()` then copy; reuse one `DataView`. • Native / systems: `memcpy(dst + off, ...)` or Go `copy(dst[off:], src)` instead of building sub-stream objects. • Embedded: Static DMA descriptors reused with new offsets instead of per-transfer descriptor structs.
Watch out: A cached view carries mutable position state: it must never escape to callers or be used re-entrantly, or two users corrupt each other's cursor.
Seen in Netty: 02cb7adf03 2012-05-25 "Reduce GC in ChannelBuffer bulk operations": each buffer keeps one `tmpBuf` view and resets its position and limit. 01aa1647bb 2012-05-25 One reused `DatagramPacket` per OIO datagram channel. • 7f7bf304b0 2013-06-10 Return the cached `memory` ByteBuffer instead of a `duplicate()`. 283feda119 2013-06-13 `internalNioBuffer()` exposes a reusable view ("reduce even more garbage"). • 27c68647df 2015-10-18 Direct-to-ByteBuffer copies use one `copyMemory` call with no temporary view. • 85c1590a90 2018-12-04 Pooled buffers cache their internal NIO buffer. c6bdc2b7dc 2019-11-16 `PoolArena.memoryCopy` reuses the destination's internal view. ed46c4ed00 2018-02-21 Read-only heap ByteBuffers are copied without an intermediate buffer. • 7f9bff976e 2022-04-05 netty5 `copyInto` uses `System.arraycopy` instead of slice and duplicate. fedc291450 2021-02-23 A swapped-order view in the QUIC header parser was 9% of all HTTP/3 allocations; it is now created only when multi-byte values are read. • 06f8920da0 2025-09-30 092ce05a65 2025-09-30 5fe1eea925 2025-12-15 3cae6f60e0 2025-12-18 Removed `duplicate()`, used `slice(int,int)` and absolute `put`. ee7ab44061 2026-10-16 NIO views had been 13% of a Pulsar broker's allocations. 797f1180c7 2025-01-17 Byte cursors without `duplicate()` so they scalarize. • 2f24b94c13 2025-04-26 The adaptive buffer's temporary NIO view is created on first use: allocate-and-release went from 90.0M to 246.9M ops/s at 256 B.

### netty-055 — Allocate in the memory kind the final consumer needs
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Native (off-heap, DMA-able, pinned) memory saves a copy only when native code consumes it. Produce output directly in the kind the next stage needs, and use ordinary heap memory when the consumer works on arrays.
Why it is faster: The JDK copies a heap buffer into a temporary direct buffer for every socket write, so encoders that emit direct buffers skip that copy. The reverse also holds: a JDK TLS engine or a stream socket works on `byte[]`, so feeding it direct buffers forces an extra copy back. Unpooled direct memory is also expensive to allocate and free, which makes a cheap heap copy better for one-off buffers.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Allocate pinned host memory for CUDA transfers and DMA-capable buffers for NIC staging up front. • Embedded: Put network buffers in the DMA-reachable SRAM region from the start instead of copying at transmit time. • Web / backend: Keep data as `Buffer`s rather than JS strings when it is headed to a socket or a native addon.
Watch out: Native memory is usually slower to allocate and lives outside the GC's accounting. Without pooling, the allocation can cost more than the copy it saves.
Seen in Netty: 36e804bbec 2010-01-27 NETTY-282 added `isDirect()` to buffers and its own direct buffer pool to reduce heap/direct copies in the NIO transport. 2b786116b5 2010-01-28 "No need to copy if bufferFactory created a direct buffer". 972620d431 2009-10-16 Wrap direct or array-backed NIO buffers instead of copying them, "for less memory bandwidth consumption". • a9a29bdf3f 2013-03-08 Netty used direct I/O buffers wherever possible once its direct buffers were as fast as heap ones. • a0e74ff984 2014-02-15 With an unpooled allocator, heap buffers are copied into a per-thread reusable direct buffer (default 64 KiB) before the syscall. 0dc6a8dccf 2014-08-13 `Unpooled.copiedBuffer()` always uses heap, because direct allocation is expensive for unpooled buffers. • fa84e86f78 2016-07-12 The LZ4 encoder allocates direct output. aa8917115c 2015-02-08 693633eeff 2015-12-18 41d0a81691 2016-02-13 Composite, Base64 and full-message allocations go through the channel's (pooled, direct) allocator instead of `Unpooled` heap. • d0c43c9e42 2017-08-08 The blocking OIO transport prefers heap buffers. 678983f2a7 2019-10-12 7150b42a56 2019-10-23 `SslHandler` uses heap buffers for the JDK engine. e4ef8f6ff3 2019-05-21 Direct I/O buffers are used when direct memory is pooled, even without Unsafe.

### netty-056 — Reuse callback, task and future objects; offer allocation-free call shapes
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: A closure, listener, task or future allocated per event is pure garbage when it carries no per-call state. Keep one pre-built instance per owner, create it only when it will be used, and give callers APIs that do not force an allocation when they do not need a result.
Why it is faster: Capturing lambdas allocate on each call unless escape analysis removes them, and that often fails across non-inlined boundaries. A field holding one reusable task costs one reference. A void or fire-and-forget variant removes the future that most callers ignore. Creating the listener only when the operation has not already completed skips it on the synchronous fast path.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Pre-bound handlers instead of `() =>` per event in JS; `useCallback` in React; `void` overloads of async writes. • Native / systems: Completion structs embedded in connection objects instead of a `std::function` per call. • Embedded: Static callback descriptors registered once per peripheral.
Watch out: A reused task must not be scheduled twice at once. Fire-and-forget APIs drop errors, so they fit only where failure is reported another way (for example, the connection closes).
Seen in Netty: cb718a07c8 2012-05-11 175acb7899 2012-05-13 A per-channel void future for fire-and-forget writes. 36304e1f05 2018-01-25 `VoidChannelPromise` reuses its listener. • 6d1220b55e 2013-07-12 A `flushTask` field in the channel. 43f12569aa 2013-07-12 A cached flush task per context. 506474f569 2013-01-15 All per-context runnables created lazily. 9fd8bf329d 2013-09-13 A cached flush task for incomplete writes. • f47cf0c4d3 2019-07-06 Two fewer objects per HTTP/2 header write. c25eec0658 2019-10-17 A listener created only if the write is not already complete. f6005c5b2d 2019-07-21 HTTP/2 stream events reused. f9888acfdd 2017-12-16 A single-argument overload avoids a varargs array. • 19b4adf79c 2019-10-17 Promise and scheduled tasks hold a Runnable directly instead of wrapping it in a Callable adapter. • d7108a36bb 2025-12-13 `LocalChannel` keeps a `finishReadTask` field. a0faa5e3c7 2026-09-14 The io_uring completion callback is cached in a final field: 84 lambda events / about 78 MB of event-loop allocation dropped to 0 / about 17 MB at about 11k req/s. • 61ab453cc5 2026-09-10 `SslHandler.wrap()` checks for pending writes before creating a promise, removing about 79% of sampled promise allocations. 0419ec28b9 2026-01-27 In 5.0, outbound operations take a stateless completion handler instead of a promise.

### netty-057 — Emit results directly instead of staging them in intermediate objects
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Do not collect results in a temporary list, array or result-holder object and then iterate it. Pass each result on as it is produced, update the caller's cursor directly, or write into the final buffer.
Why it is faster: Staging costs an allocation, a write per element and a second pass. Emitting immediately also releases buffers sooner and lowers latency. Holder objects that return (value, consumed) pairs allocate on every field decoded.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Generators or callbacks instead of building arrays in JS/Python pipelines; parsers that update `this.pos` instead of returning tuples. • Native / systems: Out-parameters or multiple returns in Go and C instead of heap result structs. • Data / databases: Push-based (produce-and-consume) executors instead of materializing intermediate result lists.
Watch out: Emitting immediately changes re-entrancy: downstream code may run while the producer is mid-loop and may even remove it. Make the producer's state safe for that.

A later attempt, c88cdcd3ca 2025-05-20, was reverted in cb084854a6 2025-06-13.

### netty-058 — Parse and format without temporary strings
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Avoid `split`, `String.format`, `toHexString`, number-to-string comparisons and concatenation on per-request paths. Scan in place, write digits straight into the output buffer, and feed hashers in pieces.
Why it is faster: Each of these creates one or more strings or arrays per call, and formatting APIs also parse a format pattern. A manual scan or an in-place digit writer does the same work with zero allocation. A reusable, minimal char buffer avoids the synchronization and checks of general-purpose builders.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Go `strconv.AppendInt`; avoid `split` and template strings in hot JS parsers. • Native / systems: C++ `std::to_chars` into the output buffer; incremental hash updates instead of concatenation. • Data / databases: Format numbers straight into the wire buffer in DB protocol encoders.
Watch out: Hand-written scanners are easy to get wrong on edge cases (empty fields, whitespace, overflow). Keep thorough tests next to them.

### netty-059 — Share immutable instances for markers, empty results and defaults
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Return one shared, immutable object for every empty collection, terminator marker, stateless parser, default configuration or recurring event, instead of a new one per call.
Why it is faster: Allocation drops to zero for the common empty or default case, and consumers can compare by identity. The object must be truly immutable, or callers that mutate it corrupt every other user.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `Object.freeze`d default options and empty arrays in JS libraries. • Native / systems: Static empty strings and sentinel nodes; flyweight events in GUI toolkits. • Data / databases: Shared NULL and empty-value singletons in row decoders.
Watch out: Shared instances must be deeply immutable, including reference counts and mutable buffer indices; share buffers only through a non-releasable wrapper.

### netty-060 — Predict the next buffer size from recent history
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: When the size of the next read or encoded message is unknown, predict it from recent sizes: step up quickly after full reads, step down slowly, and keep the size classes no finer than the allocator can serve.
Why it is faster: A fixed size either wastes memory on small messages or forces extra reads and regrowth on large ones. A predictor that jumps up immediately and decays slowly tracks bursts without oscillating. Coarse classes aligned with the allocator keep the lookup table small. A weighted moving average does the same for encoder output.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Size JSON serializer output from the previous response size; adaptive read sizes in proxies. • Native / systems: TCP-autotuning-style receive buffers in user-space stacks. • Data / databases: Adaptive fetch and batch sizes in consumers and drivers.
Watch out: Predictors are per connection or per encoder, so their state adds to footprint. Mixed traffic can make them flap; asymmetric step sizes and a minimum help.
