### netty-061 — Damp grow/shrink and create/destroy oscillation with hysteresis
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Grow a cached resource immediately when needed, but shrink or release it only after sustained under-use, and keep one spare instead of destroying the last one.
Why it is faster: A workload that hovers at a boundary makes a naive policy allocate and free the same expensive resource over and over. Requiring many consecutive small uses before shrinking, keeping a warm minimum, or retaining the last destroyed chunk turns that churn into a single allocation. The price is some memory held after load drops.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Vector shrink policies; jemalloc dirty-page decay instead of immediate `munmap`. • Web / backend: Thread pools with keep-alive and min-idle; WebSocket frame buffers shrunk only after sustained small messages. • Data / databases: Connection pools with min-idle; autoscalers with scale-down delays.
Watch out: Hysteresis keeps peak memory for longer after a burst. Pair it with an eventual trim so idle processes give memory back.
Seen in Netty: 3070247ca7 2012-07-30 A per-thread receive buffer reallocates at once when too small, but when too big only after repeated small reads. f129ee1e28 2012-07-30 The rule became "under 80% of capacity, 16 times in a row". • dbab41cc50 2013-06-21 The last subpage of a size class is never freed. • 3af3083f73 2024-09-09 Heap arenas keep the last destroyed chunk for reuse. • ee2cb981ce 2026-06-05 Caches age idle chunks by epoch and evict past a threshold while keeping a warm minimum.

### netty-062 — Bound pools, adapt them to the environment, and measure that they pay
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Every pool trades memory for speed. Cap what a pool can retain, turn pooling down or off on small heaps and low core counts, and keep a pool only if a measurement shows it helps.
Why it is faster: An unbounded pool turns an allocation burst into permanently retained memory, which raises GC cost or causes out-of-memory errors. On a small heap or a two-core container, striping and per-thread caches can hold more memory than the workload uses. Measuring read and write paths separately shows that a pool can help one and not the other.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Size caches and pools from cgroup memory limits in containers, not host RAM. • Embedded: Fixed maximum pool sizes with a fallback to direct allocation when exhausted. • Data / databases: Buffer-pool sizing from available memory; max-idle limits on connection pools.
Watch out: Environment detection can be wrong (container limits, unusual JVM flags). Keep the policy overridable, and re-benchmark when hardware changes, because atomics and allocation costs differ across CPUs.
Seen in Netty: 36e804bbec 2010-01-27 A 4-slot soft-reference direct buffer pool. a9c90d0e4a 2010-02-18 Reverted: "doesn't seem to improve the performance as much as additional memory consumption... often led to OOME". 263e1ed601 2010-02-18 "using buffer pool during read doesn't seem to help performance at all". 545acfdf42 2010-02-18 Brought back as a read-only receive pool. • 108fbe5282 2018-02-12 `ByteBufUtil` stopped caching direct buffers by default. • 07bf4ad46a 2025-10-20 With a heap of 512 MiB or less, the adaptive allocator uses one stripe and disables large-buffer pooling and thread-local magazines: 5,185,536 to 4,399,104 bytes at 128 live buffers in the simulator. 8f7d7a68c6 2025-10-02 Unpooled allocator by default on small heaps. • 4797b88d89 2022-03-03 24018418c3 2022-03-09 Thread-local caches are on by default only for event-loop and FastThreadLocal threads.

### netty-063 — Serve small sizes from per-class slabs tracked by bitmaps or free lists
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Split a page or chunk into equal slots of one size class, track free slots in a bitmap or an index free list, and keep partially used slabs per class so a small allocation or free is O(1).
Why it is faster: Giving each small object a whole page wastes memory, and bump allocation fragments when small long-lived objects pin a large chunk. A 64-bit bitmap word covers 64 slots with shift-and-mask operations, and remembering the most recently freed slot makes most allocations skip the scan. Keeping at least one partially used slab per class stops the pool from creating and destroying pages at a boundary.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Kernel `kmem_cache`-style slabs; memcached slab classes. • Embedded: Fixed-size packet and session pools tracked by a bitmap in an RTOS. • Data / databases: Free-slot bitmaps in slotted pages and tuple arenas.
Watch out: Each class needs its own partially full slabs, so many classes multiply idle memory. Pick classes from the real size distribution.
Seen in Netty: b47fc77522 2012-12-06 `PoolSubpage` tracks slots in a `long[]` bitmap (`bitmap[idx >>> 6] |= 1L << (idx & 63)`) with tiny (16-byte steps below 512) and small (512 up to page size) class pools. • e5ed69241b 2014-05-30 The `nextAvail` hint set by `free()` makes tiny allocations as fast as larger ones even with 1 MiB pages. dbab41cc50 2013-06-21 The last subpage in a pool is never removed, to avoid create/destroy flapping. • c52e5a1b9c 2022-11-11 A loop that wrote zeros into a freshly allocated bitmap was removed. 7b4cabc669 2023-07-21 The bitmap is sized by element count, not page size. • 5ca90a70db 2025-06-27 The adaptive allocator added one chunk per size class with an `MpscIntQueue` free list so any freed segment is reused at once; classes are "power of two plus a bit" because protocols add headers to power-of-two payloads. c78193caf1 2025-06-29 Reuse the same chunk while free segments remain. • 2b73259c50 2025-08-05 The free list stores precomputed segment offsets instead of ids, removing a multiply from the allocation path. • 8134aefc28 2025-07-08 The size-class lookup became a `byte[]` table indexed by `(size+31)>>5` instead of a binary search.

### netty-064 — Bump-pointer chunks sized from observed request sizes
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Allocate by advancing an offset inside a chunk and free the whole chunk when all its pieces are released. Choose the chunk size from a histogram of recent request sizes rather than a fixed constant.
Why it is faster: Bump allocation is one addition and one compare, with no per-object metadata. A per-chunk count of live pieces says when the chunk can be reused. Sizing chunks from a high percentile of recent requests keeps most requests inside one chunk without wasting memory on rare huge ones. If borrows happen on one thread and releases on any, split the count into a plain increment and an atomic decrement so the borrow path does no atomic operation.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Request-scoped arenas sized from the observed p99 body size. • Native / systems: Arena allocators (APR pools, Rust `bumpalo`) for per-request or per-frame data. • Data / databases: Per-query arenas in executors; memtable arenas freed as a unit. • Embedded: Pools sized from measured traffic instead of the theoretical maximum.
Watch out: One long-lived small piece pins its whole chunk. Mix in size-class slabs for small long-lived objects, and watch fragmentation under skewed lifetimes.
Seen in Netty: 32ef9e3e52 2010-02-19 `SocketSendBufferPool`: 64 KiB direct slabs, writes served as 16-byte-aligned slices by advancing a pointer, a live-slice count per slab, retired slabs on a soft-reference free list. d9de1675d0 2010-02-19 Applied to UDP. • 188ead1f63 2024-04-23 `AdaptivePoolingAllocator`: bump allocation in chunks sized at p99 × 10 of a sliding-window histogram with 8 power-of-two buckets and short counters, recomputed less often while the size is stable. • dc2555d8c9 2024-05-20 Chunks use a split count: a plain/lazySet "up" count by the owner and an atomic "down" count by anyone; free when equal. • f2c27dadba 2025-02-05 Chunk sizes round to whole page units, and mismatched chunks are released probabilistically (0.5% per unit of deviation) to stop alloc/free oscillation. • c7f950a746 2024-09-18 A chunk that cannot fit a request but still has much space is parked as next-in-line; the smaller of two candidates is retired. 99275750f3 2024-12-14 Central-queue chunks are reused even when a too-small local chunk is cached. • 50e7b2d0e3 2024-09-10 The adaptive allocator became the default.

### netty-065 — Do not zero memory that will be overwritten or that is already zero
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Use uninitialized allocation for buffers whose contents are fully written before being read, and remove explicit zero-fill passes over memory the runtime or OS already zeroed.
Why it is faster: Zeroing is a memset across the whole allocation, and for large buffers it dominates allocation cost and memory bandwidth. Where the contract says contents are undefined, skipping it is free. Encoding tables so that zero already means the right default removes initialization passes too.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `malloc` over `calloc` for overwritten buffers; rely on mmap's zero pages instead of memset; Rust `MaybeUninit`. • Web / backend: `Buffer.allocUnsafe` in Node when the buffer is immediately filled. • Embedded: Place large buffers in a non-initialized RAM section (`.noinit`) to shorten boot.
Watch out: Uninitialized memory can leak old data (including secrets) if any byte is read or sent before being written. Use it only where every byte is provably overwritten.
Seen in Netty: 96d3726a38 2009-10-21 The bundled zlib no longer zeroes a freshly allocated hash array. • ee198f9c35 2017-03-25 `PlatformDependent.allocateUninitializedArray` (Java 9+). d7fa7be67f 2018-10-27 Used in AsciiString, protobuf, OIO datagram and more. 83710cb2e1 2018-06-29 `toArray(new T[0])` instead of a pre-sized zeroed array. • ccc128fa0e 2022-08-16 netty5 stopped zeroing new off-heap buffers. 0a6295146b 2022-06-30 Unsafe heap buffers use uninitialized arrays. c52e5a1b9c 2022-11-11 A loop writing zeros into a fresh `long[]` was removed. • e0d7510483 2026-11-06 A 64K-entry hex decode table stores value+1 so the default zero means "invalid" and the `Arrays.fill(-1)` pass disappears.

### netty-066 — Release memory explicitly; keep finalizers and the GC out of buffer lifetimes
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Give buffers an explicit owner and reference count, return them to the allocator the moment the last user releases them, and avoid finalizers or cleaners on the allocation path. Use `realloc` to resize native memory.
Why it is faster: GC-tracked native memory is reclaimed late and through slow paths (finalizer queues, synchronized cleaner lists, global handshakes), so memory piles up and releases contend. With explicit release, memory goes back to a pool immediately. `realloc` can grow or shrink in place where allocate-copy-free always copies. GC-based cleanup still makes sense for short-lived unpooled memory when the explicit free is the expensive part.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Rust `Bytes`/`Arc` ownership; refcounted pages pinned in a buffer pool; `realloc` for growable arrays. • Web / backend: Explicit `close()` or `free()` on native-backed objects in Node and Python instead of relying on `FinalizationRegistry` or `__del__`. • Data / databases: Release query memory contexts at operator end instead of waiting for global cleanup.
Watch out: Manual release brings leaks and use-after-free back. Keep a sampled leak detector in development, and measure the cost of the free path itself before replacing GC cleanup everywhere.
Seen in Netty: 81e2db10fa 2012-11-16 All allocation goes through a pluggable `ByteBufAllocator` reachable from the channel context. 8d813b127c 2012-07-20 `free()` replaced by reference counting. a6e89aa142 2012-10-04 Direct buffers are destroyed explicitly at shutdown instead of waiting for GC. • 3d29bcfc8d 2016-05-23 `UnpooledUnsafeNoCleanerDirectByteBuf` calls malloc/free directly with its own accounting (the JDK cleaner was `static synchronized`). 3a7dcde320 2016-05-23 Growth via `reallocateMemory`. 20059f130d 2017-07-05 Realloc for shrinking too. 7d774584c8 2016-07-14 Finalizer-free reference-counted OpenSSL engine and context. • e816018569 2019-10-31 The Recycler's finalizer was removed. 9412424091 2023-06-06 dc16c5818a 2023-07-24 A thread cache's finalizer moved into a small side object created only for caches that actually pool. • 67a46b102f 2022-11-16 netty5 gives each event loop its own Cleaner: 2,995 to 50,096 ops/ms with 10 CPUs, and Reactor-Netty H2C went from 132k to 380k req/s. dc46a3918a 2022-12-08 No cleaner attached by default; allocator throughput roughly doubled on an M1. • c79ce23a7b 2026-03-14 On JDK 25 FFM, closing a shared arena triggers a global handshake, so unpooled buffers are left to the GC while pooled chunks keep explicit close. dc4d8b4dc6 2025-06-18 f265f09b2a 2025-07-10 malloc/free-based cleaners for direct buffers.

### netty-067 — Compact accumulation buffers lazily and partially
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Do not move unread bytes to the front of a buffer after every read. Compact only when incoming data would not fit, or when the read position has passed a threshold, and move only the live part.
Why it is faster: A memmove of the unread tail on every read costs bandwidth proportional to the tail size, even when space at the end is plentiful. Deferring it until the buffer is genuinely full, or until at least half of it is dead, amortizes one move over many reads. Copying only the part after a parser checkpoint, or only a small live fragment that pins a large buffer, bounds the cost further.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Stream parsers in JS that keep a read offset and compact only when appending would overflow. • Native / systems: Go `bufio`-style readers; choose a ring buffer when compaction would be frequent. • Data / databases: Compaction thresholds for log segments and slotted pages (compact when dead space passes a fraction).
Watch out: Deferring compaction keeps dead bytes resident longer. If a peer sends many tiny partial frames, add a periodic bound so the buffer cannot grow without limit.
Seen in Netty: c2d2f0b254 2012-02-28 "FrameDecoder discards readableBytes only when it has to" and "ReplayingDecoder does not perform memory copy if possible". ea6ed5cb61 2012-02-29 Copies only the part after the checkpoint. • e329cc3524 2012-06-19 The zero-copy frame decoder copies a fragment only when the capacity it pins exceeds a threshold, and copies only the live bytes. • a2aadef4da 2012-08-08 `discardSomeReadBytes()` compacts only when `readerIndex >= capacity >>> 1` or on a full drain. 689c47980b 2012-02-29 Minimum cumulation allocation of 256 bytes. • c8a941d01e 2015-09-25 The decoder discards read bytes every 16 reads to bound memory without compacting on each one.

### netty-068 — Drop references early so dead objects do not keep live ones reachable
Lens: allocation · Source: Netty
Principle: Null out links and cached pointers when an object leaves a structure or goes back to a pool, and swap finished tasks for a sentinel, so the GC can reclaim what they point to.
Why it is faster: In a generational GC, a dead old-generation node that still points to young objects keeps them alive through minor collections ("nepotism"), and they get promoted, which makes later collections more expensive. A pooled object that keeps pointers to its last chunk or view holds that memory while it sits idle.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Clear `next` on dequeue in JS or .NET linked queues and LRU caches. • Native / systems: In reference-counted systems, break cycles explicitly when unlinking. • Data / databases: Clear row references in reused executor batches so large values are freed.
Watch out: Clearing links while an iterator or another thread still walks the structure causes crashes or lost elements. Do it only when the node is fully detached.
Seen in Netty: d6bf388343 2016-03-22 The HPACK encoder's header-entry `remove()` clears its `before`, `after` and `next` links. • dcf4292647 2017-01-25 Pooled buffers null their temporary NIO view and chunk before recycling. • 85a663fa52 2019-09-27 A completed task's callable reference is replaced by a static sentinel so the GC can collect it.

### netty-069 — Replace per-item computation and branch chains with small precomputed tables
Lens: caching · Source: Netty
Principle: Map an input byte, nibble, code or size directly to its output through an array built once. Size the table to stay cache-resident, and to the full index range when that lets the compiler drop bounds checks.
Why it is faster: A table lookup is one load in place of formatting calls, branch chains or a search, and it has no data-dependent branches to mispredict. A table must also stay small: a 512 KiB table thrashes the cache and does the same job as a 2 KiB one. Decoding several bits per step from a state table beats walking a tree bit by bit.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Hex, Base64 and escaping tables in JS encoders; arrays instead of `Object.values(...).find` for enum decoding. • Embedded: 256-entry CRC tables rather than larger slice-by-N tables on small MCUs; lookup tables in flash. • Native / systems: Size-class maps in malloc; Huffman fast tables in zlib and Brotli; UTF-8 validation DFAs. • Data / databases: Opcode dispatch tables; collation and character-class tables.
Watch out: Large tables cost cache misses and startup time. Build rarely used tables lazily, and measure: a computation that fits in a few ALU instructions can beat a table that misses in L1.
Seen in Netty: e1709c8e4b 2008-09-05 A hex-dump table built with `String.format` became a flat array built with shifts. c79bbd063f 2009-02-05 Its heap use went from 512 KiB to 2 KiB with a 256 × 2 char table. 7a5f4721b9 2012-05-12 `LoggingHandler` uses `BYTE2HEX`, `HEXPADDING`, `BYTEPADDING` and `BYTE2CHAR` tables. • 65d8ecc3a0 2019-07-02 The HPACK Huffman decoder decodes 4 bits at a time from an int-packed state table: ASCII SMALL 426k to 473k ops/s, MEDIUM 57.8k to 66.7k. 760bd4ab90 2019-07-08 Flags folded into table entries. • 1e4f0e6a09 2019-12-23 `decodeHexNibble` uses a `byte[Character.MAX_VALUE+1]` table so the JVM can drop the bounds check. • 0a8c9192e5 2020-08-31 `MqttMessageType.valueOf` uses an array indexed by the wire code instead of scanning `values()` (which also clones an array per call). 319a4bc3ba 2020-09-04 Same for four more MQTT enums. • 142027220a 2023-09-22 `HttpStatusClass.valueOf` replaced five range checks with `statusArray[code / 100]`, the division done as a reciprocal multiply. • 8134aefc28 2025-07-08 Allocation size-class lookup became a dense `byte[]` index table instead of a binary search.

### netty-070 — Pre-encode well-known constants once, with their hash
Lens: caching · Source: Netty
Principle: Convert fixed protocol strings (header names, versions, status lines, delimiters) into their wire bytes once, store their hash alongside, and emit them by bulk copy or by sharing a read-only buffer.
Why it is faster: Encoding `"HTTP/1.1"` or `"\r\n"` on every message repeats a charset conversion and an allocation that always produce the same bytes. A pre-encoded constant is a memcpy, or with a shared read-only buffer just a new pointer and indices. A stored hash means lookups in case-insensitive maps skip rehashing.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Pre-serialized JSON keys and static response headers; pre-built static iovecs for delimiters. • Embedded: Constant TLV headers and frames stored in flash. • Data / databases: SQL keywords interned with precomputed hashes; pre-encoded protocol message headers.
Watch out: Shared buffers must be read-only and must not be released by consumers; wrap them so release is a no-op.
Seen in Netty: 7f57c5ed05 2013-11-28 `HttpHeaderEntity` holds a name, its hash and its bytes; `HttpVersion` and `HttpResponseStatus` cache their encoded bytes and write them with `encode(ByteBuf)`. • b9e279064d 2013-08-11 The HTTP encoder keeps `CRLF` and the `0\r\n\r\n` terminator as unreleasable direct buffers and emits `duplicate()`s. 0e8fb21554 2013-05-24 CRLF written as one `byte[]` instead of two single-byte writes. • fbc0ce4784 2015-01-11 Status, method and version use precomputed `AsciiString` forms. • df568c739e 2017-07-04 Two- and three-byte constants are written as a single `short` or medium in the HTTP, STOMP and Redis encoders.

### netty-071 — Intern well-known values and compare by identity first
Lens: caching · Source: Netty
Principle: When decoding a value that matches a known constant, return the shared constant instead of a new object. Downstream code can then test equality with a pointer comparison before falling back to a content comparison.
Why it is faster: Decoding `"Host"` into a fresh string on every request costs an allocation and later a full compare at every lookup. Returning the interned constant removes the allocation, and an identity check then settles most comparisons in one instruction. Checking a cached hash first gives a fast negative.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Canonical header-name constants in Go (`textproto`) and Node's well-known header cache. • Native / systems: Symbol interning in compilers and Lisps; atom tables. • Data / databases: Dictionary-encoded strings compared by code instead of by content.
Watch out: Identity is only a fast path; always fall back to content comparison, because the same value can still arrive as a different object. An unbounded intern table fed with attacker data is a memory leak.
Seen in Netty: 0daaec3ac2 2023-01-27 The HTTP decoder returns `AsciiString.cached("Host")`, Content-Length and similar constants instead of new strings when a header name matches. e2c8a97747 2024-02-08 "Accept" added. • cce3d5b636 2024-01-31 `HpackStaticTable` is built from the pseudo-header constants, so lookup does a switch on length and then `header == METHOD.value()`. 3b81f2c294 2024-01-12 The pseudo-header lookup runs before validation, so known names skip validation. • b7ceeb1797 2019-01-30 An `==` fast path in `HttpMethod.equals`. 664f20315c 2025-04-02 `HttpMethod.valueOf` compares against interned strings. 078cdd2597 2021-05-25 `ByteBufUtil.equals` returns immediately when both arguments are the same object. • e404690574 2017-09-08 `DefaultChannelId.equals` compares cached hash codes first for a fast negative.

### netty-072 — Cache derived forms (hash, string, address) on the object
Lens: caching · Source: Netty
Principle: If an object's hash code, string form or native address is derived from state that does not change, compute it once and keep it in a field, invalidating it on the rare change.
Why it is faster: Hashing or stringifying a key on every map access repeats work proportional to its length, and recomputing an address through several validity checks repeats branches on every call. A cached field is one load. A benign race (two threads may both compute the same value) avoids synchronization on immutable objects.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Memoized `toString` on immutable value objects; cached hash on composite cache keys. • Native / systems: Cached `data()` pointers in views; precomputed physical addresses in DMA descriptors. • Data / databases: Store the hash of a key in the hash-table entry so rehashing and comparisons skip recomputation.
Watch out: Every cached field adds per-object memory and an invalidation obligation; cache only what is read far more often than it changes.
Seen in Netty: d50322f27f 2009-02-09 `AbstractChannel.toString()` is cached while the channel is connected. 1e3fe3ffc3 2012-08-25 `hashCode()` returns the precomputed channel id instead of `System.identityHashCode`. • 4875a2aad4 2017-08-13 `AsciiString` caches its hash and string form with benign races, and keeps the source `String` when created from one. 05480c190f 2019-10-23 `toString` cached on Netty's singleton idle events. • b4fa8af079 2013-09-26 Each outbound buffer entry caches its NIO buffer count and views across write retries. • f1cfbe1ea6 2024-01-12 HTTP/2 streams hash on a per-connection increasing id instead of the identity hash, which forces the JVM to generate and store one in the object header. • b23c19dfc6 2026-10-15 A chunk caches its root address and each buffer stores `address + startIndex` at init; the checks this removed were 3.3% of I/O-thread CPU in a Pulsar broker.

### netty-073 — Turn per-call derivations into fields computed at construction
Lens: caching · Source: Netty
Principle: Convert ratios, thresholds, strides and limits into the absolute values the hot path compares against, once, when the object is built. Do setup work in the constructor or a shared context instead of in every operation.
Why it is faster: A usage percentage computed on every allocation is a division; comparing free bytes against a precomputed threshold is one integer compare. A precomputed upper bound lets a search skip whole lists that cannot satisfy a request. Storing offsets instead of ids removes a multiply. Registration or setup repeated per handshake can move to construction.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Store a resize-at count in hash tables instead of checking a load factor; precompute byte quotas for rate limits. • Native / systems: Precomputed strides and byte offsets in pools and page tables. • Data / databases: Free-space maps that let inserts skip pages below the needed free bytes; prepared statements instead of per-call planning.
Watch out: Precomputed fields go stale if the inputs can change at run time (resizable tables, reconfigured limits). Recompute them at every point where the inputs change.
Seen in Netty: 0241120ace 2008-09-26 Each queued task stores its estimated size, so the estimator is not run again on decrement. • cfa258e096 2016-04-06 `PoolChunkList.maxCapacity` is computed from its minimum usage, so allocation returns early instead of walking chunks that cannot fit. 1b4a5609d3 2016-04-06 The full-chunk list is never searched. • dc69c04434 2020-03-31 Usage-percentage checks became free-byte comparisons against thresholds computed in the constructor. • 2b73259c50 2025-08-05 The segment free list stores precomputed offsets, which double as segment identity. • b880fed195 2026-02-18 Registering the SSL pointer in the engine map moved from `handshake()` to the constructor.

### netty-074 — Compute immutable data once and share it across instances
Lens: caching · Source: Netty
Principle: When every instance of a class builds the same tables or configuration-derived arrays, build them once in a shared context and reference them, instead of computing a copy per instance or per connection.
Why it is faster: Per-instance copies multiply construction time and memory by the instance count. Inheriting from a class that builds tables gives each subclass instance its own copy, while composition shares one. Per-connection results derived from per-context settings can be computed once per context.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Per-tenant configuration objects shared by all requests; compiled regexes and templates held at module level. • Native / systems: Static Huffman tables shared across codec instances; flyweight tables in parsers. • Data / databases: TLS session templates and prepared plans shared across connections.
Watch out: Shared data must be immutable, or concurrent readers need synchronization. A shared object that occasionally needs per-instance overrides should be copied on write, not mutated.
Seen in Netty: f9e20ac049 2023-12-12 `PoolArena` holds a `SizeClasses` instead of extending it: each allocator builds 2 table sets instead of 32, saving at least 42,720 bytes for the default allocator. • 1e718ed4ad 2026-11-14 Enabled TLS protocols are computed once per OpenSSL context as a shared immutable array, saving about 280 bytes per connection.

### netty-075 — Memoize per-type metadata and capabilities
Lens: caching · Source: Netty
Principle: Results that depend only on a class (reflection lookups, annotation checks, which callbacks a handler overrides, name-to-class resolution) should be computed once per class and cached, then consulted as cheap flags on the hot path.
Why it is faster: Reflective calls are slow, sometimes synchronized, and block inlining. A per-class cache turns them into a map hit at setup time and a field read per event. A precomputed bitmask of overridden callbacks lets an event chain jump directly to the next handler that cares, skipping pass-through hops. Weak keys keep the cache from pinning classes that should unload.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Express/Koa-style middleware chains that precompute which hooks each layer implements; DI containers caching reflection metadata. • Native / systems: Caching symbol lookups in plugin hosts; precomputed vtable capability flags. • Data / databases: Type registries in serializers; removing pass-through operators from plans once at planning time.
Watch out: Strong class keys leak class loaders; use weak keys. Global caches can become contention points, so make them per-thread or concurrent.
Seen in Netty: 7e6d07afaf 2011-11-22 Weak- and soft-caching class resolvers for deserialization. 99e9da1e75 2011-11-22 One resolver cache shared across streams. b858fab8d7 2011-11-22 A redundant second cache lookup removed. • 1c4d0d0f57 2012-07-10 The per-class `Field[]` chain is cached with a `FAILURE` sentinel. • 1eafffbec5 2013-02-08 The type-parameter matcher cache moved to a thread-local `IdentityHashMap`. adcc2d4b4b 2014-03-10 `isSharable()` is cached in a thread-local `WeakHashMap<Class, Boolean>`. 148abe52f9 2013-03-07 Generated handler names are cached per class. 98eb69f169 2015-10-28 That cache moved to per-thread maps instead of a synchronized one. • b8866aef33 2013-08-30 Inbound/outbound flags are precomputed per context, so pipeline traversal does not run `instanceof` on every hop. • 8fdf373557 2019-01-22 8f7ef1cabb 2019-04-09 `ChannelHandlerMask`: a per-class 17-bit mask of overridden callbacks (via a `@Skip` annotation, cached per thread). Propagating an event past 4 extra handlers went from 267k to 825k ops/s (about 3x).

### netty-076 — Memoize per-message parsed values and validation results
Lens: caching · Source: Netty
Principle: Parse a header value (such as Content-Length) or run a validation once per message, store the result in a field with a "not computed" sentinel, and reset it when the next message starts.
Why it is faster: Decision points in a decoder may each look up and parse the same header from a map. One parse plus a field read replaces all of them. A "validated" flag on an object makes repeated checks on reuse free.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Middleware that parses `Accept`, cookies or query strings once per request and stores the result on the request object. • Data / databases: Memoized computed columns per row within a query.
Watch out: Forgetting to reset the memo between messages leaks one message's value into the next, a classic request-smuggling hazard in HTTP decoders.
Seen in Netty: 0052d83db1 2013-12-18 `HttpObjectDecoder.contentLength()` is cached with a `Long.MIN_VALUE` sentinel and reset per message. • 25f96b1644 2013-07-22 `DefaultChannelPromise` records that it has been validated, reducing write overhead from promise checks.

### netty-077 — Keep one instance per thread of expensive, non-thread-safe objects
Lens: caching · Source: Netty
Principle: Objects that are costly to create and unsafe to share (MACs, message digests, encoders, date formats) should be created once per long-lived thread and reused, not created per call or guarded by a lock.
Why it is faster: Creating a crypto MAC or digest involves provider lookups and key setup. A per-thread instance needs no lock and pays that cost once. It only works for a bounded set of long-lived threads.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Per-worker zstd/zlib contexts (`ZSTD_CCtx`) instead of one per stream. • Web / backend: Per-worker `TextEncoder`, hasher and regex objects in Node worker pools. • Data / databases: Per-connection compression and checksum contexts.
Watch out: With thousands of threads (or virtual threads) per-thread instances multiply memory; use a bounded pool instead.
Seen in Netty: 2a2a21ec59 2014-06-09 Date formats and charset encoders moved onto Netty's fast thread-locals. • 79634e661b 2015-12-18 MD5 and SHA-1 `MessageDigest`s are kept per thread for WebSocket handshakes. • 0fa01f4e51 2023-11-22 The QUIC connection-id generator caches its `Mac` in a fast thread-local.

### netty-078 — Cache results of expensive deterministic operations, including failures and in-flight work
Lens: caching · Source: Netty
Principle: When the same input always yields the same expensive result (a compressed certificate, a converted cipher name, a platform probe), cache it. Cache negative results too, and let concurrent callers for the same key share one in-flight computation.
Why it is faster: Handshakes and lookups repeat the same inputs constantly. A cache hit replaces compression, regex conversion or OS enumeration. Caching failures stops impossible inputs from being retried at full cost. Sharing an in-flight query collapses a thundering herd into one operation.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Go `singleflight`; CDN request collapsing; caching gzip of static assets and signed headers. • Native / systems: Cache CPU-feature and kernel-capability probes at startup. • Data / databases: Negative caching of missing keys; plan caches keyed by query text.
Watch out: Bound every cache, and decide how entries expire. Negative caches turn transient failures into persistent ones unless they expire.

### netty-079 — Look up thread-local or context state once and pass it down
Lens: caching · Source: Netty
Principle: If the top of a call chain already has the thread-local value, executor or context, pass it as a parameter or store it in a field of a thread-confined object, instead of looking it up again at every level.
Why it is faster: Each thread-local access is a hash probe or at best an indexed load plus a thread lookup, and repeated lookups in one call chain multiply it. A parameter is a register. Objects already confined to one thread can hold the value in a field permanently.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Pass request context explicitly instead of repeated `AsyncLocalStorage` or `ThreadLocal` reads. • Native / systems: Pass a per-thread arena pointer down instead of calling `pthread_getspecific` in every helper; Go's explicit `ctx`. • Data / databases: Pass the executor's memory context down through operators.
Watch out: A cached thread-local value is wrong if the object later runs on another thread. Only cache it in objects that are confined to one thread for their lifetime.
Seen in Netty: 5de4b23c7a 2013-11-14 The HTTP decoder caches its thread-local builder in a field. • edb2250d35 2015-11-14 One `IovArray` per epoll event loop instead of a fast-thread-local lookup on every write. • 82ac20bec0 2019-07-18 A looked-up handler context is cached instead of searched again. • 05b7af7ae4 2020-04-06 The allocator passes the thread cache down to arenas and chunks: 57.1 to 50.7 ns per get-and-release. 46524db7d8 2022-09-16 The NIO event loop reuses a local reference.

### netty-080 — Keep hot functions small: move rare branches into separate cold functions
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: Optimizing compilers inline only functions that fit a size and depth budget. Move error handling, resizing and rare cases out of the hot function so that the common path fits the budget and gets inlined into its callers.
Why it is faster: Inlining removes the call, and it also lets the compiler see across the call boundary: constants propagate, checks fold, values stay in registers and escape analysis can work. HotSpot inlines hot methods only up to about 325 bytes of bytecode (`FreqInlineSize`), and ordinary call sites only up to about 35 bytes. It also stops at an inlining depth of about 9. A method one byte over the limit stays a real call, and everything it calls stays opaque to the compiler. Splitting out cold code costs nothing on the common path. The rare path pays one extra call.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: V8 and other JS JITs have inlining budgets too. Keep request-path functions small, and put `throw` and logging in helper functions. • Native / systems: mark error paths `__attribute__((cold, noinline))` or `[[unlikely]]` in C/C++, or `#[cold]` in Rust, so the hot loop stays compact in the instruction cache. • Embedded: outlining rare paths keeps the hot ISR or loop inside a small I-cache or tightly coupled memory. • Data / databases: in an expression interpreter, keep the per-row operator function minimal and handle NULLs and overflow out of line.
Watch out: inlining decisions depend on the runtime version and its flags, so check them with `-XX:+PrintInlining` or the equivalent tool rather than guessing. Manual inlining duplicates code. Language features that look free, such as private access from nested classes and lambdas, can add hidden call levels.
Seen in Netty: 42afa85e53 2009-02-03 The rare synchronized fallback in the vendored `LinkedTransferQueue` moved into its own method, "likely to help JVM optimize QNode more aggressively". ### netty-081 — Validate once at the boundary, then use unchecked internal accessors
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: Check bounds and liveness once, where data enters an API. Internal hot paths and wrappers that have already validated a range then call unchecked primitives instead of the public, checked ones.
Why it is faster: Layered abstractions re-check the same range at every layer: a slice checks against itself, then calls its parent's public getter, which checks again. Each check costs compares, branches and sometimes a volatile load, and compilers often cannot prove the checks redundant across virtual calls. Splitting the API into a checked public method and an unchecked internal method (`getX` calls `checkIndex` and then `_getX`) removes the duplicates without giving up safety at the edge.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: in Rust, use `get_unchecked` after one slice-range assert. In C++, use `operator[]` instead of `at()` inside loops whose range was checked once. • Data / databases: a page reader can validate the page header and slot directory once, then read fields raw. • Web / backend: a JS parser can hoist a single length check before a loop over a `Uint8Array`. • Embedded: validate the packet length once at the driver boundary, and let the protocol layers index raw.
Watch out: an unchecked accessor reachable from untrusted input is a memory-safety bug when it backs onto raw memory (Unsafe or C). Keep unchecked accessors package-private or internal, and give each one a single, obvious owner of the check.
Seen in Netty: aef2ab453a 2008-08-08 The initial import already had `UnsafeDynamicChannelBuffer` with an empty `checkReadableBytes()`, because the outer `ReplayingDecoderBuffer` did the check. 081395410d 2011-12-08 created it only when needed. • 1c1570ffc4 2013-03-06 gave `AbstractByteBuf` its `checkIndex` plus abstract `_getByte`/`_setByte` structure ("avoiding unnecessary boundary checks"). f88dfd0430 2014-07-14 and 93c306602a 2014-07-14 treated redundant checks that had crept back as performance regressions. fe796fc8ab 2014-09-04 made `writeUtf8`/`writeAscii` call `_setByte` after one `ensureWritable(len*3)`: writeAscii ran at 9.40M ops/s versus 3.23M, and writeUtf8 at 5.67M versus 3.56M. • 4c287d4e27 2015-10-08 / 8c93f4b1ef 2015-10-08 added `SlicedAbstractByteBuf`/`DuplicatedAbstractByteBuf`, which call the parent's `_getXxx` directly. 5a6238ed4c 2015-10-16 added a refcount-free `checkIndex0` for slice bulk operations. • dd07caec6b 2019-08-22 made `HttpObjectDecoder` use `charAtUnsafe`. 05dd1f05d5 2019-08-24 wrote `indexOf` as a loop over `_getByte`. 1e4f0e6a09 2019-12-23 sized the hex-decode table to the whole `char` range (65,536 entries) so that the JIT can prove every index is in bounds and drop the check. • 69d0bec043 2024-09-09, da3ca4b6ab 2025-07-11 and 4c0d9df922 2025-06-30 removed double checks in `AdaptiveByteBuf`, slices and read-only wrappers. 6001499649 2026-03-24 caches `(AbstractByteBuf) buf` in `CompositeByteBuf.Component` so that `_getXxx` skips the checks. d972caeed7 2026-10-15 uses `System.arraycopy` directly once the outer check has run. a6c493c659 2024-02-21 keeps the `byte[]` in a local so that repeated bounds checks disappear.

### netty-082 — Keep call sites monomorphic: specialize per concrete type instead of looping over an abstraction
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: A hot loop that calls virtual or interface methods on varying concrete types becomes megamorphic and stops inlining. Dispatch on the type once, outside the loop, into a copy of the loop specialized for that type.
Why it is faster: JITs speculate on the receiver types seen at each call site. With one type (monomorphic) or two (bimorphic) they inline behind a cheap type guard. With more types the call becomes a vtable or itable dispatch, and inlining stops. A per-element virtual call in a loop costs a dispatch per byte and blocks vectorization and check elimination. Hoisting the type test out of the loop makes the inner loop call only static or final code. The price is duplicated code.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: in V8, keep object shapes stable at a call site. A function that sees five shapes goes megamorphic, so split it per shape. • Native / systems: use C++ templates or Rust generics instead of `virtual`/`dyn` in inner loops, and pay the dispatch once per batch. • Data / databases: vectorized engines specialize kernels per column type rather than calling `getValue()` per row.
Watch out: specialization multiplies code size and maintenance, so specialize only paths the profiler proves hot. Some overrides do not pay, as Netty's own measurement of `getCharSequence` showed.
Seen in Netty: 54d44c6ac1 2013-02-08 generated a tiny class per message type with Javassist whose `match()` is a plain `instanceof`, instead of a reflective `Class.isInstance`, so that the type check inlines. • 162e59848a 2020-09-09 made `ByteBufUtil.writeUtf8`/`writeAscii` dispatch once to `unsafeWriteUtf8(array|address)`, `safeArrayWriteUtf8`, `safeDirectWriteUtf8` or a straight copy for `AsciiString`, so there is no longer an `AbstractByteBuf` virtual call per character. 06f70a4cf5 2023-02-07 batch-encodes `AsciiString` input. • 0daaec3ac2 2023-01-27 HTTP/1.1 parsing found that "bi-morphic inheritance and loop fusion" were "not working". It split `LineParser` from `HeaderParser` so each has its own call site. • 4624b6309d 2020-10-02 gave `DefaultAttributeMap` a hand-rolled binary search on `key.id()`, avoiding the megamorphic `Comparator` call. • 2177c4dfe4 2025-03-21 added `AsciiString`-specific fast paths to `regionMatches`. 31c033a1d9 2025-05-14 overrode `setCharSequence` in `AdaptiveByteBuf`, up to 50% faster for direct buffers (HTTP header encoding). The commit also measured that the `getCharSequence` sibling was not worth overriding. 1c203d6784 2026-02-25 uses `Integer.toString` instead of the generic `String.valueOf(Object)`.

### netty-083 — Test exact classes before interface types on hot, multi-core paths
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: Order type checks so the common concrete classes are tested with exact-class compares before any interface `instanceof`. Where possible, replace a polymorphic field with separately typed fields.
Why it is faster: In HotSpot, an `instanceof` against an interface the class does not implement, or a site that alternates between several interfaces, walks the secondary-supers array and writes the class's one-element `secondary_super_cache` (JDK-8180450). When many cores do this on the same class, that cache line bounces between them, so a check that costs nanoseconds on one core stops scaling. An exact-class compare is one load and one compare, and it writes nothing. Other runtimes have similar costs on megamorphic type checks, for example V8 `instanceof` chains and inline-cache misses.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: any JVM service that dispatches messages by interface type (Kafka, gRPC, Jackson) can hit this. Profile on many cores, not just one. • Native / systems: the general lesson is that a read-only operation can hide a shared write. Look for caches inside the runtime that every thread updates. • Data / databases: expression evaluators that type-test values per row should test concrete types first.
Watch out: exact-class checks miss subclasses, so keep the interface check as the fallback for correctness. The effect appears only with real parallelism, and single-threaded benchmarks will not show it.
Seen in Netty: 423a385b05 2022-08-25 "Scalable HTTP encoding": `HttpObjectEncoder` duplicated code to reduce how many types each `instanceof` check sees, and stopped calling inherited release helpers. • 22d31519bc 2022-11-29 replaced `DefaultPromise`'s `Object listeners` field plus `instanceof DefaultFutureListeners` with two typed fields. • 9ec938c78f 2023-02-17 made `ChannelOutboundBuffer.progress` check `getClass() == VoidChannelPromise.class`, then the concrete progressive promise, and only then the interface. 9fe796e10a 2023-05-10 added another concrete fast path. • f67a55609d 2023-04-18 added exact-class checks to the HTTP and WebSocket handlers, with `HttpRequestResponseBenchmark`. 7700680138 2023-12-29 did the same in `AbstractCoalescingBufferQueue.remove`, which had tested ByteBufs against `ChannelFutureListener`.

### netty-084 — Make expected errors cheap: preallocated, stackless or lazily created exceptions
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: For frequent, expected failures (closed channel, cancellation, timeout), do not build a fresh exception with a captured stack trace each time. Reuse a static instance, disable stack capture, or create the exception only when something will actually receive it.
Why it is faster: Capturing a stack trace walks every frame and allocates an array of frame descriptors, which costs microseconds, compared with nanoseconds for the surrounding logic. When one close fails thousands of pending writes, creating an exception per write multiplies that cost. A shared instance with `fillInStackTrace()` returning `this` costs nothing. Creating the exception lazily (only if the pending queue is non-empty) avoids even that.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: in JS, preallocate `Error` objects for hot rejection paths or lower `Error.stackTraceLimit`. In Go, use sentinel errors (`var ErrClosed = errors.New(...)`) instead of a `fmt.Errorf` per failure. • Native / systems: on hot paths, prefer error codes or `Result` types to C++ exceptions. • Embedded: use static error descriptors and never format error strings on the fast path. • Data / databases: a bulk loader that rejects many rows should share one error object per error class.
Watch out: a shared exception has no useful stack, and a mutable one (with `addSuppressed` or `initCause`) can leak state between callers. Make shared instances immutable, and add a synthetic frame so logs still say where the error came from.
Seen in Netty: d52b238d62 2008-09-18 NETTY-46 addressed "excessive exception creation overhead when there are many pending write requests in a closed channel". cd35c1d130 2009-04-26 stopped creating an unused exception on close. 6e68d6ba4f 2009-09-29 reuses the caught `ClosedChannelException` for all pending writes. • a159d3ebac 2013-11-19 made the `CancellationException` in `DefaultPromise.cancel()` static. b5989e2449 2013-04-24 added static `SSLENGINE_CLOSED`, `HANDSHAKE_TIMED_OUT` and `CHANNEL_CLOSED` with `EMPTY_STACK_TRACE`. • df033e150c 2016-06-30 caches the `ECONNREFUSED` exception. e845670043 2016-06-09 gives the preallocated exceptions one synthetic `StackTraceElement` (class and method) so they remain debuggable. • 0280bd2063 2019-09-05 uses a sentinel for cancellation in `DefaultPromise` and builds the `CancellationException` lazily. b769ec0934 2018-01-23 creates one `StreamException` per HTTP/2 stream instead of one per queued write. • 07632a5a4c 2020-09-01 added `StacklessClosedChannelException`, because close "showed a lot of overhead" in benchmarks. 5bb535e78a 2020-09-21 creates `ConnectTimeoutException` only when needed, and 19625e5407 2021-03-17 creates the QUIC stream-close exception only if the queue is non-empty. 33931652a8 2023-10-13 throws a stackless exception for the HTTP/2 RST flood limit. 862efc618c 2020-02-16 removed `synchronized` from an overridden `fillInStackTrace`. 8f744ecfab 2026-03-18 fixed GC pressure under connection churn with a stackless exception in `SslHandler.channelInactive`.

### netty-085 — Do not use exceptions for control flow on hot paths; use explicit checks and resumable state
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: When "need more data" or "buffer full" is a normal outcome, express it with a return value or a state machine instead of throwing and catching. Even a stackless throw costs an unwind, and a throwing design usually forces re-work.
Why it is faster: Throwing, even with a preallocated exception, unwinds frames and goes through exception tables. Code that throws often also gets deoptimized. A "replaying" parser that throws on short input re-parses from the last checkpoint on every partial read, so its cost grows with the number of fragments. An explicit state machine checks the available bytes, returns, and resumes exactly where it stopped. Letting the runtime's bounds check signal "grow the buffer" saves one compare per append, but the throw is much slower when it does happen, and the JIT may not optimize the catch path well.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: in Python, use `dict.get` instead of `try/except KeyError` in hot loops. Streaming JSON and HTTP parsers should return "incomplete" rather than throw. • Native / systems: write resumable parsers (llhttp or picohttpparser style) that keep a cursor and state across reads. • Data / databases: CSV and wire-protocol readers can report a short buffer with a status code and keep the parse state.
Watch out: Netty's own catch-to-grow trick was later reverted. Benchmark the rare path, not only the common one: a design that is fast on average can stall under adversarial or fragmented input.
Seen in Netty: aef2ab453a 2008-08-08 The original `ReplayingDecoder` threw a static `REPLAY` error when bytes ran short. dbd973d825 2012-05-18 turned this into a `Signal` with `fillInStackTrace() { return this; }`. d6cedb008b 2012-05-30 made the multipart decoder's `SeekAheadNoBackArrayException` a singleton. • fda8808210 2014-11-04 rewrote `HttpObjectDecoder` from `ReplayingDecoder` to an explicit-state `ByteToMessageDecoder`, about 177% faster on `HttpRequestDecoderBenchmark`. • a7713069a1 2015-07-25 once made `AppendableCharSequence.append` do `chars[pos++] = c` and grow inside `catch (IndexOutOfBoundsException)`. Two years later 4587800d82 2017-06-12 went back to explicit capacity checks, and so did 3f0085c267 2017-06-11 for `HpackHuffmanDecoder`. f5fbceeef5 2017-06-07 removed `SeekAheadNoBackArrayException` from multipart decoding entirely.

### netty-086 — Route bulk work to runtime intrinsics instead of hand-written loops
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: Library routines that the compiler treats as intrinsics (array equality, array copy, fill, atomic add, min/max, string construction) compile to SIMD or single instructions. Arrange the call so that its fast contract applies, and do not replace these routines with a clever loop of your own.
Why it is faster: An intrinsic is replaced by hand-tuned machine code, often vectorized, chosen for the exact CPU. `getAndAdd` becomes `LOCK XADD`, `Arrays.equals` becomes a vectorized compare, and `new String(bytes, charset)` takes a specialized decoder. A hand-rolled equivalent may beat it on an old runtime but loses on newer ones, and it can also make the JIT pick a slower generic stub.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: in JS, prefer `TypedArray.set`, `fill`, `indexOf`, `TextDecoder` and `Math.clz32` over loops. • Native / systems: `memcmp`, `memcpy`, `std::atomic::fetch_add` and `__builtin_*` beat hand-written loops and CAS-based adders. • Data / databases: page copy, checksum and compare routines should call the platform's bulk primitives, such as hardware CRC32C.
Watch out: intrinsics have contracts (full arrays, alignment, Java version) and a fixed call overhead, so tiny inputs can lose (see the small-memset entry below). Re-check old workarounds whenever the runtime gets new intrinsics.
Seen in Netty: db6b72da19 2016-05-23 zeroes buffers with `Unsafe.setMemory` instead of a store loop. 7b121c26ae 2016-04-06 uses `Math.min/max` in `PoolChunkList`. • 89e93968ac 2016-12-09 dropped Netty's own Unsafe-based `Atomic*FieldUpdater`s (added in 2014) for the JDK's, whose `addAndGet` had since become an intrinsic. • 87ec2f882a 2018-10-19 rewrote `decodeString` as `new String(bytes, charset)` over a scratch array, about 2-4x faster (US-ASCII 8 B: 10.8M to 22.4M ops/s). 0f42eb1ceb 2019-12-19 did the same for `QueryStringDecoder`: 0.92 to 1.39 ops/us. • 7f9bff976e 2022-04-05 makes `NioBuffer.copyInto` use `System.arraycopy` instead of slice and duplicate views. e0f857523e 2022-11-09 overrides bulk `writeBytes`, which had defaulted to a per-byte `setByte`. • 0daaec3ac2 2023-01-27 added a full-length, offset-0 branch so the JIT picks a simpler arraycopy stub at that call site (with a JDK-8295496 note). fdfdf90656 2023-12-21 calls `Arrays.equals` (vectorized) when both ranges cover their whole arrays.

### netty-087 — SWAR byte search: test 8 bytes per step with word arithmetic
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: To find a byte (CR, LF, a delimiter, an uppercase letter), load 8 bytes as one 64-bit word, XOR it with the target repeated in every byte, and use the has-zero-byte bit trick to test all 8 positions at once. Count trailing or leading zeros to get the match position.
Why it is faster: A byte loop does one load, one compare, one branch and often one bounds check per byte. SWAR (SIMD within a register) does one load and a handful of ALU operations per 8 bytes, and branches only once per word. That cuts loads and checks to about an eighth, and it works without vector instructions. An "any match?" test runs before the more expensive index calculation, so words without a match stay cheap. Unaligned 8-byte loads must be cheap on the platform, and the trick must respect byte order.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: scan for newlines or delimiters in log, CSV or HTTP parsers. In JS, view the bytes through a `BigUint64Array` or `Uint32Array`. • Native / systems: `strlen`/`memchr`-style scanning where SIMD intrinsics are unavailable or not portable. • Embedded: 32-bit SWAR on MCUs without SIMD halves or quarters the time to scan a frame for a sync byte. • Data / databases: ASCII validation and structural-character search, as in the scalar fallback of simdjson.
Watch out: short inputs do not amortize the setup, so keep a byte loop for heads and tails. The bit tricks depend on byte order, and on strict-alignment CPUs the unaligned loads may trap or be slow.
Seen in Netty: 5337d3eeb4 2021-01-15 added `ByteBufUtil.firstIndexOf`/`SWARByteSearch`: an unrolled 0-7 byte prefix, then a loop over `_getLongLE`, used only when `PlatformDependent.isUnaligned()` holds. • 0daaec3ac2 2023-01-27 reused SWAR to find (CR)LF in HTTP/1.1 line and header parsing. • 0a1781c863 2024-03-27 added `SWARUtil.applyPattern` with a "test for any set bit before computing the index" step. a38a85c908 2024-04-09 added `lastIndexOf`, 3bd40777a0 2024-04-04 added `bytesBefore`, and b9af2e686a 2024-04-15 switched call sites to `indexOf`. • ed63318c9a 2024-03-25 made `AsciiStringUtil.toLowerCase/UpperCase` use a SWAR `containsUpperCase` with an unrolled int/short/byte tail. It returns `this` when there is nothing to convert.

### netty-088 — Compare and hash byte strings a word at a time
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: Compute equality, constant-time equality and hashes over 8-byte (or 4-byte) loads instead of single bytes. Handle the tail with a short 4/2/1 cascade, and fold case-insensitivity into the arithmetic with a bit mask.
Why it is faster: Wide loads cut the number of memory operations and loop iterations by 4-8x. For ASCII, masking each byte with `0x1f` maps upper and lower case to the same value, so a case-insensitive hash needs no lowercase copy and works on whole words. A compact tail (4, then 2, then 1) replaces an 8-way switch, which keeps the method under the inlining budget. Constant-time compares get the same speedup by XOR-accumulating words.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: case-insensitive header maps can hash with the `0x1f` or `0x20` mask instead of calling `toLowerCase()`. • Native / systems: wyhash and xxHash-style word loads. Use `memcmp` on whole words for keys. • Data / databases: hash join keys and case-insensitive SQL identifiers can be hashed per word.
Watch out: a hash that depends on byte order gives different results on big-endian machines, which matters if the hash is persisted or shared. Constant-time compares are slower on purpose, so use them only where timing leaks matter.
Seen in Netty: c2de195f87 2015-04-13 made `AsciiString.equals` compare 8-byte words via `Unsafe.getLong`: 10 bytes went from 118.8M to 248.1M ops/s, and 1,000 bytes from 2.87M to 16.9M ops/s. • b4b791353d 2015-10-28 added `hashCodeAscii`, which hashes 8 bytes per step with `value & 0x1f1f1f1f` case folding and a fast path for small inputs. 9cfa467554 2016-10-21 fixed its byte-order dependence on big-endian machines. • 70651cc58d 2016-06-22 added a word-at-a-time `equalsConstantTime` (XOR-accumulate) for HPACK. 03ad809a3b 2019-10-23 uses it only for sensitive headers, with an early-exit compare for everything else. • a258cd6f42 2019-03-07 shared one `diff` offset and a 4/2/1 tail across `equals`, `equalsConstantTime` and `hashCodeAscii`: HpackUtil equals SMALL went from 17.2M to 29.2M ops/s.

### netty-089 — Hoist invariant loads and calls out of loops, and cache derived values that never change
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: Read a field, getter, status word or computed address once into a local (or once per object lifetime into a field) instead of re-reading it inside the loop or on every call.
Why it is faster: Compilers cannot hoist loads they cannot prove invariant: fields that may alias, getters behind virtual calls, volatile or memory-mapped state, native calls. Each repeated read is a load or a call, and a chain of dependent loads (context, then channel, then event loop) also stalls the pipeline on cache misses. A local copy can stay in a register. A value derived once at init, such as base address plus offset, removes checks and arithmetic from every access.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: in JS, hoist `arr.length`, property lookups and closures out of hot loops. • Embedded: snapshot a status register into a local once per ISR rather than reading the MMIO register repeatedly. • Native / systems: cache `data()` pointers in views, and precompute DMA descriptor addresses. • Data / databases: resolve the column offset once per batch, not once per row.
Watch out: a cached copy goes stale if the source can change. Hoist only within a scope where the value is truly invariant, or invalidate the cache explicitly. For volatile or shared state, see the concurrency entry on reading shared state once.
Seen in Netty: 243264efb0 2008-08-31 hoisted `getWriteSpinCount()` out of the write loop. 77742f5cf9 2009-05-21 NETTY-156 reads `readyOps()` once per key and tests bits locally instead of calling `isReadable()`/`isWritable()`. dd5354858e 2010-02-02 removed repeated `Thread.currentThread()` calls. • c0462c0c3b 2014-06-26 copies `freeBytes`, `subpages` and `pageSize` into locals inside `PoolChunk` hot methods. 1547876e97 2013-08-09 caches `out.size()` outside a loop. • 0d7320ca4c 2025-02-04 caches the context's executor in one field, replacing the dependent-load chain from context to channel to event loop. • b23c19dfc6 2026-10-15 caches the chunk root address and stores `address + startIndex` at init. The checks it replaced were 3.3% of I/O-thread CPU in a Pulsar broker during gathering writes.

### netty-090 — Flatten dispatch chains: precomputed capability bits and hand-inlined hops
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: When an event passes through a chain of handlers, compute once per handler class a bit mask of the callbacks it implements, and skip the handlers that would only forward the event. Inline the "find next and invoke" hops by hand so the chain costs one call per handler that does work.
Why it is faster: A naive pipeline makes a virtual call per hop, even into adapters that only forward, and each hop uses up inlining budget and stack depth. An integer mask tested with `&` replaces `instanceof` or `EnumSet` checks. Skipping no-op handlers removes whole calls. Collapsing wrapper methods lets the JIT inline from the dispatch site into the actual handler.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Express or Koa middleware stacks can precompute which hooks each layer defines and compile the stack into a flat loop. • Native / systems: netfilter-style hook lists filtered by flags, and threaded-code dispatch in interpreters. • Data / databases: a plan executor can drop pass-through operators at plan time. ECS game engines iterate only the systems registered for a component mask.
Watch out: the masks are derived by reflection or annotation, so a handler that overrides a method without updating its marker will be skipped silently. Cache the masks per class, not per instance.
Seen in Netty: 24e1f936a8 2012-06-09 "Use simpler bit mask operation for pipeline traversal": `DIR_INBOUND = 0x1`, `DIR_OUTBOUND = 0x80000000`. 13d7ee1b2f 2012-06-03 replaced getter calls with field accesses, and 4440386494 2012-06-01 caches `channel` in the context. • b8866aef33 2013-08-30 precomputes `inbound`/`outbound` flags so that traversal does not run `instanceof` per hop. • 8fdf373557 2019-01-22 (and the 4.1 backport 8f7ef1cabb 2019-04-09) added `ChannelHandlerMask`: 17 event bits, a `@Skip` annotation on adapter methods, and a per-class cache. propagateEvent through 4 extra handlers went from 267k to 825k ops/s (about 3x). • 0d7320ca4c 2025-02-04 inlined the inbound operations by hand: propagateEvent went from 6.52M to 6.90M ops/s. a9d93f9524 2026-01-07 did the same for outbound.

### netty-091 — Keep reflection off hot paths: direct type tests, cached lookups, method handles, compile-time stubs
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: Reflective calls such as `Class.isArray`, `Field.get` and `Method.invoke` are slow and block inlining. On hot paths, use direct type tests, cache what reflection discovers, call through constant `MethodHandle`s, or link directly against stubs.
Why it is faster: Reflection boxes arguments, runs access checks and goes through native or generic stubs that the JIT treats as opaque. A `static final MethodHandle` is a constant to the JIT and inlines like a direct call. A cached field array or Unsafe offset turns a lookup into a load. Reflection on init paths also runs interpreted before warmup, so startup pays for it too.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: serializers should generate accessors or use `MethodHandle`/`VarHandle`, and DI containers should cache metadata per class. In .NET, use compiled delegates instead of `MethodInfo.Invoke`. • Native / systems: Go encoders cache `reflect.Type` field indexes. Python and Rust FFI layers precompute struct offsets. • Data / databases: ORMs and row mappers can codegen the per-column setter once per schema.
Watch out: caches keyed by `Class` can pin classloaders, so use weak keys. A `MethodHandle` held in a non-final field is not a JIT constant and loses most of the gain.

### netty-092 — Access memory by raw address with wide loads, and keep a safe fallback chain
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: For buffer reads and writes, compute `base + offset` once and do raw, possibly unaligned, multi-byte loads and stores, skipping the wrapper object's position, limit and byte-order logic. Detect the capability once at startup, with ordered fallbacks: raw access, then typed views, then byte-by-byte.
Why it is faster: Wrapper APIs such as `ByteBuffer` add bounds checks, mutable cursor state and byte swapping to every access. Raw access with `Unsafe` becomes one machine load. With a precomputed array base offset, heap and off-heap memory share one code path, and large copies become one `copyMemory`. On runtimes without Unsafe, `VarHandle` byte-array and byte-buffer views still let the JIT emit a single wide load in the right byte order, instead of 8 byte loads plus shifts. If capability detection breaks, the code silently falls back to the slow path, so the detection itself needs care.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: in JS, read multi-byte fields through `DataView` with explicit byte order, or through aligned typed-array views, not byte by byte. • Native / systems: the `memcpy`-into-integer idiom, or `u32::from_le_bytes` in Rust, compiles to one load and is alignment-safe. • Data / databases: mmap-based page access can read fixed-width columns as words. • Embedded: check the core's unaligned-access support (for example Cortex-M0 has none) before using word loads on packed protocol structs.
Watch out: raw access bypasses every safety net, so one wrong offset corrupts memory silently. Runtimes restrict these APIs over time (Unsafe removal, module encapsulation), so keep the fallback chain tested, and benchmark the fallback too.

### netty-093 — Cheaper check arithmetic: fold several range tests into one sign test, detect overflow by wrapping
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: Combine several range conditions into a single comparison using bit operations. Detect overflow by computing the result once and testing its sign. Encode states so that a validity check is one compare, and use sentinels instead of null.
Why it is faster: `(index | length | (index+length) | (capacity-(index+length))) < 0` tests four conditions with one branch, because any negative term sets the sign bit. Computing `next = cnt + inc` once and checking `next < 0` replaces a subtract-and-compare, and the same `next` is reused for the CAS. If live reference counts are always even, the liveness check becomes a parity test. A sentinel `EMPTY` array instead of null removes a null-check branch.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: use `__builtin_add_overflow` or Rust `checked_add`, and unsigned compares (`(size_t)i < n`) that cover the negative and too-large cases at once. • Web / backend: in JS, `(i >>> 0) < len` folds the negative check into the upper-bound check. • Data / databases: length-prefix validation in wire-protocol decoders.
Watch out: these tricks rely on two's-complement wraparound. In C and C++, signed overflow is undefined behaviour, so use unsigned types or builtins there.
Seen in Netty: ffe7aafd82 2015-10-09 added `AbstractByteBuf.isInvalid(...)` with the OR-sign trick. • 01523e7835 2016-07-20 / 3fa8f31055 2016-08-02 made refcount `retain(increment)` test `(nextCnt = refCnt + increment) < 0`. • a95fd91bc6 2018-04-04 assigns `EMPTY_BYTES` on free instead of null, which removes a check from `capacity()`. 35161ad174 2019-02-28 made `isAccessible()` test `rawCnt == 2 || ... || (rawCnt & 1) == 0`, so the common exact values short-circuit. • feb5326025 2025-07-10 drops the `capacity-(index+length)` term when the capacity is trusted (`isOutOfBoundsTrustedCapacity`), which leaves fewer operations for the JIT to fold.

### netty-094 — Make per-access safety checks switchable with a constant flag
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: Guard redundant per-access checks (bounds, liveness) with a `static final` or compile-time flag, so trusted deployments can turn them off and the compiler deletes the checks entirely.
Why it is faster: A liveness check on every `getByte` is a volatile load of the reference count. That load costs time and also stops the JIT from reordering and hoisting the surrounding code. A constant flag that is false lets the compiler remove the branch and everything behind it. A best-effort plain read is enough for a check that is advisory anyway.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: use `NDEBUG`-gated asserts, or checked iterators that are on in debug builds only. • Embedded: keep a debug build with full checks, and a release build that relies on validation at the boundaries. • Data / databases: skip constraint checks during a trusted bulk load, and validate afterwards.
Watch out: with checks off, use-after-free and out-of-bounds bugs become silent corruption (or a JVM crash with Unsafe). Keep them on by default, and turn them off only after testing with them enabled.
Seen in Netty: 9697afc106 2015-10-09 added `-Dio.netty.buffer.bytebuf.checkAccessible=false`, with `ByteBufBenchmark`. • c78be33443 2018-09-03 added the static final `io.netty.buffer.checkBounds` around every `AbstractByteBuf` range check. • e542a2cf26 2018-09-07 reads the refcount non-volatilely for the accessibility check: setGetLong went from 217M to 281M ops/s. 4a5712f8d9 2019-12-05 removed duplicate `ensureAccessible()` calls.

### netty-095 — Power-of-two arithmetic with bit operations: clz for log2, masks instead of modulo
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: Round up to a power of two and compute log2 with count-leading-zeros or bit smearing instead of loops. Size tables and rings to powers of two so that `x & (n-1)` replaces `x % n`.
Why it is faster: Integer division takes tens of cycles and a shift loop takes O(bits) iterations with branches. `numberOfLeadingZeros` is a single LZCNT or CLZ instruction, and a mask is one AND. A masked index is also never negative and wraps around without a branch, which simplifies probe loops. Choosing the variant once at construction (power-of-two length or not) keeps the hot path free of the test.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `__builtin_clz` for allocator size classes. Ring buffers such as io_uring SQ/CQ and LMAX use masked indexes. • Web / backend: use `Math.clz32` in JS, and round-robin load balancing with a mask. • Embedded: MCUs without a hardware divider benefit most, so size every queue to a power of two. • Data / databases: hash table capacity and bucket selection.
Watch out: forgetting `x--` before the smear doubles exact powers of two, a bug Netty shipped for 10 months. Forcing power-of-two sizes can waste up to half the memory, and masking hashes needs good low bits.
Seen in Netty: a2f232720b 2013-06-20 replaced `while (n < req) n <<= 1` with the `x |= x>>>1 ... x++` smear in `normalizeCapacity`. 15d11289b0 2014-04-14 fixed the missing decrement first: a 1,024-byte request used to get 2,048 bytes. 1bce46dbb3 2014-03-17 uses `numberOfLeadingZeros` for log2. • 722f6c5e1a 2014-03-10 picks an executor chooser once at construction: `idx & (len-1)` for power-of-two counts, instead of `Math.abs(idx % len)`. • 7aac50a79a 2015-04-13 uses masks in place of two modulus operations per probe in the primitive maps, and c5faa142fb 2016-05-08 made the probe step `(index+1) & mask` with no conditionals. a47685b243 2015-10-28 forces the leak-sampling interval to a power of two. 6af56ffe76 2016-06-23 enforces power-of-two HPACK tables. • 71b7dbc009 2020-03-24 replaced the `PoolThreadCache.log2` loop with `31 - numberOfLeadingZeros`. 23601902ab 2021-09-01 replaced the capacity-doubling loop with `findNextPositivePowerOfTwo`. 7c4311df5c 2024-01-29 / 31e3c9c601 2024-02-09 size the timer wheel the same way, and 27dea9100e 2025-05-20 uses bit operations instead of modulo in the WebSocket encoder.

### netty-096 — Write multi-byte constants and integers with one wide store
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: Emit a 2-, 3-, 4- or 8-byte value (CRLF, an int, an id) as a single short, medium, int or long store, byte-swapped if needed, instead of one store per byte or a copy from a constant array.
Why it is faster: Each per-byte store on an abstract buffer pays a bounds check and an index update. Copying from a constant array adds setup cost. One wide store does all the bytes at once, and with unaligned access it costs the same as a single byte store.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: serialize headers with `memcpy(&p, &v, 4)` or Rust `to_be_bytes`. • Web / backend: use `DataView.setUint32` instead of four byte writes in a JS binary encoder. • Embedded: packed 32-bit stores into TX FIFOs.
Watch out: byte order. Wide stores must match the wire order, and a missing swap can pass tests on one architecture and fail on another.
Seen in Netty: 0e8fb21554 2013-05-24 writes a `byte[] CRLF` in one call instead of two `writeByte` calls. • df568c739e 2017-07-04 writes CRLF as `writeShort` and three-byte constants as `writeMedium` in the HTTP, STOMP and Redis encoders, and replaces two `setByte` calls with one `setShort`. • e19c91f73a 2024-04-11 made `DefaultChannelId.writeInt/writeLong` use `PlatformDependent.putInt/putLong` with a byte swap. 900ad15179 2026-04-17 later removed the byte array altogether and kept the id in fields.

### netty-097 — Word-wide bit processing: XOR masks and bit buffers per 32 or 64 bits
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: Byte-wise transforms with a repeating key (XOR masking), and bit-level readers, should work on 32- or 64-bit words. Replicate the key into a word, or keep a 64-bit bit accumulator, and handle the tail separately.
Why it is faster: XORing a long processes 8 bytes with one load, one XOR and one store, instead of 8 iterations with index arithmetic. A bit reader that refills a 64-bit accumulator in large chunks touches the source buffer far less often than one that refills a byte at a time.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: WebSocket libraries in any language, and in JS, `Uint32Array` views for masking. • Native / systems: Huffman, deflate and video bitstream readers use 64-bit bit buffers, and RAID parity is computed word by word. • Embedded: simple XOR ciphers and scramblers on 32-bit MCUs.
Watch out: the key must be rotated to match the starting offset and byte order. Off-by-one errors in tail handling corrupt only the last few bytes, so test odd lengths.
Seen in Netty: 4eb1529d2c 2014-10-02 made WebSocket unmasking 4 bytes at a time with a byte-order-adjusted int mask: "up to 50%" more throughput. • c13419750d 2014-07-25 widened the `Bzip2BitReader/Writer` bit buffer from int to long: 13% faster in general. • 425b5b8321 2022-08-08 widened WebSocket masking to 8 bytes: `setLong(i, getLong(i) ^ longMask)`, then an int tail and a byte tail, with JMH benchmarks. 6debb1bc0c 2022-11-09 dropped the intermediate mask array and uses the int directly.

### netty-098 — Match short known tokens as packed integer constants
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: To recognize "GET", "Host", "HTTP/1.1" or "Accept", read the bytes as an int or long and compare against a precomputed constant after a length and first-byte filter. Return a shared, interned object on a match.
Why it is faster: One integer compare replaces a byte loop or a `String` allocation plus `equals`. Returning a cached constant avoids allocating a new string for the most common header names. Later lookups can then use reference equality.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: u32 compares for HTTP methods in C servers, and FourCC or magic-number checks in media and file parsers. • Data / databases: SQL keyword detection in tokenizers. • Embedded: command-word matching in AT-command or Modbus-style parsers.
Watch out: the constants depend on byte order and on case. HTTP header names are case-insensitive, so mismatches must fall through to the general path.
Seen in Netty: 0daaec3ac2 2023-01-27 added `GET_AS_INT`, `POST_AS_INT`, `HTTP_1_1_AS_LONG`, `HOST_AS_INT`, `CONNECTION_AS_LONG_0`/`_SHORT_1` and `CONTENT_AS_LONG` to `HttpRequestDecoder`. It switches on first character and length, and returns `AsciiString.cached("Host")` and similar constants. • e2c8a97747 2024-02-08 added the 6-byte `Accept` header as `ACCEPT_AS_LONG`.

### netty-099 — Branch on a cheap discriminator (first byte, length, reference) before a full compare
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: When matching input against several constants, switch on the first character or the length first, or test reference equality against interned constants. Only then run a content compare, and only against the one or two remaining candidates.
Why it is faster: Several sequential `equals` or `regionMatches` calls each repeat setup and compare work. A switch on one byte prunes most candidates with a single jump table. When constants are interned, `==` settles the common case without reading the content.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: lexers and Redis-style command dispatch can switch on the first byte, then memcmp. • Web / backend: router tables bucketed by path length or first segment. • Data / databases: enum or keyword parsing in query languages.
Watch out: reference equality works only if every producer of the value uses the interned constant. Always keep the content compare as a fallback.
Seen in Netty: a979433f91 2009-06-15 switches on `line.charAt(0)` instead of `startsWith`, and f059f91ff4 2009-06-15 restructures the header loop the same way. 2ac79dcb74 2010-04-16 makes `HttpClientCodec` switch on the method's first character before calling `equals`. • 2e433889b2 2019-02-04 has `DateFormatter.tryParseMonth` compare characters directly instead of calling `regionMatches` 12 times: 3-15% faster (Dec: 3.78M to 4.34M ops/s). b7ceeb1797 2019-01-30 adds an `==` fast path to `HttpMethod.equals`. • cce3d5b636 2024-01-31 has `HpackStaticTable` switch on length and then compare `header == METHOD.value()`. 664f20315c 2025-04-02 makes `HttpMethod.valueOf` compare against interned "GET"/"POST".

### netty-100 — Do not rely on escape analysis on hot paths: hoist captured callbacks and avoid temporary views
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: Escape analysis can remove temporary allocations, such as capturing lambdas, varargs arrays and view objects, only if everything inlines. On hot paths that cross non-inlined boundaries, keep reusable callbacks in fields and avoid temporary wrapper objects.
Why it is faster: A JIT allocates a short-lived object on the stack, or scalar-replaces it, only if it can prove the object never escapes. Proving that needs every method that touches the object to inline. A capturing lambda passed into a native completion path, or a `duplicate()` view passed through a megamorphic call, escapes, and so it allocates on every event. Hoisting the callback to a final field, or removing the view, makes the allocation-free outcome deterministic.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: in JS, create bound handlers once rather than an arrow function per event (the same idea as React's `useCallback`). • Native / systems: store a C++ functor once instead of building a `std::function` per call, and in Go keep closures out of hot loops (check with `-gcflags=-m`). • Data / databases: per-row lambdas in query engines should be compiled once per plan.
Watch out: whether escape analysis succeeds changes between JIT versions and with code size. Verify with allocation profiling (JFR, async-profiler alloc mode) rather than assuming.
