Seen in Netty: f9888acfdd 2017-12-16 added a single-argument `addLast` overload so the common call does not allocate a varargs array. • 797f1180c7 2025-01-17 removed `duplicate()` from the byte cursors so that they scalarize. • d7108a36bb 2025-12-13 keeps `LocalChannel`'s `finishReadTask` in a field. a0faa5e3c7 2026-09-14 caches the io_uring completion callback in a final field: JFR showed lambda allocations drop from 84 events (about 78 MB of event-loop allocation) to 0 events (about 17 MB) at about 11k req/s.

### netty-101 — Make configuration and singletons compile-time constants
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: Store feature flags, singleton references and method handles in `static final` (or `const`/`constexpr`) fields that are initialized eagerly, so the compiler treats them as constants and folds away null checks and disabled branches.
Why it is faster: The JIT treats a `static final` as a constant once the class is initialized. A lazily initialized field needs a null check and a branch on every access, and a mutable flag must be re-read. A constant lets the compiler delete dead code, for example a disabled check. It also lets a `MethodHandle` inline.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: use module-level `const` bindings in JS, and Go package-level variables initialized once. • Native / systems: `constexpr` tables and flags, and link-time constants for feature toggles. • Embedded: compile-time configuration (`#define` or const in flash) instead of RAM variables read on every call.
Watch out: eager initialization moves cost to startup. Constants cannot be changed at runtime, so a flag must be decided before the class loads.
Seen in Netty: 3ad55eb839 2016-03-21 made `slowThreadLocalMap` `static final` and eagerly initialized, removing null-check branches from FastThreadLocal's slow path. c9364616c8 2015-10-29 fixed the benchmark to run on `FastThreadLocalThread`: 55,452 versus 35,482 ops/s for the JDK `ThreadLocal` (+56%). • c78be33443 2018-09-03 made the bounds-check switch a static final, so disabling it deletes the checks.

### netty-102 — Remove divides, multiplies and data-dependent branches from the critical path
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: Replace a division by a constant with a multiply by its reciprocal and a shift. Replace binary searches or range-check chains with a dense table indexed by a shifted value. Store precomputed products, such as offsets, instead of recomputing `id * stride`.
Why it is faster: Integer division costs tens of cycles. `(x * 1374389535L) >> 37` computes `x / 100` exactly for non-negative ints in a few cycles. A binary search over size-class boundaries has data-dependent branches that mispredict, while `table[(size+31) >> 5]` is one shift, one compare and one load. A stored offset removes a multiply from a chain of dependent instructions.
Applies elsewhere: Embedded: MCUs without a divider: reciprocal multiplication for unit conversion and decimal formatting. • Native / systems: malloc size-class mapping tables (tcmalloc), and itoa routines. • Data / databases: histogram bucket selection and page-slot offset arrays.
Watch out: magic reciprocals are exact only over a stated input range, so assert or test that range. Lookup tables must stay small enough to remain in L1.
Seen in Netty: 142027220a 2023-09-22 made `HttpStatusClass.valueOf(int)` use `statusArray[fast_div100(code)]` instead of five `contains()` checks, with `HttpStatusValueOfBenchmark`. • 8134aefc28 2025-07-08 replaced `Arrays.binarySearch(SIZE_CLASSES, size)` with a `SIZE_INDEXES` byte-array lookup. • 2b73259c50 2025-08-05 stores precomputed `segmentOffsets[i]` in the segment free list, and the offset doubles as the segment identity.

### netty-103 — Decode variable-length integers branch-free, a word at a time
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: Read 4 bytes at once, find the terminating byte with `~w & 0x80808080` and a trailing-zero count, and compact the 7-bit groups with masks and shifts. This replaces a loop with one branch per byte.
Why it is faster: Varint lengths vary unpredictably across messages, so the per-byte "continue?" branch mispredicts often. Computing the length arithmetically removes those branches, and one wide load replaces several byte loads.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: protobuf and LEB128 decoders, and WebAssembly binary parsing. • Data / databases: row and index formats that use varints (SQLite, RocksDB). • Web / backend: JS protobuf runtimes reading through a `DataView`.
Watch out: reading 4 bytes needs 4 readable bytes, so keep a bytewise path for the end of the buffer. Malformed (over-long) encodings still need validation.
Seen in Netty: 09b4ea6a1f 2024-05-15 made `ProtobufVarint32FrameDecoder.readRawVarint32` work word-at-a-time, with separate 24-bit and 40-bit slow paths and a `VarintDecodingBenchmark`. • 5880f3662a 2025-05-20 unrolled the MQTT variable-length int encoding: about 2x.

### netty-104 — Shape loops by hand: fuse passes the compiler will not merge, split loops that defeat it
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: When two passes run over the same data (copy, then validate), fuse them into one loop if the compiler does not. When a fused loop mixes a vectorizable search with branchy work, split it into separate loops.
Why it is faster: Fusing halves memory traffic and loop overhead. Splitting (loop fission) lets each loop be simple enough for the JIT to optimize: a tight SWAR search loop, then a copy. Compilers do both transformations only sometimes, and the generated assembly is the only reliable check.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: validate UTF-8 while copying, and checksum while copying (as TCP stacks do). • Data / databases: tokenize and count in one pass, or separate a predicate scan from materialization so the scan vectorizes. • Web / backend: in JS, run one pass that both escapes and measures output length instead of two.
Watch out: fusion can make a loop too complex to vectorize, and fission adds a second pass over memory. Measure, and read the generated assembly.
Seen in Netty: 0daaec3ac2 2023-01-27 split the HTTP/1.1 line-parsing loop into a SWAR find loop and a separate copy loop, noting that "loop fusion" was "not working". • 16835704b4 2026-07-10 made `AsciiString.cached(String)` do the char-to-byte conversion and the Latin-1 check in one loop, after PrintAssembly showed C2 did not fuse them. This fixed a regression from ef1097214f 2026-07-08.

### netty-105 — Avoid runtime-generated identity hashes: use a cheap counter id and identity-based sentinels
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: Give objects that serve as hash keys a cheap precomputed id (a sequence number) instead of relying on the runtime's identity hash. Use a singleton sentinel with `==` equality instead of boxed values in concurrent maps.
Why it is faster: On HotSpot, the first `System.identityHashCode` call on an object generates a hash and installs it in the object header. That is slow, and it can inflate a lock. A `long` counter is one increment. Using a boxed `Boolean` as a map value makes `remove(key, value)` run an `instanceof`, a cast and `equals`, while a sentinel singleton compares by reference.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: in JS, key Maps by a numeric id rather than using objects with WeakMap-style identity when hashing is hot. • Data / databases: sequence ids as cache keys for rows or pages. • Native / systems: ECS entity ids instead of hashing pointers.
Watch out: counter ids must be unique within the map's scope. A per-connection counter is fine for per-connection maps, but not for a global one.
Seen in Netty: 1e3fe3ffc3 2012-08-25 made `Channel.hashCode()` return the precomputed channel id instead of calling `System.identityHashCode`. • fd0ae840b6 2016-12-01 keeps the leak detector's map as `ConcurrentMap<DefaultResourceLeak, LeakEntry>` with a `LeakEntry.INSTANCE` sentinel. • f1cfbe1ea6 2024-01-12 made HTTP/2 `DefaultStream.hashCode()` use a per-connection increasing `identity` long.

### netty-106 — Bound long uninterruptible operations, and remove the workaround when the runtime fixes it
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: A single huge intrinsic copy can block the runtime's safepoints, and with them GC and profiling. Split it into bounded chunks, and gate the chunking on runtime versions that still need it.
Why it is faster: A multi-megabyte `Unsafe.copyMemory` on Java 8 runs with no safepoint poll. Every other thread waiting for a GC pause stalls until it finishes, so a single copy can cause latency spikes across the whole process. Copying in 1 MiB chunks bounds the stall. Newer JVMs handle this themselves, and there the chunk loop is pure overhead.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: yield during large array copies or JSON serialization on the JS main thread. • Native / systems: chunked memcpy in cooperative schedulers or kernels with preemption points. • Data / databases: bounded batch sizes for vacuum or compaction so that foreground latency stays flat.
Watch out: chunking adds loop overhead. Gate it on the runtime version or capability, and delete it when it is no longer needed.
Seen in Netty: 7252934f9b 2014-05-20 made `PlatformDependent0.copyMemory` loop in `UNSAFE_COPY_THRESHOLD = 1 MiB` pieces. • 182ffdaf6d 2018-08-18 chunks only on Java 8 and older.

### netty-107 — Inline tiny bulk operations below a size threshold
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: Netty
Principle: General bulk routines (memset, memcpy, native fills) have a fixed call overhead. For small sizes, an inline loop of 8-byte stores is faster. Above a threshold, call the bulk routine.
Why it is faster: A runtime bulk call can cost a thread-state transition, argument checks or a non-intrinsic call, which takes longer than zeroing 64 bytes with 8 stores. On CPUs that require alignment, writing single bytes until the address is aligned keeps the word stores legal.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: inline small, fixed-size memcpy and memset (compilers do this when the size is a constant, so make it one). • Web / backend: in JS, call `TypedArray.fill` or `set` only above a measured length. • Embedded: word-store loops for short DMA descriptor clears.
Watch out: the crossover point depends on the runtime and CPU. Benchmark it and keep it as one named constant.
Seen in Netty: 5a58f0431e 2023-11-13 made `UnsafeByteBufUtil.setZero` use a `putLong` loop plus a byte tail for lengths up to 64 bytes, with `zeroTillAligned` when unaligned access is not allowed. Larger sizes still call `Unsafe.setMemory`. `ByteBufZeroingBenchmark` was added.

### netty-108 — Use plain fields for thread-confined state: no volatile, no atomics, no locks
Lens: concurrency · Source: Netty
Principle: State touched by only one thread, or already ordered by an executor's or lock's happens-before edge, needs no volatile, atomic or lock. Use plain fields and let the compiler optimize them.
Why it is faster: A volatile write emits a StoreLoad fence, an atomic does a locked read-modify-write, and both stop the compiler from caching the value in a register or reordering code around it. In an event-loop design most per-connection state is touched by exactly one thread, so those costs buy nothing. Removing them also makes the surrounding code easier to optimize. Counters that only sample or approximate do not need cross-thread visibility either.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: per-connection state in actor systems (Akka, Orleans) or Node worker threads should be plain fields. Use JS `Atomics` only for memory that is actually shared. • Native / systems: per-worker counters can use `Cell` instead of `AtomicUsize` in Rust, or `memory_order_relaxed` for statistics in C++. • Embedded: remove `volatile` from variables that are not shared with an ISR or DMA engine. • Data / databases: state private to a query executor thread needs no latch.
Watch out: the confinement must actually hold. A single access from another thread (a metrics reader, a close called from a user thread) makes it a data race. Netty's refcount relaxation was reverted for exactly this reason. Document and assert the owner thread.
Seen in Netty: a462d0e85e 2010-02-19 removed volatile from decoder state "protected by happens-before condition... Executor's internal synchronization". e8c320c6c3 2010-02-19 removed volatile from fields already under `synchronized`, and aa8e48df9f 2010-02-19 from fields guarded by a `BlockingQueue`. 0857f398e0 2010-02-09 made `inWriteNowLoop` non-volatile. • 3b368125b0 2011-12-08 replaced `AtomicReference`/`AtomicBoolean` in `ReplayingDecoder`. 31cebd7ce2 2012-07-03: "No need for AtomicBoolean as we run in the eventloop". • cedcee3f42 2013-02-09 made the leak detector's sampling counter per-instance and non-volatile, and f4e128b807 2013-07-23 made its `disabled` flag non-volatile. 12f1d96914 2013-03-08 read the refcount non-volatilely. That one was reverted in f23d68b42f 2014-02-07, because the refcount is a real cross-thread value. • 0b5e75a614 2016-09-13 made `IdleStateHandler` fields (touched only by the event loop) non-volatile and narrowed its state from int to byte. dcd93e3be0 2016-05-25 made cached Runnables non-volatile: in the worst case they are recreated. 4d8132ff24 2016-07-05 made `DefaultPromise.listeners` non-volatile. • b7b6156505 2019-11-22 dropped the atomic for handler state once pipeline operations became loop-confined. 69575cf5a6 2019-10-13 removed a CAS that ran inside `synchronized`. 361ad99ebd 2022-10-18 uses a plain `state =` store for a thread-local recycler handle.

### netty-109 — Shrink critical sections: validate, free and iterate outside the lock
Lens: concurrency · Source: Netty
Principle: Hold a lock only around the shared mutation itself. Validate arguments before taking it. Free memory, call out to other code and iterate after releasing it, working on a copy. Never release and re-acquire the same lock within one operation.
Why it is faster: Under contention, throughput is roughly one divided by the hold time. Expensive work done under a lock (munmap, logging, notifying listeners, taking a nested lock) serializes every thread that waits. Releasing and re-acquiring a lock doubles the hand-off cost and opens a race window. Reading final or immutable fields needs no lock at all.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: call `munmap` or `free` outside allocator locks, and use RCU-style "swap under the lock, reclaim later". • Data / databases: reduce Postgres-style LWLock scope, and validate the request before taking row locks. • Web / backend: reload configuration by building the new object outside the lock and swapping one reference.
Watch out: moving work out of a lock can expose state between steps. Check every invariant the lock protected, especially across lock splits.
Seen in Netty: 92bfeeca1b 2015-05-13 keeps the arena lock across `allocateNormal` after a subpage miss, instead of releasing and re-acquiring it. dce0dd9b78 2015-05-25 destroys chunks outside the arena lock. ab1bb9136b 2015-05-20 synchronizes on the per-size subpage head instead of the whole arena. 9687d77b5a 2016-06-15 validates arguments before entering `synchronized`. • 536b83a1a8 2020-02-10 copies resolvers to an array under the lock, then iterates outside it. 4e86f768b9 2020-07-16 moved an increment of a concurrent-safe counter out of `synchronized`. dcfcae685d 2023-01-19 stops locking unpooled chunks and uses a narrower `runsAvailLock` instead of the arena lock. 433aa18b41 2023-08-16 reads the final `elemSize` without the lock. • caf240ced2 2024-11-26 frees the old magazines outside `magazineExpandLock`, avoiding nested locks. d9850cd032 2024-03-15 dropped the lock around a metric that was only ever an estimate.

### netty-110 — Make read-mostly shared state lock-free to read
Lens: concurrency · Source: Netty
Principle: For data that is read on every operation but rarely changed (options, attributes, registries), let readers proceed without a lock. Use a concurrent map, a read-write lock, or an immutable copy-on-write snapshot behind one volatile reference, or give each thread its own cache.
Why it is faster: A monitor around a map read serializes all I/O threads, even though none of them writes. Concurrent maps give lock-free reads. A copy-on-write array gives readers a plain array to binary-search. Writers pay for copying, which is cheap when writes are rare and the structure is small. Per-thread caches of immutable facts, such as per-class metadata, need no synchronization at all.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: routing tables and feature flags as immutable snapshots behind an atomic reference (Go `atomic.Value`, Java `AtomicReference`), and `sync.Map` for read-mostly keys. • Native / systems: RCU in the kernel, and pthread rwlocks for registries. • Data / databases: catalog and schema caches published as versioned immutable snapshots.
Watch out: copy-on-write is O(n) per write, so it is wrong for write-heavy or large structures. Read-write locks can starve writers and cost more than a plain lock when critical sections are tiny.
Seen in Netty: 243264efb0 2008-08-31 made the selector guard a `ReentrantReadWriteLock`, so registrations proceed concurrently against an exclusive select. • 2d9e0f53a5 2014-05-13 replaced a synchronized `IdentityHashMap` with a lazily created 4-bucket `AtomicReferenceArray` with per-bucket locking. adcc2d4b4b 2014-03-10 and 98eb69f169 2015-10-28 moved per-class caches (`isSharable`, handler names) from a synchronized `WeakHashMap` to per-thread maps. • a05955d337 2017-06-14 uses a `ReadWriteLock` instead of class-level `synchronized` in `ReferenceCountedOpenSslContext`. 089c6daff2 2019-08-16 and 254732b43c 2019-12-06 store bootstrap options and attributes in `ConcurrentHashMap` instead of a synchronized `LinkedHashMap`. • 4624b6309d 2020-10-02 rebuilt `DefaultAttributeMap` as a `volatile DefaultAttribute[]` sorted by key id. Readers binary-search without locks. Writers copy, insert (walking backwards, since new ids tend to be larger) and CAS.

### netty-111 — Remove globally shared counters and logs from per-event paths
Lens: concurrency · Source: Netty
Principle: A single atomic counter, id generator or synchronized list that every thread updates on every event makes all threads contend for one cache line. Make the counter per-owner, stripe it (LongAdder or per-core slots), derive it from rarer events, or sample instead of recording every event.
Why it is faster: A contended cache line moves between cores for every write, which costs tens to hundreds of nanoseconds, and gets worse with more cores. A per-owner counter is a plain increment. A striped counter spreads the writes over several lines and sums them on read. Sampling (1 in N, or with probability 2-n) cuts the writes to a shared structure to almost none.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: request metrics in high-QPS services (LongAdder, Prometheus client striping), and Snowflake-style per-node id ranges instead of a global sequence. • Native / systems: per-CPU statistics in kernels, merged on read. • Data / databases: per-core transaction counters, and sampled lock-wait statistics.
Watch out: striped counters make reads expensive and only approximately consistent. Sampling misses rare events, so always keep the first and last records, as Netty does.
Seen in Netty: 42fba015ce 2016-10-21 rebuilt `ResourceLeakDetector` with `ThreadLocalRandom` sampling and a `ConcurrentHashMap`. Before, a gRPC QPS benchmark spent about 3 CPU-seconds per wall-second in it with 12 threads. 74adbcd25d 2017-09-18 then removed all locks: it records onto a lock-free stack with exponentially falling probability, about 20 records per 1M accesses. • 2791f0fefa 2019-09-24 moved the `ScheduledFutureTask` id from a static `AtomicLong` to a plain per-executor counter assigned at enqueue: scheduleLots(100k) went from 346 to 655 ops/s. • 6a11330061 2022-01-12 switched the pinned-memory counter from `AtomicInteger` to a `LongAdder`-based counter. 80c9a41c25 2025-08-21 added a LongAdder `ChunkRegistry`, and ae6017d555 2025-09-17 removed the duplicate per-magazine `AtomicLong usedMemory`, because chunk creation and destruction already account for it.

### netty-112 — Use fetch-and-add instead of CAS loops for counters, and correct invalid results afterwards
Lens: concurrency · Source: Netty
Principle: For reference counts and accounting, apply the change with one atomic add (`getAndAdd`, which is `LOCK XADD` on x86). Detect a now-invalid state after the fact and repair it or throw, instead of reading, checking and CAS-ing in a retry loop.
Why it is faster: A CAS loop under contention fails and retries, and every retry costs another cache-line transfer. XADD always succeeds in one operation. Most operations are valid, so checking afterwards and repairing the rare invalid case (for example, incrementing a count that had already reached zero) is cheaper than validating before every update.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `shared_ptr`-style refcounts use `fetch_add`, and so does memory accounting in allocators. • Web / backend: rate-limiter token counters, using a fetch-and-add then giving back on overdraft. • Data / databases: buffer-pool pin counts and statistics counters.
Watch out: with optimistic updates, other threads can briefly see the invalid intermediate value, so the encoding must make that value harmless (Netty's even/odd scheme). Getting this wrong causes use-after-free.
Seen in Netty: 83a19d5650 2017-09-25 moved retain and release to `getAndAdd`, about 2x faster under contention (reference counting was about 1% of CPU in gRPC). • fc0a4e15ab 2018-11-28 hardened the scheme: even values mean live and the final release goes to odd. Retain stays XADD. Release is a CAS whose first attempt uses a non-volatile read, with `Thread.yield()` after a failed CAS. It is still about 2x the original. e75b7edaa3 2019-04-09 centralized this logic in `ReferenceCountUpdater`. c14efd952d 2018-09-07 initializes the count with a plain store. • 2e0922d773 2018-11-27 tracks direct-memory usage with `addAndGet` instead of a CAS loop. 89e93968ac 2016-12-09 returned to the JDK field updaters because their `addAndGet` is intrinsified.

### netty-113 — Use the weakest memory ordering that is correct: plain initial stores, lazySet, relaxed offers, best-effort reads
Lens: concurrency · Source: Netty
Principle: Initialize fields with plain stores (safe publication covers them). Publish to a single consumer with release-only stores (`lazySet`, ordered puts). Use relaxed queue operations within one thread. Read advisory values with plain loads.
Why it is faster: A full volatile store needs a StoreLoad fence, the most expensive barrier on x86. A release store (`lazySet`) needs only to keep earlier stores in order, which costs almost nothing on x86 and little on ARM. Initializing a volatile in a constructor pays a fence that safe publication already makes unnecessary. A plain read for an advisory check lets the JIT hoist and combine loads.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: use `memory_order_release`/`acquire` or `relaxed` in C++ and Rust lock-free code instead of defaulting to `seq_cst`. • Web / backend: in Java, use `VarHandle.setRelease`/`getAcquire` or `lazySet` for single-writer publication. • Data / databases: single-writer log-tail pointers published with release stores.
Watch out: weaker orderings are easy to get subtly wrong, and x86's strong memory model hides bugs that appear on ARM. Use them only with a clear happens-before argument, and test on weakly ordered hardware.
Seen in Netty: 09100e5043 2014-08-18 changed the MPSC queue's `peekNode` to spin on `next()` instead of re-reading the volatile head, so it does fewer volatile accesses. • e542a2cf26 2018-09-07 reads the refcount non-volatilely for the accessibility check (setGetLong went from 217M to 281M ops/s). c14efd952d 2018-09-07 initializes the refcount with a plain store. • af1dd7c0bc 2022-01-06 switched the `Recycler` to `relaxedOffer`/`relaxedPoll`. f9e765e0ce 2022-04-14 sets the initial refcount through `safeConstructPutInt`, a plain store, with a benchmark. 321098569a 2022-04-07 relies on the default initial value instead of a volatile set. • 06c314f0b5 2023-03-07 replaced volatile operations with ordered ones in the netty5 Recycler and `ReferenceCountUpdater`. 3ad931d466 2023-02-17 relaxed an atomic reset in the decoder hot path.

### netty-114 — Read shared state once per operation: one volatile snapshot instead of many reads
Lens: concurrency · Source: Netty
Principle: Group related settings into one immutable object behind a single volatile reference, and read volatile fields into locals once per operation instead of on every use.
Why it is faster: Each volatile read is a load the compiler cannot cache or reorder, and several separate volatile fields can be observed in an inconsistent combination. One reference read gives a consistent snapshot for the cost of one barrier, and the rest of the operation works on plain locals.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: copy-on-write feature-flag snapshots read once per request. • Native / systems: RCU config pointers in C, and `std::atomic` loaded once per function. • Embedded: copy a shared status struct into a local once per control-loop iteration.
Watch out: a snapshot is stale by design, which is fine for configuration and wrong for values that must reflect concurrent changes during the operation.
Seen in Netty: 0241120ace 2008-09-26 "Optimization.. (reduced volatiles)": `MemoryAwareThreadPoolExecutor` holds a `volatile Settings settings` object for its limits. It also caches each task's estimated size so the estimate is not recomputed. • 6ff87cc20d 2013-07-25 reads `writeSpinCount` once per `doWrite` ("limit volatile reads"). 94ef7dc1b9 2013-04-03 minimized volatile reads in the codec framework. • 867fb70994 2017-05-07 uses a plain volatile read instead of an updater `get()`. 6423e80932 2019-09-09 makes `DefaultPromise.get` read `result` once.

### netty-115 — Lifecycle state as one atomic integer with CAS transitions instead of locks
Lens: concurrency · Source: Netty
Principle: Encode a component's lifecycle (not started, started, shutting down) in one atomic int, and move between states with `compareAndSet`/`getAndSet` instead of synchronized methods. This matters most when every submit checks the state.
Why it is faster: If every `execute()` from a foreign thread takes a lock only to ask "is the worker started?", that lock becomes a global point of contention. A volatile read of the state answers the common case without a lock, and the rare transition costs one CAS.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: lazily starting worker pools, and Go `atomic.CompareAndSwapInt32` on server start. • Native / systems: `std::atomic<int>` lifecycle states, and init-once flags in drivers. • Data / databases: connection state transitions in poolers and proxies.
Watch out: multi-step transitions that must be atomic together still need a lock. Lock-free rewrites of complex executors are easy to get wrong, as Netty's one-day revert shows.
Seen in Netty: 9f712e3291 2011-11-24 / caa925198e 2011-11-24 made `OrderedMemoryAwareThreadPoolExecutor` lock-free. 6b7b822f72 2011-11-25 reverted it the next day, which shows the limits of the approach. • 88124d88ce 2012-08-15 replaced `synchronized` in `BufferedWriteHandler` with an `AtomicBoolean` flush gate. 543cb17acd 2012-10-16 gave `HashedWheelTimer` start/stop an `AtomicInteger workerState`, and 9c921b050f 2012-10-17 simplified stop to `getAndSet(2) != 1`. • 16a85e6cca 2014-03-13 "Remove synchronized bottleneck": `SingleThreadEventExecutor.startThread()` and shutdown use a field-updater CAS loop instead of `synchronized(stateLock)`. d8d822fddd 2014-04-25 removed extra synchronization in the pipeline, and 4222610b2a 2013-07-29 / 6ce8571df3 2013-07-29 removed it in the SPDY and zlib codecs.

### netty-116 — Give the owner thread a private, unsynchronized fast path
Lens: concurrency · Source: Netty
Principle: When a known long-lived thread (an event loop) uses a resource heavily, give it a dedicated instance with plain, non-atomic operations. Shared instances keep their locks and atomics for everyone else. Pools that are only ever used from one thread can drop their double-use guards.
Why it is faster: Even an uncontended lock or CAS costs a locked instruction (tens of cycles on x86). The owner thread's private instance does plain loads and stores. Drawing the line at "known event-loop threads" keeps memory bounded, which per-thread caches for every thread would not.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: per-core run queues in schedulers, and per-connection lookaside allocators (SQLite). • Web / backend: per-worker arenas in Node- or Go-style runtimes, with a shared, locked fallback for other threads. • Data / databases: per-executor-thread buffers, with a shared pool for background threads.
Watch out: an unguarded pool turns a double-release bug into silent corruption. Use it only where ownership is guaranteed by construction.
Seen in Netty: 28fe6a9b91 2024-05-06 gives event-loop threads a dedicated `FastThreadLocal` magazine, which "save[s] 2 atomic operations (write lock/unlock)". 7b4fe3d25c 2024-11-11 lets event-loop-owned magazines use a thread-local Recycler, so only shared magazines pay for MPMC queues. accd981104 2026-01-06 removes more atomics from the thread-local fast path with unguarded recyclers and a local LIFO free list. • e778364ac1 2025-10-02 added `Recycler(boolean unguarded)` and `Recycler(Thread owner, boolean unguarded)`, which skip the per-recycle state CAS and the thread-local lookup.

### netty-117 — Indexed thread-local storage: one array load instead of a hash probe
Lens: concurrency · Source: Netty
Principle: Give each thread-local variable a global integer index when it is created, and store values in a per-thread array (on a custom thread class) indexed directly. Other threads fall back to the standard mechanism. Keep the per-thread slots compact.
Why it is faster: The JDK `ThreadLocal` hashes into a per-thread map with linear probing, which costs a hash, probes and possible stale-entry cleanup. A direct index into an `Object[]` on the thread object is one bounds-checked load. Thread-local state is how allocators and pools avoid locks, so this lookup sits on very hot paths. With very large thread counts (virtual threads), per-thread state needs bounding.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: use direct `__thread`/`thread_local` slots instead of `pthread_getspecific` key lookups. • Web / backend: per-worker context arrays in async runtimes, indexed by a registered slot id. • Data / databases: per-backend state arrays indexed by subsystem id.
Watch out: slot indexes are global and never reused, so every thread's array grows with the number of variables. Per-thread caches multiply memory by the thread count, which matters with virtual threads or goroutine-style concurrency.
Seen in Netty: 2a2a21ec59 2014-06-09 added `FastThreadLocal` with `FastThreadLocalThread`, about 10-20% faster than `ThreadLocal`, and switched the allocator cache, Recycler and codec state to it. 085a61a310 2014-06-17 reworked it into `InternalThreadLocalMap` (`fastGet` versus `slowGet`), and 776ac4ba19 2014-06-14 applied it in more places. • c9364616c8 2015-10-29 corrected the benchmark to run on a `FastThreadLocalThread`: +56% over `ThreadLocal` (55,452 versus 35,482 ops/s). • 010dbe3c73 2018-04-24 stores the per-variable cleanup flags in a BitSet, halving slot usage. dc104de503 2019-10-28 stops populating a per-thread WeakHashMap when it is not needed. • c83c1f5526 2025-04-02 and 2f6d2af01a 2025-01-13 keep thread-local Recyclers and temporary arrays off virtual threads and other non-`FastThreadLocalThread`s. 2233eaa7ac 2025-08-09 made the fallback-thread check O(1) with a `LongLongHashMap` keyed by `threadId>>>6` plus a 64-bit bitmap, instead of a binary search.

### netty-118 — Find and avoid hidden locks and thread stops inside runtime and library calls
Lens: concurrency · Source: Netty
Principle: Innocent-looking library calls (socket state queries, class metadata, Cleaner registration, thread-state inspection) can take internal locks or even pause the target thread. Track the state yourself, cache the answer, or use a cheaper equivalent.
Why it is faster: Such calls do not stand out in code review, but under load they serialize every thread that makes them. A volatile state field updated on transitions answers the question with one load. Calling an operation and handling its failure avoids a racy pre-check that takes a lock. Choosing `isAlive()` over `getState()` avoids an implementation that halts the target thread.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: cache fd state in user-space wrappers, and try the operation and handle EBADF instead of a `fcntl` pre-check. Prefer `kill(pid, 0)` over parsing `/proc`. • Web / backend: watch for global locks in logging frameworks, DNS resolvers and the locale or charset lookups of popular libraries. • Data / databases: catalog lookups that take a global latch should be cached per session.
Watch out: a self-maintained state field must be updated on every transition, error paths included. Otherwise it disagrees with the OS.
Seen in Netty: 80b6a5b7ae 2009-09-21 removed the `isConnected()` pre-check and handles `ClosedChannelException` instead. 60d3502ba1 2010-01-09 keeps a volatile `ST_OPEN/BOUND/CONNECTED/CLOSED` state, because the JDK's `isConnected()`/`isBound()` took a lock that "causes unnecessary contention". • a7d1dc362a 2015-04-18 makes `StringUtil.simpleClassName` avoid `Class.getPackage()`, which synchronizes internally. 862efc618c 2020-02-16 removed `synchronized` from an overridden `fillInStackTrace`. • 3d29bcfc8d 2016-05-23 avoided the JDK Cleaner for direct buffers partly because `Cleaner.clean()` was `static synchronized`. • 139cb8d332 2023-04-24 uses `!isAlive()` instead of `getState() == TERMINATED`. On OpenJ9, `getState` calls `haltThreadForInspection`, which was a scalability bottleneck.

### netty-119 — Pad producer- and consumer-owned hot fields onto separate cache lines, but only where contention is real
Lens: concurrency · Source: Netty
Principle: Fields written by different threads (a queue's head and tail, per-thread hot state) should not share a 64-byte cache line. Pad them apart, keep padded objects sized to a multiple of the line, and skip padding for structures that are numerous and lightly contended.
Why it is faster: When two cores write different fields on the same line, each write invalidates the other core's copy, so the line moves back and forth even though no data is logically shared. Padding removes this. Padding also costs memory (about 128 bytes or more per object), and with many objects that cost can exceed the gain.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `alignas(64)` per-thread counters in C++, Rust's `CachePadded`, and the sequence counters in LMAX Disruptor. • Data / databases: per-core statistics slots, and the lock word separated from hot data. • Embedded: on multicore MCUs, keep variables written by different cores in separate cache lines or separate memory banks.
Watch out: adding a field silently breaks the padding, so measure object sizes with JOL or `sizeof`. Some CPUs prefetch adjacent lines, so 128 bytes may be needed. Padding thousands of objects wastes cache.
Seen in Netty: 32aab3b0b3 2014-06-20 gave `MpscLinkedQueue` the class hierarchy `Pad0`, `HeadRef`, `Pad1`, `TailRef` (16 longs per pad), replacing `AtomicReference`/`PaddedRef` indirection that had "passive false sharing". • cf9dc40990 2022-04-26 removed one 8-byte pad from `InternalThreadLocalMap` after a new field had pushed it from 128 to 136 bytes, which restored a whole number of cache lines. • 36dbdacbed 2024-11-01 uses JCTools' unpadded MPSC queues for the many lightly contended `MemoryRegionCache`s, choosing footprint over padding.

### netty-120 — Stripe shared resources and use tryLock-and-move-on instead of blocking
Lens: concurrency · Source: Netty
Principle: Split a contended resource into N independent stripes. A thread hashes to a preferred stripe and tries its lock. If the lock is busy, the thread moves to the next stripe instead of waiting. Grow the stripe count when tries keep failing, and fall back to a lock-free steal when every stripe is busy.
Why it is faster: Blocking on a busy lock costs a park/unpark (microseconds). Trying another stripe costs one more CAS. Memory grows with measured contention rather than with the thread count, which matters for runtimes with millions of threads. Choosing the least-used stripe, rather than round-robin, avoids hot spots after threads come and go. Sharding a runtime-global structure per event loop removes its lock entirely.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: jemalloc arenas chosen by tryLock, and striped locks in hash tables. • Web / backend: striped connection pools, where a busy shard hands the request to the next one. • Data / databases: a buffer pool partitioned by page-id hash, and striped latches on hot index pages.
Watch out: stripes multiply per-stripe memory and fragment free space, so Netty dropped to a single stripe on heaps of 512 MiB or less. Moving on to another stripe gives up locality.
Seen in Netty: b47fc77522 2012-12-06 created N allocator arenas (N = cores) with threads bound round-robin. 0da48e7e7f 2013-06-14 capped N by the memory budget, e0ef01cf93 2015-06-17 went to 2x cores, and b88a980482 2016-03-14 assigns each new thread cache to the arena with the fewest users. • 67a46b102f 2022-11-16 gave each event loop its own `Cleaner` through FastThreadLocal: 2,995 to 50,096 ops/ms with 10 CPUs, with idle CPU going from 68% to 1.3%. Reactor-Netty H2C went from 132k to 380k req/s. • 188ead1f63 2024-04-23 `AdaptivePoolingAllocator` picks magazines by thread id and guards them with `StampedLock.tryWriteLock`. Repeated failures grow the array up to 2x cores. ec41d5fd36 2025-06-30 starts the array at 1 and allows 2x-length attempts before expanding, which cut memory waste by about 15%. • c29f4a51da 2025-01-24 added `allocateWithoutLock`: when every magazine is busy, the thread takes the next-in-line chunk with `getAndSet`, or polls a central queue. • 07bf4ad46a 2025-10-20 limits the allocator to one stripe on heaps of 512 MiB or less, where striping cost more memory than it saved in contention.

### netty-121 — Embed atomics in the owning object: field updaters or VarHandles instead of Atomic* wrappers
Lens: concurrency · Source: Netty
Principle: Declare a `volatile` primitive or reference field and operate on it through a static field updater (or VarHandle), instead of storing a separate `AtomicLong`/`AtomicReference` object per instance.
Why it is faster: An `Atomic*` field is an extra object per owner: a header, a pointer and an indirection on every access, plus GC work. With a million connections times several fields, that adds up to gigabytes. An updater gives the same CAS semantics on an inline field. Early JDK updaters ran access checks on every call, which Netty worked around until the JDK versions were intrinsified.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: put `std::atomic` members inside the struct rather than heap-allocating them. Intrusive refcounts avoid `shared_ptr` control blocks. • Web / backend: in modern Java, use `VarHandle` on fields. • Data / databases: keep latch words and pin counts inline in buffer descriptors.
Watch out: updaters are tied to field names via reflection, so a refactor can break them at runtime. An inline atomic next to other hot fields can cause false sharing.
Seen in Netty: 1bb003d9ae 2013-01-30 turned 4 `AtomicReference`s in `DefaultChannelHandlerContext` into volatile fields plus updaters, an estimated 2 GB saved at 10 contexts times 1M connections. • 9bee78f91c 2014-02-04 added Unsafe-based updater implementations that skip the JDK's per-call reflection checks, used for refCnt, `DefaultAttributeMap` and `ChannelOutboundBuffer`. Later uses: a64484249c 2014-10-09, 2f7d60f234 2014-05-02, 16a85e6cca 2014-03-13. • 89e93968ac 2016-12-09 went back to the JDK updaters once their `addAndGet` was intrinsified.

### netty-122 — Split reference count: plain increments by the owner, atomic decrements by anyone
Lens: concurrency · Source: Netty
Principle: When only one thread acquires references but any thread may release them, keep two counters: a plain (or lazySet) "up" count written only by the owner, and an atomic "down" count. The object is free when down equals up.
Why it is faster: The acquire path, which is the hot path, does no locked instruction at all. Only releases pay for an atomic, and they are spread over time and threads. Uncontended atomics are cheap on Apple M-series but noticeably slower on x86, so the gain depends on the platform.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: slab allocators counting outstanding objects per slab, and Arc-like handles where only the producer clones. • Web / backend: zero-copy buffer sharing where one I/O thread hands out slices to many consumers. • Data / databases: page pins taken by one scan thread and released by workers.
Watch out: the "free" test must read both counters consistently, and the owner must publish "up" before anyone compares against it. Netty uses lazySet plus ordering through the atomic decrement.
Seen in Netty: dc2555d8c9 2024-05-20 turned the allocator's `ChunkByteBuf` into a slim `Chunk` with `refCntUp`/`refCntDown`, using `lazySet` and `unguardedRetain()` for the magazine owner's bump allocations.

### netty-123 — Use the simplest lock that fits: non-reentrant tryLock locks, a reader-writer guard, a plain shutdown lock
Lens: concurrency · Source: Netty
Principle: Do not default to reentrant locks. A minimal non-reentrant lock with `tryLock` is cheaper and turns accidental recursion into a visible failure. A reader-writer lock suits a structure that many threads read and one operation needs exclusively.
Why it is faster: Reentrant locks track the owner thread and a hold count, and callers often add `isHeldByCurrentThread()` checks. A non-reentrant lock is a single CAS on acquire and a single store on release.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `std::mutex` with `try_lock` instead of `recursive_mutex`, and try-spinlocks in kernels. • Web / backend: read-mostly registries behind an `RWMutex` in Go. • Data / databases: shared/exclusive latches on pages.
Watch out: a non-reentrant lock deadlocks or fails on recursion, so the code structure must rule recursion out. Read-write locks cost more than mutexes for very short critical sections.
Seen in Netty: 686ef795f9 2010-02-01 added `NonReentrantLock` and replaced `ReentrantLock` or monitor objects with it in `NioSocketChannel.writeLock` and `SslHandler`, removing the `isHeldByCurrentThread()` checks. • 243264efb0 2008-08-31 uses a `ReentrantReadWriteLock` for the selector guard. 8fb2f7c789 2008-09-28 found "a simple shutdown lock" enough for a single acceptor.

### netty-124 — Bypass the portable abstraction with a thin native layer over the OS primitive
Lens: platform · Source: Netty
Principle: When a portable runtime API (the JDK selector, a generic event library) adds bookkeeping, synchronization or semantics you do not need, call the OS primitive (edge-triggered epoll, io_uring) directly through a thin native layer. Keep the portable path as a fallback.
Why it is faster: The JDK NIO selector maintains a `HashSet` of selected keys, synchronizes registration, re-reports level-triggered readiness, and boxes file descriptors. A direct binding removes all of that, can use an `int`-keyed map for fd-to-channel lookups, and exposes Linux-only features (`TCP_CORK`, `SO_REUSEPORT`, splice, multishot I/O) that the portable API cannot. The price is a native build per platform and a second code path to maintain.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: native addons for Node, or uvloop for Python asyncio, replace the default loop with one tuned for the platform. • Native / systems: Rust and Go runtimes with raw epoll or io_uring backends, and custom reactors in C servers. • Data / databases: storage engines that use io_uring or O_DIRECT instead of buffered stdio. • Embedded: talk to the vendor HAL or hardware registers directly instead of a generic POSIX shim on hot I/O paths.
Watch out: native code brings platform builds, crash risk and a second implementation to keep consistent. Netty kept NIO as the fallback. Edge-triggered mode also changes the contract: a channel that does not drain until EAGAIN never gets notified again.
Seen in Netty: 9330172f80 2014-02-15 introduced `transport-native-epoll` (about 3.3k lines of Java and C): edge-triggered mode, `TCP_CORK`, and less GC than NIO. 31211487e9 2014-06-25 maps fds to channels with `IntObjectHashMap`, so there is no `Integer` boxing. • 627c3b7392 2026-01-08 has the io_uring channels submit `SEND` instead of `WRITE`, chosen from flamegraphs of TechEmpower runs. This kind of choice is only possible with direct control of the syscall. • The io_uring transport, with ring setup flags, multishot operations and provided buffers, is described in the two io_uring entries below.

### netty-125 — Cross the native boundary rarely: batch inside one call, cache results, skip calls whose answer is known
Lens: platform · Source: Netty
Principle: Every managed-to-native call costs far more than a normal call. Do several units of work per crossing, fetch values lazily and memoize them, reorder conditions so the native call runs only when needed, and implement trivial logic on the managed side.
Why it is faster: A JNI crossing costs a thread-state transition, argument marshalling and a loss of inlining on both sides, about tens of nanoseconds, compared with near zero for an inlined call. TLS engines call into OpenSSL for state queries (cipher, handshake count, pending bytes) that rarely change. Caching, reordering or computing those values in Java removes whole crossings. At high request rates, each skipped call shows up as a percentage of CPU.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Node N-API and Python C extensions: batch per-element work into one call taking arrays, and minimize the number of WASM-to-JS calls. • Native / systems: batch GPU driver calls (WebGL, Vulkan command buffers) and syscalls in general. • Data / databases: JDBC/ODBC drivers should fetch rows in batches, and UDF frameworks should pass vectors instead of single values.
Watch out: a cached native value goes stale if the native state can change (for example after renegotiation). Cache only values that are immutable after a known point, or invalidate them explicitly.
Seen in Netty: 405cdc89dd 2014-11-26 makes `OpenSslEngine` call `getCipherForSSL`/`getNextProtoNegotiated` only on first access ("Calling JNI methods is pretty expensive"). 9125060752 2014-05-18 returned to feeding OpenSSL multiple records per call, after one-at-a-time feeding cost throughput. • f20439b6d3 2015-01-27 reduced JNI calls in `OpenSslEngine`, removed double calculations, and wraps several buffers in one loop. bdfdf3094d 2015-04-13 returns static `SSLEngineResult`s during the handshake. 66c3c58d3e 2015-09-29 reuses a `ByteBuffer[1]` for single-buffer wrap/unwrap. 1bb818bb59 2014-11-24 lets `unwrap` take a `ByteBuffer[]`, so a scattered input is decrypted in one call without coalescing. • c68a6adce0 2017-02-03 lets `OpenSslEngine` wrap and unwrap several TLS packets per call. • ec82b13f1b 2024-06-11 parses QUIC headers in Java instead of through quiche, avoiding JNI calls and copies. • 889337655d 2025-07-07 / 708fafc379 2026-03-02 reorder checks so `SSL.getHandshakeCount` is never called for TLS 1.3. dfcf1f9f0a 2026-11-13 skips the renegotiation check on BoringSSL/AWS-LC servers (about 2% of CPU), and 5fe1f1fd65 2026-11-14 queries `SSL_pending` lazily (about 1.7% of samples). 13a47d81de 2026-02-19 passes the protocol in instead of calling `SSL.getVersion()` again. d8a78d8b79 2025-03-21 reads the immutable io_uring ring mask and entry count once inside JNI.

### netty-126 — Pass plain numbers across the boundary: addresses, byte arrays, error codes, packed results
Lens: platform · Source: Netty
Principle: Native functions should take memory addresses and lengths (not managed buffer objects), return raw bytes (not formatted strings or managed objects built in C), report errors as numeric codes (format the message only on failure), and pack several results into one primitive.
Why it is faster: Handing objects to native code forces callbacks into the VM: `GetDirectBufferAddress`, field reads, method calls to update the position. Building managed objects in C (an `InetSocketAddress` from a string) means allocating and parsing on the managed side. Fetching an error string to check for success allocates on every call. Passing a `long` address and `int` length, returning a `byte[]` or an `int`, and packing `(count, flag)` into one `long` keeps each crossing to primitives.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: pass `Buffer`/`ArrayBuffer` plus offsets to N-API or WASM, not strings or objects. Return status codes and decode lazily. • Native / systems: check `errno` before calling `strerror`, check `ERR_peek_error` before formatting, and return `(status, length)` as a u64 across FFI. • Data / databases: binary over text protocols between processes (binary row formats, not CSV). • Embedded: RPC between cores or to a co-processor should pass buffer descriptors, not marshalled structs.
Watch out: raw addresses are valid only while the memory is pinned and alive. A GC move or an early free on the managed side corrupts native code. Critical array regions block GC and must stay very short.
Seen in Netty: 6bef9c8489 2014-02-17 added `Native.writeAddress`/`readAddress`. 47fab2bfe8 2014-02-18 makes gathering writes pass addresses ("reduce gc pressure"). 14096d85cd 2014-07-18 removed the JNI callback that incremented the buffer position. • 512d1a11ff 2014-07-21 uses `GetPrimitiveArrayCritical` (usually pinned, no copy) instead of `Get<Type>ArrayElements` in `epollWait` and the sockaddr helpers. • 73c0f85d63 2014-10-22 makes accept return a compact `byte[]` (address, port, scope id) and build the address with `InetAddress.getByAddress`, instead of `new InetSocketAddress(String, int)`, which had been an accept-path bottleneck. cb1bf1a74e 2014-09-07 strips the `::ffff:` prefix natively, so Java's exception-driven IPv4-mapped parsing is avoided. 8910d62544 2019-08-30 builds datagram addresses from `byte[]` rather than strings. • 5c57b5de5b 2014-10-17 uses `SSL.getLastErrorNumber()` and formats the error string only on failure. • ed98ce27e1 2015-02-02 returns a negative errno from `epollWait0` instead of throwing from JNI. 86004b7303 2022-03-18 packs the ready count and a "timer was armed" flag into one returned `long`. 00cd6d2406 2016-12-21 switched tcnative to a `ByteBuffer`-based BIO, removing a copy and the per-connection BIO pair memory.

### netty-127 — Share native memory with the kernel and fill native structs directly from managed code
Lens: platform · Source: Netty
Principle: Let the kernel write results (epoll events) into native memory that managed code reads directly, and build kernel input structs (`struct iovec[]`, `mmsghdr[]`) in preallocated native memory with raw stores. Do not build managed arrays and copy them across in C.
Why it is faster: The classic pattern fills a native array in C and copies it into a managed array (or the reverse), with a translation loop on each crossing. When both sides use the same off-heap memory, the copy, the managed array allocation and the translation all disappear. Growing the array only when it fills up keeps it the right size.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: io_uring SQ/CQ rings and DPDK descriptor rings are this idea taken to its conclusion. • Web / backend: Python C extensions writing into `memoryview`s, and WASM modules sharing linear memory with JS. • Data / databases: Arrow-style shared columnar buffers between engine and UDF runtime.
Watch out: struct layouts (pointer size, padding, byte order) must match the platform ABI exactly. Netty sizes iovec entries by the platform's address size. Shared buffers must not be reused while the kernel still owns them.
Seen in Netty: 88bd6e7a93 2014-07-22 added `IovArray`: a thread-local off-heap block of `IOV_MAX` iovecs filled with `PlatformDependent.putLong`. wrk with 256 connections and pipelining went from 2,887,885 to 3,080,169 req/s (+6.7%). • ed98ce27e1 2015-02-02 added `EpollEventArray`: the kernel writes `struct epoll_event` into native memory passed as an address, with the fd stored in `data.fd`. wrk went from 4,553,438 to 4,634,343 req/s. 6b8ec6b781 2015-01-29 doubles the array when an `epoll_wait` fills it completely. • 9428d51e2f 2014-09-03 builds the `mmsghdr[]` for `sendmmsg` by reusing the iovec array (the syscall-batching side is covered in the I/O category).

### netty-128 — Preallocate native scratch memory per I/O thread, size it to the real limit, allocate it as one block
Lens: platform · Source: Netty
Principle: Native helper memory used on every syscall (iovecs, msghdrs, control-message buffers) should be preallocated once per event loop and passed by address, not malloc'd and freed per call. Size it to the real kernel limit rather than a theoretical product of maxima, create it only when the feature is used, and carve many small structs from one block.
Why it is faster: A per-call `malloc`/`free` in JNI glue costs allocator time and possible lock contention on every send. A per-thread block costs nothing per call. Over-sizing to `IOV_MAX` times `UIO_MAXIOV` wastes megabytes per thread. Thousands of separately managed native buffers mean thousands of frees or arena closes, while one block means one.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: per-thread iovec and cmsg arrays in C servers, and one `mmap` sliced into ring entries. • Web / backend: FFI scratch arenas in Python or Node addons, reused across calls. • Embedded: static descriptor pools sized from measured peaks, not spec maxima.
Watch out: shared per-thread scratch must never be held across a yield point or used re-entrantly. Respect per-call kernel limits (`IOV_MAX`) even when the pool is larger.
Seen in Netty: edb2250d35 2015-11-14 keeps one `IovArray` per `EpollEventLoop`. • 630c82717d 2018-07-29 creates `NativeDatagramPacketArray`/`IovArray` on first use. d96c02fc68 2019-02-02 shares one `IovArray` across all packets, which removed 16 MB of direct memory per `EpollEventLoop`. • 3b9282f2bb 2023-03-13 "Dont malloc all the time": `sendToAddresses` takes a control-message address and length preallocated per Epoll/KQueue event loop. d45c962b9c 2023-11-28 uses stack memory in C for a QUIC value that is never read back. • 279cc34a01 2025-04-15 shares one `IovArray` per `IoUringIoHandler` (fixed in ec8db20e71 2026-10-20 to apply `IOV_MAX` per write). 0306ded58e 2026-02-10 allocates `MsgHdrMemoryArray` as one segment instead of 1,024 x 4 cleanable buffers, turning 4,096 shared-arena closes into 1.

### netty-129 — Free native memory deterministically, and grow it with realloc
Lens: platform · Source: Netty
Principle: When ownership is explicit (reference counted), allocate native memory with malloc, release it with free when the count reaches zero, and resize with realloc. Do not leave native memory to GC-driven finalizers or cleaners.
Why it is faster: GC-tracked native memory is released only when the collector notices the small wrapper object, so native usage can grow without bound between collections. Release also goes through a reference queue, and on older JDKs a `static synchronized` `Cleaner.clean()`. Explicit free returns memory immediately. `realloc` can extend or shrink in place without copying, where allocate-copy-free always copies.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Node N-API buffers with deterministic release, and Python extensions that free in `close()` rather than relying on `__del__`. • Native / systems: `Vec` and `std::vector` growth through realloc where element types allow it. • Data / databases: query-scoped memory contexts freed at query end (Postgres MemoryContexts).
Watch out: explicit free brings back use-after-free and leaks. Netty pairs it with reference counting and a sampling leak detector. You also need your own accounting to enforce memory limits.
Seen in Netty: a6e89aa142 2012-10-04 "Manually GC direct ByteBuffer": the cached receive buffer is destroyed explicitly at shutdown. • 3d29bcfc8d 2016-05-23 added `UnpooledUnsafeNoCleanerDirectByteBuf` with its own direct-memory accounting (`OutOfDirectMemoryError`). 3a7dcde320 2016-05-23 expands with `reallocateMemory`, and 20059f130d 2017-07-05 shrinks with it too. • 7d774584c8 2016-07-14 added `ReferenceCountedOpenSslEngine/Context`, opt-in TLS objects with no finalizer. • dc4d8b4dc6 2025-06-18 and f265f09b2a 2025-07-10 introduced malloc/free-based cleaners for direct buffers on newer runtimes.

### netty-130 — Attach finalizers and cleaners only when needed, and know what native release costs on your runtime
Lens: platform · Source: Netty
Principle: Finalizers and phantom-reference cleaners add GC work and often a shared lock. Attach them only to objects that actually own native resources, or only when leak detection is on. When native release itself is expensive (closing a shared FFM arena), let short-lived memory go back through the GC instead.
Why it is faster: A finalizable object takes more GC cycles to reclaim and goes through a single finalizer thread. Cleaner registration goes through a synchronized list per Cleaner, which is a global contention point on allocation-heavy paths. On JDK 25 FFM, closing a shared `Arena` triggers a handshake with every thread, so explicit per-buffer close for many short-lived buffers costs more than leaving them to the GC. The right release mechanism therefore depends on the runtime version and on how long the memory lives.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: in JS, register `FinalizationRegistry` entries only for objects owning native memory. In .NET, call `GC.SuppressFinalize` after explicit dispose. • Native / systems: per-core deferred-free lists, and RCU-style batched reclamation instead of synchronous per-object frees. • Data / databases: embedded engines with handle wrappers (SQLite in managed languages) should close explicitly and keep finalizers only as a safety net.
Watch out: without any cleaner, a forgotten release leaks native memory permanently. Keep leak detection available and switchable. Re-evaluate the policy when the runtime changes, as Netty did for FFM.
Seen in Netty: 6eb9674bf5 2017-12-21 moved the Recycler's finalizer to `ObjectCleaner`, and e816018569 2019-10-31 removed it entirely. • 67a46b102f 2022-11-16 gave each event loop its own `Cleaner`: 2,995 to 50,096 ops/ms on 10 CPUs. dc46a3918a 2022-12-08 stopped attaching a cleaner by default (`alwaysAttachCleaner=false`), which roughly doubled allocator throughput on an M1. • 9412424091 2023-06-06 skipped finalization for thread caches that hold no small or normal caches. dc16c5818a 2023-07-24 replaced that with a small `FreeOnFinalize` side object that only pooling caches reference. • c79ce23a7b 2026-03-14 keeps unpooled buffers out of shared FFM arenas ("greatly improves system-wide performance and scalability"), with `io.netty.ignoreExpensiveClean` as the switch. Explicit close is kept for pooled chunks.

### netty-131 — Tell the kernel your threading model: io_uring single-issuer, deferred task-run and registered rings
Lens: platform · Source: Netty
Principle: When one thread owns a ring, declare it (`SINGLE_ISSUER`, `DEFER_TASKRUN`) so the kernel skips cross-CPU signalling and locking. Register the ring fd to avoid a lookup per enter, drop optional indirections, and size the queues to the real submission-to-completion ratio.
Why it is faster: By default the kernel must assume any thread can submit, and it runs completion task work immediately, often by sending inter-processor interrupts (IPIs). With the single-issuer and deferred flags, completions are processed in batches when the owner thread enters the kernel, on that thread's CPU. Registered ring fds skip `fdget` on every `io_uring_enter`. Multishot operations produce many completions per submission, so a large CQ and a small SQ fit that ratio, and large SQ batches only add latency.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: any io_uring server (liburing `io_uring_queue_init_params`), and tokio-uring or glommio in Rust. • Data / databases: storage engines with per-core rings (SPDK style), which fit the single-issuer model exactly. • Web / backend: thread-per-core proxies that pin one ring per worker.
Watch out: these flags need recent kernels and impose real constraints. Submitting from a second thread on a `SINGLE_ISSUER` ring fails. With `DEFER_TASKRUN`, completions wait until the thread enters the kernel. Probe for the features and fall back.
Seen in Netty: f17fb05475 2025-01-21 enabled `SINGLE_ISSUER` and `DEFER_TASKRUN`: with 1,000 connections, aggregate bandwidth went from 13.26 to 22.51 Gbps and the packet rate from 1.14M to 2.08M per second. • b48603c0c2 2025-01-23 registers the ring fds. a16967c372 2025-01-20 sets `SUBMIT_ALL`, so all entries are submitted even when one fails. 7515c263b9 2025-07-07 uses `NO_SQARRAY` to drop the SQ index-array indirection. • f52405d2b4 2025-07-12 sizes the rings at SQ 128 / CQ 4096, because completions far outnumber submissions with multishot.

### netty-132 — Let the kernel pick buffers: multishot requests with provided buffer rings
Lens: platform · Source: Netty
Principle: Arm one multishot request that keeps producing completions (accept, recv, poll) instead of re-arming per event. Give the kernel a ring of buffers to choose from at completion time, so idle connections hold no receive buffer. Fill the ring lazily, grow it on demand, refill it eagerly, and do the refill on the I/O thread.
Why it is faster: Re-arming costs one submission per event. Multishot costs one per many events. A pre-posted read buffer per connection costs memory multiplied by the connection count, mostly idle. With provided buffers, memory is committed only when data arrives. Refilling the ring eagerly prevents stalls when the kernel runs out of buffers. Filling from the I/O thread keeps buffer release on the same thread, which is cheap.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: high-connection-count proxies and game servers with mostly idle sockets. AF_XDP and DPDK buffer pools use the same idea. • Web / backend: WebSocket fan-out services where most connections are idle at any moment. • Embedded: NIC drivers with shared RX descriptor pools instead of per-socket buffers.
Watch out: an empty buffer ring stalls receives (ENOBUFS), so monitor refill rates. Kernel-chosen buffers arrive out of order relative to requests, so track buffer ids carefully.
Seen in Netty: 70e9549697 2025-02-13 added `ACCEPT_MULTISHOT`, 626707b524 2025-02-15 `RECV_MULTISHOT`, and 028d055fda 2025-02-22 `POLL_ADD_MULTI`. • f5bc749b19 2025-01-29 added provided buffers. df496ccf93 2025-02-10 fills the ring lazily, a10d88f7e3 2025-03-11 grows it on the fly, and a4acd66fee 2025-02-19 refills it eagerly: roughly 37 to 70 Gbps on tcpkali. • eeeda13e48 2025-02-22 added an adaptive buffer-ring allocator, and 9c3a01dd21 2025-02-22 fills the ring from the I/O thread.

### netty-133 — Probe platform capabilities once, cheaply, and gate them by version
Lens: platform · Source: Netty
Principle: Decide platform features (native transports, unaligned access, Unsafe, io_uring opcodes, memory limits) once at startup with the cheapest reliable source: a system property, a runtime-internal field or a version check. Cache the result. Avoid regex parsing, reflection chains and expensive probes on init paths.
Why it is faster: Startup code runs interpreted, before the JIT has warmed up, so regex compilation, `String.format`, reflective probes and management beans cost milliseconds. Reading a runtime field directly, or deciding from cheap metadata first, avoids them. Caching a probe result (such as supported io_uring opcodes) prevents repeating syscalls. Version gates skip probes that cannot succeed. Linking directly against stubs removes reflection entirely.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: read CPUID or `HWCAP` once into a feature struct, and dispatch SIMD paths through function pointers set at init. • Web / backend: serverless cold starts, where you read cgroup limits from files once and avoid heavy imports and reflection-based DI at boot. • Embedded: detect the board revision once at boot and store the result, rather than probing peripherals repeatedly.
Watch out: reading runtime internals is brittle across versions, so always keep a slow but correct fallback. A wrong cached capability (claiming unaligned access on a strict CPU) causes crashes, not slowness.
Seen in Netty: 93c1dd58ba 2008-08-30 infers the NIO constraint level from OS, vendor and `SelectorProvider` before running a socket probe: "should start up faster with Sun JVM". • 5db037beed 2023-10-02 reads `java.nio.Bits` max direct memory through Unsafe as a fast path, instead of allocating MXBeans and parsing JVM arguments. • 86b970dfd3 2024-06-13 runs Unsafe reflective probes only on Java 24 and later. 1658c6e687 2025-05-24 normalizes platform names without regex. ec465e7513 2025-07-14 caches the io_uring probe result. • b6fad79c6a 2026-10-27 calls FFM directly through a compile-time `netty-ffm-stub` and links malloc/free on first use: startup went from 83.7 to 69.3 ms with CDS and from 37.2 to 24.3 ms with AOT.

### netty-134 — Treat native glue as hot code: optimize the build and the calling conventions
Lens: platform · Source: Netty
Principle: Compile JNI and FFI glue with full optimization, mark small helpers `static inline`, pass structs by const pointer rather than by value, and keep frame pointers so profilers can walk native stacks.
Why it is faster: Glue code runs on every syscall, so a default `-O0`/`-O2` build or by-value struct copies add cost to every call. Inlined helpers remove call overhead inside the native side. Frame pointers cost about one register but make native frames visible in flamegraphs, which is how the other native optimizations here were found.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Node native addons and Python wheels often ship with default flags, so set the optimization level explicitly. • Native / systems: Rust FFI shims (`#[inline]` on tiny wrappers), and frame pointers on in production for continuous profiling. • Embedded: HAL wrappers compiled `-O2`/`-Os` with LTO so register-access helpers inline.
Watch out: `-O3` can increase code size and occasionally expose undefined behaviour in glue code. Test the optimized build. Frame pointers cost a few percent in register-starved code on 32-bit x86.
Seen in Netty: deda3f5b65 2015-06-20 builds `transport-native-epoll` with `-O3`. • 7a3d91f43d 2015-02-05 makes the throw, epollCtl and getOption helpers `static inline`. 6c24dd4d8f 2015-02-25 passes `const struct ... *` parameters. • 8d664fa0fd 2016-12-06 adds `-fno-omit-frame-pointer` for flamegraphs.

### netty-135 — Coalesce writes and flush once per batch
Lens: io · Source: Netty
Principle: Separate "queue this output" from "push queued output to the OS", and push only at natural batch boundaries such as the end of a read burst, so many logical writes share one syscall.
Why it is faster: Each flush can become a `write`/`writev` syscall, a TLS record and, with Nagle disabled, a separate packet. Holding writes until a read burst ends or a higher layer flushes turns N syscalls, N records and N packets into one of each. A lock-free gate (a CAS flag) keeps the batching itself cheap. The cost is a little latency for the first message in the batch, so bound the batch with a forced flush after a count.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Node `socket.cork()`/`uncork()` around a burst of writes; respond to pipelined Redis or HTTP requests with one write per event-loop tick. • Data / databases: group commit in a WAL: many transactions share one write and one fsync. • Native / systems: `TCP_CORK` or `MSG_MORE` while assembling a response; flush the buffer in the reactor's "before poll" hook. • Embedded: accumulate UART or radio frames in a TX buffer and kick DMA once per scheduler tick.
Watch out: deferring a flush that nothing will trigger later stalls the connection. Every deferred path needs a guaranteed flush point (read-complete, a count limit, or a scheduled task), and interactive single-message traffic gains nothing.
Seen in Netty: c35231ae95 2009-12-16 NETTY-209 `BufferedWriteHandler` queues writes and flushes them together, optionally consolidating them into one buffer; 88124d88ce replaced its `synchronized` blocks with an `AtomicBoolean` flush gate. • 5ef30b6d8b 2013-08-07 `writeAndFlush` runs write and flush inline on the event loop, or crosses threads as one fused task instead of two. • e3c8a92499 2016-07-05 `FlushConsolidationHandler`: flushes during a read loop are deferred to read-complete, with a forced flush after 256; 3a8b8c9219 also consolidates outside reads by bouncing the flush through the executor ("lower CPU usage and less context switching"). • 2407b5b8c2 2017-03-10 `ChunkedWriteHandler` flushes per chunk only for chunked input; ee9057ad99 `CorsHandler` uses `write` instead of `writeAndFlush`; d56f403c69 runs `channelReadComplete` before `flush` so more data joins the batch; 0a8e1aaf19 makes the flush task call `flush0()` directly instead of walking the pipeline. • 179bc4277a 2020-11-07 / e3300675c7 the QUIC codec writes during the read burst and flushes once in `channelReadComplete`, which lets the datagram transport batch with `sendmmsg`.

### netty-136 — Send scattered segments with one gathering write
Lens: io · Source: Netty
Principle: When output lives in several buffers (header plus body, a list of queued messages, segments of a rope), hand all of them to the OS in one vectored call instead of one call each or one merge copy first.
Why it is faster: `writev`/`sendmsg` take an array of (pointer, length) pairs, so N segments cost one syscall and zero user-space copies. The alternatives are N syscalls or an O(total bytes) memcpy into a staging buffer. This lets encoders emit a small freshly encoded header and the untouched payload as separate buffers. Limits: at most `IOV_MAX` entries per call, and building the iovec array has its own cost, so cap the bytes per call near the socket send-buffer size.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Node streams implementing `_writev`; Go `net.Buffers` for header plus body. • Native / systems: `writev` of a static status line, generated headers and a cached body in a C HTTP server; batched `IORING_OP_WRITEV`. • Embedded: scatter-gather DMA descriptor chains instead of copying into one TX buffer. • Data / databases: write a log record header and payload from separate buffers in one call.
Watch out: for tiny segments the iovec bookkeeping costs more than a memcpy (see the next technique), and heap or managed memory may need to be copied to native memory anyway, which removes the zero-copy benefit.
Seen in Netty: c5ab2f5dad 2012-04-19 (#267) composite buffers write through `GatheringByteChannel.write(ByteBuffer[])` on JDK 7+; 6e2e9fb3c5 adds `GatheringSendBuffer` to the NIO send path, 510692d7e7 benchmarks direct, heap and mixed cases, 670c4fa42f enables it by default. • 46ea0d4e7b 2013-07-18 `NioSocketChannel` gathers every flushed message via `ChannelOutboundBuffer.nioBuffers()`; a403da3042 and 904385117f change the HTTP and WebSocket encoders to emit header and content as separate buffers so they can be gathered. • 88bd6e7a93 2014-07-22 `IovArray` fills a native `struct iovec[]` directly for epoll gathering writes: wrk with 256 pipelined connections went from 2,887,885 to 3,080,169 req/s (+6.7%). 695fbc9140 skips the per-buffer index adjustment loop when everything was written. • 1504d2044d 2014-08-19 composite buffers are added to the iovec array component by component instead of being flattened (3cf65e3987 for datagrams); 23baef8fb4 takes a single-component fast path. • b215794de3 2017-12-07 caps the bytes per gathering write and auto-scales that cap from `SO_SNDBUF` and observed success, so it does not build iovecs the kernel cannot accept.

### netty-137 — Do as much I/O as possible per readiness event
Lens: io · Source: Netty
Principle: When the OS says a socket is ready, keep reading (or accepting) until it is empty or a fairness budget runs out, and stop as soon as a short read proves it is drained.
Why it is faster: A readiness event costs a poll syscall and a trip through the loop. Taking one message per event pays that cost per message. Reading in a loop amortises it across the burst, and stopping on a short read (fewer bytes than the buffer had room for) avoids the extra syscall that would only return EAGAIN. A per-event message cap keeps one busy socket from starving others.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: loop `recv`/`accept4` until EAGAIN in epoll servers, with a per-fd budget per iteration. • Web / backend: drain a message queue consumer in batches per poll instead of one message per callback. • Embedded: in a NIC or UART ISR, empty the whole RX FIFO or descriptor ring per interrupt (NAPI-style budget).
Watch out: with level-triggered polling, a short-read exit is safe; with edge-triggered polling you must read until EAGAIN or you will never be notified again. An unbounded loop lets one connection monopolise the thread.
Seen in Netty: b889d3f559 2013-12-21 breaks the read loop when `localReadAmount < writable`; bab227213e defaults `maxMessagesPerRead` to 16 for byte channels. • 7800070656 2023-11-01 datagram channels read up to 16 messages per event instead of 1: a QUIC 1 GB transfer dropped from 14 s to 6 s. 163b2b659c keeps reading even when a datagram is smaller than the buffer, since that does not mean the socket is empty. • 1fb7c508c4 2021-10-06 `ServerChannelRecvByteBufAllocator` lets a server accept more than one connection per read loop. • d25d6667b9 2025-01-14 io_uring channels keep reading until empty, which means fewer `channelReadComplete` calls and preserves downstream batching.

### netty-138 — Mirror kernel state in user space and skip syscalls that change nothing
Lens: io · Source: Netty
Principle: Keep a cheap user-space copy of state the kernel also tracks (interest mask, connection state, pending submissions) and make the syscall only when the desired state actually differs.
Why it is faster: Calls like `epoll_ctl`, `interestOps()`, `isConnected()` or `io_uring_enter` cost a kernel entry or an internal lock even when they are no-ops. A field compare costs nanoseconds. The user-space copy is safe because one thread owns the fd and is the only one changing it. Reading volatile kernel-mirrored state once per event (for example the ready set) also avoids repeated synchronised accessor calls.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: cache the registered epoll mask per fd; cache the last `setsockopt`/`fcntl` value and skip identical calls. • Web / backend: skip DOM writes or style changes when the value is unchanged; skip `setTimeout` re-registration when the deadline is the same. • Embedded: keep a shadow copy of write-only peripheral registers and write only on change; read a status register once per ISR. • Data / databases: track dirty flags so unchanged pages are not rewritten.
Watch out: the shadow copy must be updated on every path that changes the real state, including error and close paths, or the program stops getting events. Deferring the real call (rather than skipping a no-op) introduces ordering bugs.
Seen in Netty: 80b6a5b7ae 2009-09-21 "Reduced unnecessary system calls triggered by `Channel.isConnected()`": drop the pre-check and handle `ClosedChannelException` instead; 60d3502ba1 replaces lock-taking `isConnected()`/`isBound()` with a volatile `ST_OPEN/BOUND/CONNECTED/CLOSED` state field. • 77742f5cf9 2009-05-21 NETTY-156 reads `k.readyOps()` once into a local and tests bits, instead of calling `isReadable()`/`isWritable()`. • a13246a2b6 2013-02-07 (#1024) limits `interestOps(..)` calls; a7b5d45bdf tests `(interestOps & OP_WRITE) == 0` before setting; 252bd25855 reads `interestOps()` only on the branch that needs it. • 250b279bd9 2019-08-18 tried collapsing several interest changes per loop into one `epoll_ctl` with a dirty bitset; it was reverted (46cff5399f) for correctness problems, which shows the risk of deferring kernel state changes. • 0f813918e3 2025-04-15 skips `epoll_ctl` when the EPOLLOUT/EPOLLIN flag is already in the desired state; 60bd05f3be adds an fd to epoll only once it is active, avoiding an EPOLLHUP spin. • 7d279b604c 2025-08-30 io_uring submits only if `sqCount > 0`, cutting extra submissions from 60% to 20%; bedd0ac63d uses `IORING_SETUP_TASKRUN_FLAG` to tell whether task work is pending: idle `io_uring_enter` calls fell from 486 to 65 (-86.6%).

### netty-139 — Write until the kernel buffer is full, then wait for writability
Lens: io · Source: Netty
Principle: Write directly while the socket accepts data; on a zero-byte or short write, stop, register write interest once, and let the readiness event resume, with a bounded number of attempts per turn.
Why it is faster: Retrying against a full send buffer burns syscalls that return 0. Registering write interest when the buffer is not full wastes an `epoll_ctl` plus a wakeup that has nothing to do. A write spin count bounds the work for one socket so others get a turn, and when the budget runs out with data left, a scheduled flush (rather than write interest) gives other channels a chance without a kernel round trip.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: handle EAGAIN in epoll servers by enabling EPOLLOUT only then and disabling it when the queue drains. • Web / backend: respect Node's `write()` return value and wait for `'drain'` instead of retrying. • Embedded: when a TX ring is full, enable the TX-empty interrupt instead of polling it.
Watch out: forgetting to clear write interest after the queue drains causes a busy loop of writability events; a spin count of 1 adds latency for large transfers.
Seen in Netty: 0857f398e0 2010-02-09 "reducing the lame write attempts on the channel whose send buffer is full already": a `writeSuspended` flag, with user-thread writes only enqueuing until the selector reports writability; dd7f588916 for UDP; 243264efb0 hoists `getWriteSpinCount()` out of the loop. • 9fd8bf329d 2013-09-13 sets `OP_WRITE` only when a write returned 0; otherwise it schedules a flush task so other channels run. • aa66f556e5 2014-07-18 epoll calls `setEpollOut()` only on a 0-byte write ("less overhead when saturate network"); 14096d85cd writes until EAGAIN; 89261fd8df honours `writeSpinCount` in native. • b215794de3 2017-12-07 makes `writeSpinCount` count syscalls rather than messages, so the fairness budget is meaningful. • 817052d019 2021-03-17 adds `MAX_MESSAGES_PER_WRITE` for datagram channels so latency-critical reads (QUIC ACKs) are not starved; 54aa4d9b68 runs one delegated TLS task per delegation for the same reason.

### netty-140 — Bound queued output with water marks and stop reading when behind
Lens: io · Source: Netty
Principle: Track bytes queued but not yet written; above a high water mark tell producers to pause (and stop reading input that would create more output), and resume below a lower mark.
Why it is faster: An unbounded outbound queue turns a slow peer into memory growth, GC thrash and eventually an out-of-memory crash, which hurts every other connection. Two thresholds (hysteresis) stop the flag from flapping on every write. Not reading from a socket pushes the backpressure into TCP's receive window, so the remote sender slows down for free.
