Servicing writes before reads on a ready socket drains and frees queued output before new input produces more.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Node stream `highWaterMark` and `'drain'`; pause the upstream request stream in a proxy while the client is slow. • Data / databases: admission control that stops accepting queries when result buffers back up; replication senders that stop reading the WAL when the replica lags. • Embedded: assert flow control (RTS/CTS or XOFF) when the RX buffer passes a threshold.
Watch out: water marks only help if producers check the writability signal; thresholds that are too low cut throughput on high-bandwidth, high-latency links.
Seen in Netty: aef2ab453a 2008-08-08 (initial import) `MemoryAwareThreadPoolExecutor` calls `channel.setReadable(false)` when a channel's pending event memory exceeds its limit, sized by an `ObjectSizeEstimator`. • 893cab5ce8 2008-11-28 high/low water marks for write overflow: 256 KiB high, 64 KiB low, with an atomic queued-bytes counter driving the writability flag. • 3cf7ccbd3c 2016-09-29 `NioEventLoop.processSelectedKey` handles `OP_WRITE` (`forceFlush()`) before reading.

### netty-141 — Copy small payloads, pass large ones by reference
Lens: io · Source: Netty
Principle: Zero-copy paths (gathering writes, zero-copy send) have fixed per-segment costs; below a size threshold, copy into one contiguous buffer and send that, and use the zero-copy path only for large payloads.
Why it is faster: An extra iovec entry, an extra buffer object, or a zero-copy send's page pinning and completion notification each cost more than a memcpy of a few hundred bytes. Copying small pieces together also lets one syscall or one zero-copy operation carry a mixed flush. Pick the threshold by measurement; around 1 KiB for gathering and tens of KiB for zero-copy send are typical.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: use `MSG_ZEROCOPY` only above a size cutoff; inline small values in a send queue instead of referencing them. • Web / backend: serialise small HTTP responses into one buffer, and stream only large bodies by reference. • Data / databases: inline small values in a log record and store large ones out of line.
Watch out: the threshold depends on hardware and kernel version; a fixed constant should be configurable or re-measured.
Seen in Netty: 43681b5608 2014-10-03 `WebSocket08FrameEncoder` uses `GATHERING_WRITE_TRESHOLD = 1024`: below it, header and payload are copied into one buffer, about 25% faster for 100-byte messages. • 7893d5f150 2024-01-24 `HttpObjectEncoder` sizes the header buffer to include small content (128 bytes or 1/8 of the header estimate), so a small full message leaves as one buffer and one pipeline traversal. • 1f94221f93 2026-11-11 io_uring `ZeroCopyBatch` copies runs of small buffers into one merged buffer (64 KiB default cap) so a mixed flush goes out as a single `SENDMSG_ZC` instead of several operations.

### netty-142 — Let the kernel move file and socket data directly
Lens: io · Source: Netty
Principle: When bytes only pass through (file to socket, socket to socket), use `sendfile`/`transferTo` or `splice` so they never enter user space.
Why it is faster: A read/write loop copies every byte twice (kernel to user, user to kernel) and pays two syscalls per chunk. `sendfile` and `splice` move page references inside the kernel. Netty also measured that mmap of the file did not beat `transferTo`. Positional reads (`pread`) avoid updating a shared file position when you do need the bytes.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: serve static files with `sendfile` (nginx `sendfile on`, Node `fs.createReadStream` piped through a framework that uses it). • Native / systems: `splice` in TCP proxies; `copy_file_range` for file copies. • Data / databases: ship log segments to consumers with sendfile (Kafka-style); read pages with `pread` so threads share one fd.
Watch out: kernel zero-copy cannot be combined with user-space TLS or compression, and very large single transfers should be chunked so one connection does not hog the loop.
Seen in Netty: 59052be709 2010-02-19 NETTY-293 `FileRegion` adds sendfile support to the NIO TCP transport; aa4b8a2a6c keeps it "since mapped buffer does not outperform transferTo()". • 1f696fa2f1 2012-06-15 uses block-wise `FileChannel.transferTo` for disk-to-disk copies, after 7c34781672 briefly replaced it with a manual loop. • 12c9ce7f67 2014-10-28 `DefaultFileRegion` opens the file descriptor only when the transfer starts, so many queued file sends do not hold many fds. • ad9bec4d9d 2015-04-14 epoll `spliceTo(...)` moves socket data to another fd via a pipe inside the kernel; 2c4a7a2539 lets the epoll transport send any `FileRegion` implementation. • e5eb94668c 2019-09-24 `ChunkedNioFile` uses positional `FileChannel.read(buf, pos)` (pread) with no shared position update.

### netty-143 — Move many datagrams per syscall
Lens: io · Source: Netty
Principle: Use multi-message syscalls (`sendmmsg`/`recvmmsg`) and segmentation offload (GSO/GRO) so one kernel crossing carries many UDP packets, and shape callers so batches actually form.
Why it is faster: UDP pays the full per-syscall cost for every packet, often 1,200-1,500 bytes. Batching N packets divides that cost by N; GSO goes further by letting the kernel or NIC split one large buffer into MTU-sized packets. The batch only forms if the application writes several packets before flushing, so redundant flushes defeat it.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: QUIC stacks, DNS servers and game servers with `sendmmsg` plus GSO. • Web / backend: telemetry agents (StatsD, syslog over UDP) that buffer metrics and send them in batches. • Embedded: batch sensor readings into one radio frame rather than one frame per reading.
Watch out: GSO and GRO require kernel and NIC support with fallbacks; batching adds a small delay to the first packet in the batch.
Seen in Netty: 9428d51e2f 2014-09-03 JNI `sendmmsg` (weak symbol, glibc 2.14+) used automatically by `EpollDatagramChannel` when more than one packet is flushed. • b3e6e41384 2019-09-03 `recvmmsg` scatters packets into slices of one large buffer; 82c330e8a5 uses `sendmmsg` on connected UDP channels too. • 99aa51b74a 2018-11-21 `DnsNameResolver` cuts `flush()` calls when querying A and AAAA together, so both go out in one `sendmmsg`. • c22c6b845d 2021-02-27 `SegmentedDatagramPacket` adds UDP_SEGMENT (GSO), used by QUIC in f31908eac3; 02c460be14 adds UDP_GRO on receive.

### netty-144 — Use the most specific syscall for what you already know
Lens: io · Source: Netty
Principle: Pick the syscall variant that does the least work given facts you already have: `read` instead of `recvfrom` on a connected socket, `send`/`recv` instead of generic `write`/`read` on sockets.
Why it is faster: `recvfrom` builds a source address for every packet even when the peer is fixed, and the runtime then wraps it in an object. Generic `read`/`write` go through VFS file dispatch before reaching the socket layer, while `send`/`recv` enter the socket code directly. Small per-call savings add up at millions of calls per second.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `connect()` UDP client sockets and use `send`/`recv`; `send(MSG_NOSIGNAL)` instead of `write` plus a SIGPIPE handler. • Data / databases: `pread`/`pwrite` for page I/O instead of `lseek` plus `read`. • Web / backend: in runtimes that expose both, prefer the socket-specific API.
Watch out: the gains are small per call and kernel-version dependent; measure before rewriting working code.
Seen in Netty: 99face616a 2019-02-01 epoll and kqueue datagram channels use `read` instead of `recvfrom` in connected mode, so no address object is created per packet. • 4292669586 2022-08-22 epoll channels switch to new `Socket.send/recv` natives. • 627c3b7392 2026-01-08 io_uring channels submit `SEND` instead of `WRITE`, chosen from flamegraphs of TechEmpower runs.

### netty-145 — Program the kernel timer only when the earliest deadline moves
Lens: io · Source: Netty
Principle: Remember which deadline the kernel timer is armed for and re-arm it only when a new task is due sooner; prefer a wait call that takes a precise timeout so no separate timer syscall is needed.
Why it is faster: Calling `timerfd_settime` before every `epoll_wait`, or for every scheduled task, adds a syscall per loop iteration or per schedule. Most new timers fire later than the earliest one already armed, so the call usually changes nothing. On Linux 5.11+, `epoll_pwait2` takes a nanosecond timeout directly; for far-off deadlines a millisecond `epoll_wait` timeout is accurate enough.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: a JS or libuv timer heap that resets the OS timer only when the heap minimum changes. • Embedded: tickless RTOS idle that reprograms the compare register only when the earliest software timer changes. • Data / databases: a background scheduler that sleeps until the next job and is woken only by an earlier job.
Watch out: coarse millisecond timeouts trade a little timer accuracy for fewer syscalls; races between "armed deadline" bookkeeping and cross-thread submissions need care.
Seen in Netty: 1dff107de1 2018-09-11 `EpollEventLoop` compares the next deadline with the one already armed and skips `timerfd_settime` if unchanged: about 2 us off gRPC p50 round trip (64.2 to about 62 us). • 1fa7a5e697 2019-08-14 arms the timerfd at submit time, and only when the new task expires before every existing one; 166caf96ef merges the wakeup flag and the armed deadline into one atomic and removes the 1 s default timeout that forced a re-arm on every sleep. • 86004b7303 2022-03-18 uses `epoll_pwait2` on Linux 5.11+, skips `timerfd_settime` for deadlines beyond a 10 ms threshold, and returns a packed result saying whether the timer was armed. • 942292d3c4 2021-02-17 the QUIC codec schedules a new timeout only if it is earlier than the pending one.

### netty-146 — Predict the next read size from recent reads
Lens: io · Source: Netty
Principle: Size each receive buffer from a short history of how much the previous reads returned: step up fast when reads fill the buffer, step down slowly when they do not, and use coarse size classes.
Why it is faster: A buffer that is too small costs extra syscalls and reassembly; one that is too large wastes memory per connection, which multiplies by the connection count. Fast growth and slow shrink (asymmetric steps) handle bursts without oscillating. Classes finer than the allocator can deliver add table lookups for no gain. A sensible starting size (at least one MTU) avoids a resize on the first read of every connection.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: user-space proxies sizing read buffers per connection the way TCP autotuning sizes windows. • Data / databases: adaptive fetch or batch sizes in a consumer, grown on full batches and shrunk slowly. • Web / backend: streaming decompression or upload handlers choosing chunk size from recent chunks.
Watch out: a predictor tuned for one traffic shape (large streaming reads) wastes memory on many idle connections; idle sockets are better served by kernel-selected buffers (see io_uring provided buffers).
Seen in Netty: 44c409ca24 2008-09-30 receive buffer predictor with a `SIZE_TABLE`, `INDEX_INCREMENT = 4` and `INDEX_DECREMENT = 1`; 368c34ace9 names it `AdaptiveReceiveBufferSizePredictor` and adds a fixed variant for datagrams. • 43a603cfde 2010-02-23 reads into a pooled direct buffer of the predicted size and copies only the bytes actually read. • a2f232720b 2013-06-20 coarsens the size table to 16-byte steps below 512 bytes, then doubling, aligned with the allocator's classes; 59d92ad6cf routes NIO, epoll and OIO reads through the same handle. • ec368fea47 2017-12-02 checks for step-up after every full read instead of only at read-complete, so a 16-read burst no longer runs under-allocated. • 1663eb5965 2020-09-22 raises the initial guess from 1024 to 2048 bytes (at least a 1500-byte MTU, ported from SwiftNIO) to avoid copies on new connections, especially with TLS; 11a4b52978 sizes QUIC datagram buffers from the peer-advertised size.

### netty-147 — Use edge-triggered notification and drain to EAGAIN
Lens: io · Source: Netty
Principle: Ask the kernel to notify on state transitions rather than while a condition holds, then consume until the operation reports it would block.
Why it is faster: Level-triggered polling re-reports a ready fd on every wait until it is drained or deregistered, and a level-triggered eventfd or timerfd must be read after each wakeup to clear it: two extra syscalls per tick. Edge-triggered mode fires once per transition, so wakeup fds need draining only on overflow and busy fds are not re-reported. The price is the obligation to drain fully.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: any epoll or kqueue (`EV_CLEAR`) server; self-pipe or eventfd wakeups registered ET. • Embedded: edge-triggered GPIO or peripheral interrupts that drain a FIFO fully in the handler.
Watch out: stopping early under ET (for a fairness budget) loses the notification; you must remember the fd is still readable and revisit it yourself.
Seen in Netty: 9330172f80 2014-02-15 the native epoll transport uses edge-triggered mode (ET) instead of the JDK's level-triggered selector. • 14096d85cd 2014-07-18 writes until EAGAIN, as ET requires. • 63f7854c1e 2019-05-31 registers the wakeup eventfd and timerfd with `EPOLLET`; if `eventfd_write` returns EAGAIN it reads once and retries.

### netty-148 — Skip attempts that will only return EAGAIN
Lens: io · Source: Netty
Principle: When you already know a socket is empty, arm readiness notification directly instead of trying the operation first; when the kernel reports more data is pending, keep going without waiting.
Why it is faster: The speculative "try recv, get EAGAIN, then poll" sequence costs a wasted operation each time a drained socket is re-armed. Completion APIs can carry hints (socket non-empty, accept would block) that let the loop choose correctly without a probe.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: track "drained" per fd in an epoll loop and skip the speculative read on the next wakeup cycle. • Data / databases: clients that know a response is not yet due can poll instead of issuing a blocking read attempt.
Watch out: a wrong "empty" assumption adds a full poll round trip of latency; use hints only where the API guarantees them.
Seen in Netty: ea25b511f3 2024-12-11 io_uring `IORING_RECVSEND_POLL_FIRST` when the last read drained the socket; 1b19e4f7d8 `ACCEPT_POLL_FIRST` for accepts. • b0323b83bb 2024-12-23 uses `CQE_F_SOCK_NONEMPTY` and `ACCEPT_DONTWAIT` to know whether more data or connections are waiting.

### netty-149 — Keep the accept path lean and spread it across threads
Lens: io · Source: Netty
Principle: Accept connections in a non-blocking loop, defer per-socket setup to the thread that will own the socket, and let the kernel balance new connections across several listening sockets.
Why it is faster: Connection rate is limited by how long the acceptor spends per connection. Temporary selectors, blocking accept with timeouts, and per-socket syscalls on the acceptor thread all add to that. Moving setup to the worker parallelises it, and `SO_REUSEPORT` removes the single shared accept queue that all acceptor threads would contend on.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `accept4(SOCK_NONBLOCK | SOCK_CLOEXEC)` to save the follow-up `fcntl` calls; one `SO_REUSEPORT` listener per worker. • Web / backend: nginx `reuseport`; Node cluster workers each binding the port; doing TLS setup on the worker, not the acceptor.
Watch out: `SO_REUSEPORT` hashes connections to sockets, so a worker that stops accepting still receives its share; it also changes behaviour on restart and across platforms.
Seen in Netty: dccc9f8665 2008-09-28 improved server-side accept throughput with a register task queue. • 31ec36f3bb 2009-07-08 "Optimized accept throughput by removing the usage of a temporary selector": the boss accepts in an inner loop; 14e8f0a75e "Some system calls can be called later to improve accept() performance" moves `configureBlocking(false)` into the worker's register task. • 6615d72db0 2014-04-11 `SO_REUSEPORT` on `EpollServerSocketChannel`; 199d2b499c on `EpollDatagramChannel`.

### netty-150 — Force data to disk once per logical unit, not per write
Lens: io · Source: Netty
Principle: Issue `fsync`/`force()` after the whole file or transaction has been written instead of after each chunk.
Why it is faster: A disk flush waits for the device and costs milliseconds on rotating media and still significant time on SSDs. Durability is usually required only at the end of the logical unit, so flushing per chunk multiplies latency without adding safety.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: wrap bulk inserts in one transaction (one commit, one fsync); WAL group commit. • Embedded: batch flash page writes and commit once, which also saves erase cycles. • Web / backend: upload handlers that fsync once after the file completes.
Watch out: a crash mid-unit loses the whole unit; that must be acceptable (or recoverable) for the data in question.
Seen in Netty: 65876fa7fb 2012-04-30 `DiskFileUpload` moves `fileChannel.force(false)` out of the write loop: "optimize force() position after multiple writes not at each step".

### netty-151 — Schedule submissions around completions
Lens: io · Source: Netty
Principle: In a submit/complete queue model, submit immediately when a producer is blocked on queued work, and submit again right after processing completions, looping until both queues are quiet.
Why it is faster: Completions free buffers and generate new submissions. If those new submissions wait for the next loop iteration, every round trip gains a loop's worth of latency and throughput drops. Submitting on backpressure and after each completion batch keeps the device or kernel busy.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: NVMe and io_uring storage engines that resubmit reads as soon as completions free slots. • Data / databases: async prefetchers that issue the next read when a completion arrives instead of on a timer.
Watch out: processing completions inline from a submission path can recurse deeply; bound it.
Seen in Netty: 4d868a6da3 2025-01-18 io_uring: when a channel becomes unwritable, submit right away and process its completions inline: 28.07 to 37.42 Gbps. • 595a07d432 2025-01-18 an extra submit after the completion queue is processed: about 74.8 to 140 Gbps on a single connection; a9ecd1cc2f restricts inline runs to the write's own completion to bound stack depth. • c4ac6613b3 2025-08-12 loops submit and complete until quiescent.

### netty-152 — Parse wire formats with index scans, not regexes, splits or temporary strings
Lens: networking · Source: Netty
Principle: Find field boundaries by scanning for the delimiter with an index and extract each field once; never run a general-purpose regex, `split` or format routine on a hot protocol path.
Why it is faster: Regex engines allocate matchers and can backtrack; `split` allocates an array and a substring per piece even when only one piece is needed; number-to-string conversions for comparison allocate a string to throw away. A hand-written scan touches each byte once and allocates only the final values. The same applies at startup, where compiling patterns runs before the JIT has warmed up.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: parse headers, cookies and query strings with `indexOf` loops; in Go use `strings.IndexByte` and `strconv.AppendInt` instead of `regexp` and `fmt`. • Native / systems: llhttp or picohttpparser style parsers; `std::to_chars` into the output buffer. • Data / databases: CSV and log ingesters that tokenise with a scan rather than regexes. • Embedded: AT-command and NMEA parsers that walk the buffer in place instead of `sscanf`.
Watch out: hand-written parsers are where request-smuggling and overflow bugs live; keep them small, fuzz them, and keep the strictness the regex gave you.
Seen in Netty: a074c0a95d 2009-03-14 `QueryStringDecoder` uses `uri.indexOf('?')` instead of `split("\\?")`. • 160866e1a0 2009-06-15 "HttpMessageDecoder does not use regular expressions to decode the initial line and headers anymore": `findNonWhitespace`/`findWhitespace` scans replace two patterns; ee8ffec265 reads headers with a CR/LF switch and one size check. • 57948458e7 2017-05-19 `NetUtil` validates IP addresses without `StringTokenizer` or allocation, 2-10x faster. • 9968e94257 2025-07-04 `HttpContentCompressor.determineEncoding` scans `Accept-Encoding` for commas without `split`; ee12997f38 writes chunk-size hex digits directly into the output instead of `Long.toHexString`; c680c29d08 compares a length numerically instead of via `Long.toString`. • 4eb5ca3c90 2026-03-18 `WebSocketServerHandshaker13` calls `digest.update` twice instead of concatenating key and GUID: 320 to 200 B/op, 200 to 165 ns/op; eaf38df0eb MQTT topic validation in one character loop, 24.5 to 14.0 ns. • 0b576697bc 2025-06-19 no `Pattern.compile` in `HttpVersion`'s static initialiser.

### netty-153 — Make decoders resumable: keep progress across partial input
Lens: networking · Source: Netty
Principle: Write stream decoders as explicit state machines that save where they stopped (state, scan offset, partially decoded fields) and continue from there when more bytes arrive, instead of restarting the message.
Why it is faster: Restarting from the start of a message whenever input runs out re-parses the prefix on every packet: O(fragments x length), quadratic in the worst case, and with exception-based "need more data" signalling each restart also pays for unwinding. Remembering the last scanned offset or the decoded prefix makes the total work linear in the input size.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: streaming JSON, multipart and Redis protocol parsers that keep a cursor across chunks. • Native / systems: llhttp-style callback parsers; resumable TLS record and protobuf readers. • Embedded: byte-at-a-time UART protocol state machines that never re-scan the frame. • Data / databases: wire-protocol clients that resume parsing a large result row across reads.
Watch out: saved state must be reset exactly at message boundaries and on errors; stale offsets after buffer compaction are a classic bug.
Seen in Netty: 70684c2247 2017-09-13 `LineBasedFrameDecoder` remembers the offset already searched for end-of-line, making large-frame decoding O(n). • fda8808210 2014-11-04 `HttpObjectDecoder` moves from the replaying (throw and restart) decoder to an explicit state machine with reusable line and header parsers: about 177% faster in `HttpRequestDecoderBenchmark` (cff98fff51). 5f94d7a319 (LZF) and 60acd54c7e (WebSocket) made the same change. • 1c230405fd 2020-11-19 `HttpPostMultipartRequestDecoder`: per-byte `readByte()` was over 95% of the profile; a bulk `bytesBefore` search plus a remembered position made it about 82% faster in the default leak-detection mode; 1529ef1794 applied it to CRLF and delimiter search. • 457faeea06 2022-03-15 `LengthFieldBasedFrameDecoder` keeps the decoded frame length in a field across partial reads. • c8ed1fe7e3 2026-11-02 QPACK keeps the Huffman-decoded header name (`pendingName`) between `decode()` calls instead of rewinding to the start of the instruction.

### netty-154 — Recognise well-known tokens cheaply and return shared instances
Lens: networking · Source: Netty
Principle: Before generic matching, filter by length and first byte, compare short tokens as packed integers or by reference, and on a match return a pre-built shared object instead of allocating a new string.
Why it is faster: A handful of tokens (methods, common header names, protocol versions) make up most of the traffic. A switch on the first character or length prunes candidates with one branch; packing 4 or 8 bytes into an int or long turns a byte loop into one compare; identity comparison against interned constants skips content comparison entirely. Returning the cached constant removes the allocation and makes later comparisons identity checks too.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: match HTTP methods with a u32 load and compare; FourCC and magic-number checks. • Web / backend: intern common header names once at startup and compare by reference in middleware. • Data / databases: SQL tokenisers that switch on length and first letter before keyword comparison.
Watch out: packed-integer compares depend on byte order and on reading past the token safely; reference equality is only valid when every producer uses the interned instance.
Seen in Netty: a979433f91 2009-06-15 header continuation detection switches on `line.charAt(0)` instead of `startsWith`; 2ac79dcb74 `HttpClientCodec` switches on the method's first character before `equals`. • acb28e3ac8 2013-07-30 detects `HttpVersion` without `toUpperCase()` by matching known versions first. • 2e433889b2 2019-02-04 `DateFormatter.tryParseMonth` dispatches on characters directly instead of 12 `regionMatches` calls: 3-15% faster date parsing. • 0daaec3ac2 2023-01-27 `HttpRequestDecoder` compares against `GET_AS_INT`, `HTTP_1_1_AS_LONG`, `HOST_AS_INT` and similar constants after a switch on first character and length, returning `AsciiString.cached("Host")` and friends; e2c8a97747 adds `ACCEPT_AS_LONG`. • cce3d5b636 2024-01-31 the HPACK static table is built from the pseudo-header constants, and lookup does `switch(length)` then `==`; 3b81f2c294 looks up pseudo-headers before validating them; 664f20315c `HttpMethod.valueOf` compares against interned "GET"/"POST".

### netty-155 — Remove waits the protocol makes optional
Lens: networking · Source: Netty
Principle: Turn on transport and TLS features that remove round trips or deliberate delays: disable Nagle for request/response traffic, send data in the SYN, start sending before the handshake completes, and resume TLS sessions.
Why it is faster: Latency on a new connection is dominated by round trips, not CPU. Nagle's algorithm holds small writes until the previous segment is acknowledged, which interacts badly with delayed ACK. TCP Fast Open carries the first bytes (here the TLS ClientHello) in the SYN; TLS False Start sends application data one round trip earlier; session resumption skips the full key exchange.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: enable TLS session tickets and 0-RTT where replay is safe; keep HTTP/2 connections alive and reuse them. • Data / databases: drivers that set NODELAY and pool connections instead of reconnecting. • Embedded: IoT devices resuming TLS sessions to save radio time and energy on reconnect.
Watch out: NODELAY without application-level write coalescing sends many tiny packets; early data and False Start have replay and downgrade caveats, and middleboxes may drop TFO SYNs.
Seen in Netty: 39357f3835 2013-01-31 `TCP_NODELAY` (and SCTP_NODELAY) on by default where the platform supports it reliably. • 32178fac7f 2020-08-18 `SSL_MODE_ENABLE_FALSE_START` when JDK-compatibility mode is off. • 56adab2743 2021-02-15 client TCP Fast Open on epoll; `SslHandler` writes the ClientHello into the unflushed outbound buffer early so it rides in the SYN. • 215329f0f5 2023-08-24 native TLS client session cache on by default.

### netty-156 — Coalesce protocol frames and batch control messages
Lens: networking · Source: Netty
Principle: Merge consecutive small payload writes into fewer, larger protocol frames at flush time, and accumulate control updates (window updates, acknowledgements) into one message per flush.
Why it is faster: Every frame carries a fixed header and triggers per-frame processing on both peers. Merging DATA frames saves the header bytes and the peer's per-frame callbacks; batching window updates replaces a stream of tiny control frames with one. A queue that tracks buffers together with their completion promises lets frames be merged or split at arbitrary boundaries without losing who to notify.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: gRPC or WebSocket servers that merge small messages written in the same tick into one frame. • Data / databases: Kafka-style producers batching records per partition; replication protocols acknowledging a range instead of each record. • Embedded: BLE or CAN stacks that pack several small signals into one PDU.
Watch out: large merged frames can hurt fairness between streams and add head-of-line delay; cap the merged size.
Seen in Netty: 8271c8afcc 2015-04-23 HTTP/2 encoders and decoders stop flushing explicitly, leaving flush decisions to the caller. • 05ce33f5ca 2015-06-04 HTTP/2 flow controllers merge queued DATA into one frame per flush (9 bytes saved per frame) and batch WINDOW_UPDATE frames. • cdbf43596d 2015-06-30 / 60c59f39af `CoalescingBufferQueue` merges and splits (buffer, promise) pairs across frame boundaries.

### netty-157 — Fill TLS records: copy small plaintext writes together before encrypting
Lens: networking · Source: Netty
Principle: Gather consecutive small application writes into one contiguous buffer up to the maximum record size before handing them to the TLS engine, and skip engine calls that cannot produce anything.
Why it is faster: Each TLS record adds a header and authentication tag (tens of bytes) and costs one encrypt call with its fixed setup. Many small writes become many small records: more bytes on the wire and more crypto calls. Copying small plaintext into one buffer is cheap compared with that, and it beats a composite buffer here because the engine wants contiguous input.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: buffer writes in user space before `SSL_write`, sized to the record limit. • Web / backend: QUIC packet coalescing; reverse proxies that buffer small upstream chunks before re-encrypting. • Embedded: DTLS on constrained links, where per-record overhead is a large share of each packet.
Watch out: aggregation needs a flush trigger or latency-sensitive small messages wait; full-size records also delay the first byte the peer can decrypt.
Seen in Netty: 86e653e04f 2017-05-08 `SslHandlerCoalescingBufferQueue` and `setWrapDataSize` aggregate plaintext up to 16 KB per wrap. • d7c977dd71 2017-12-03 prefers copying into one buffer over building a `CompositeByteBuf` when aggregating. • ab65b1e664 2017-02-03 / 623cb8d95d skip `wrap`/`unwrap` calls that have no side effects.

### netty-158 — Give the crypto engine as much work per call as it can take
Lens: networking · Source: Netty
Principle: Pass many records, or many input buffers, to each encrypt/decrypt call instead of one at a time, and flush only when the call actually produced output.
Why it is faster: Each engine call has fixed overhead: state checks, result objects and, with a native library, a JNI crossing. Feeding one record per call pays that per record. Passing a vector of input buffers also removes the copy that would coalesce them first.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: call `SSL_read` with large buffers; AEAD over iovecs in QUIC stacks. • Web / backend: batch calls into WebCrypto or a WASM codec instead of one call per small chunk. • Data / databases: encrypt pages in batches rather than per row.
Watch out: bigger batches need bigger output buffers; size them from the exact overhead (below) instead of the worst case.
Seen in Netty: 11f95c78e2 2013-11-08 `SslHandler` unwraps as many records as fit per call, estimates buffer sizes better, and flushes only when needed. • 9125060752 2014-05-18 undoes one-record-at-a-time feeding for OpenSSL: no intermediate arrays, fewer JNI calls. • 1bb818bb59 2014-11-24 `OpenSslEngine.unwrap(ByteBuffer[], ...)`: `SslHandler` passes all components of a composite inbound buffer, removing the cumulation copy. • 783567420f 2016-05-27 `wrap` keeps encrypting further source buffers until the destination would overflow; c68a6adce0 wraps and unwraps multiple TLS packets per call.

### netty-159 — Run compressors and checksums over the buffer you already have, in bulk
Lens: networking · Source: Netty
Principle: Feed compression and checksum routines the existing memory (array with offset, or native buffer) through their bulk APIs, stage through bounded pooled buffers when a copy is unavoidable, and size output from the input.
Why it is faster: Copying input into a temporary `byte[]` doubles memory traffic and allocates per call; a checksum computed byte by byte through a per-byte API can be orders of magnitude slower than a block API. Input-sized temporaries bypass the pool and cause GC pressure, so fixed chunks (up to 64 KiB) keep staging pool-eligible. Output estimated from input size (for deflate about 1.001x + 12 bytes) avoids both repeated growth and worst-case allocation.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: point zlib's `next_in` at the receive buffer; use hardware CRC32C over whole blocks. • Web / backend: pass `Uint8Array` views (not copies) to WASM codecs; stream compression in fixed chunks. • Data / databases: checksum whole WAL pages or mmap'd blocks rather than records byte by byte; size compression output with `compressBound()`.
Watch out: some library APIs silently fall back to slow paths (reflection, per-byte loops) on older runtimes; benchmark the path actually taken.
Seen in Netty: 0f7379157a 2014-01-30 `JdkZlibEncoder` passes `(array, offset, len)` to the deflater instead of copying; 65686b6c83 does the same for SPDY header compression. • 4d2b78ca3c 2014-06-26 `JdkZlibEncoder` sizes output as `ceil(len*1.001)+12` plus header and drops a fixed 8 KiB member array. • 87551fc751 2016-07-19 `ByteBufChecksum` uses `Checksum.update(ByteBuffer)` or a byte processor over buffer memory for Snappy CRC32C and zlib CRC32; bb3c4a43d8 for LZ4; 9151739577 bzip2 encoder reads via `forEachByte` without copying. • 09158a6420 2019-06-18 `Lz4XXHash32` uses xxHash32's ByteBuffer block API (about 25x throughput difference); db1e662933 fixes a reflection bug so CRC32/Adler32 actually use the ByteBuffer overload. • d80a34ecf8 2021-12-08 `JdkZlibEncoder` stages direct input through a pooled heap buffer instead of a new array per encode; ef9a583ee9 caps input staging and initial output at 64K and feeds the deflater incrementally. • 569ede081f 2021-10-11 zlib compressor and decompressor pass ByteBuffers directly on Java 11+; 60c9cbf46a FastLZ works on direct buffers; ae079d1aed Brotli/LZ4 use the cached internal view instead of a new one.

### netty-160 — Skip an optional encoding when it saves too little
Lens: networking · Source: Netty
Principle: When a protocol allows a value to be sent either raw or compressed, compress only above a size threshold where the bytes saved justify the CPU spent.
Why it is faster: Entropy coding a short value saves a few bytes but costs a table walk per character on both sides. Below a threshold the CPU cost outweighs the bandwidth saving, especially on fast links.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: gzip minimum length for HTTP responses (do not compress tiny bodies). • Data / databases: page or column compression only above a size or ratio threshold. • Embedded: skip payload compression on small telemetry messages where the CPU cost exceeds the radio savings.
Watch out: on metered or very slow links the trade-off flips; make the threshold configurable.
Seen in Netty: e96c37dea7 2019-07-01 `HpackEncoder` disables Huffman coding for header values under 512 bytes (configurable): for small sensitive ASCII headers, encoding went from 2573 ns to about 224-435 ns.

### netty-161 — Validate at the trust boundary once, not on every hop
Lens: networking · Source: Netty
Principle: Let trusted pipelines turn off per-character validation, skip validation for values known to be valid (well-known constants, data copied from an already-validated container), and remember "already validated" on the object.
Why it is faster: Per-character header validation is a measurable share of HTTP decode time, and re-validating the same data when it is copied, aggregated or re-checked per write repeats that cost. Validating once where untrusted data enters keeps the safety while removing the repeats.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: validate JSON schemas at the API edge and pass typed objects inward; skip re-validation in internal RPC hops. • Data / databases: defer constraint checks during bulk loads of trusted data. • Native / systems: validate a packet header once, then use unchecked accessors downstream.
Watch out: disabling validation on data that can be attacker-influenced reopens header injection and smuggling; make "trusted" an explicit, documented choice.
Seen in Netty: 25f96b1644 2013-07-22 `DefaultChannelPromise` keeps a `validated` flag to "reduce overhead during writes because of ChannelPromise validation". • c4130e0cf7 2013-11-13 a `validateHeaders` flag on HTTP decoders, messages and headers, reported as "a 5k perf improvement" when disabled; 8edfde3f26 the same for SPDY. • 909d1f2c0f 2014-07-04 `HttpObjectAggregator` wraps the original message and reuses its headers instead of copying them into a new object, which would re-validate ("Less GC, faster"). • 6e108cb96a 2015-10-30 copying between headers of the same kind skips name validation and rehashing. • 3b81f2c294 2024-01-12 HTTP/2 looks up known pseudo-header names before validation, so they skip it.

### netty-162 — Keep wire data as bytes and let producers write into the destination
Lens: networking · Source: Netty
Principle: Do not convert pass-through protocol fields to text and back; keep them as byte buffers, and let content producers fill a caller-supplied buffer instead of returning a new one to copy.
Why it is faster: Every bytes-to-string conversion decodes a charset and allocates, and the reverse encodes and allocates again. For fields the application only forwards, both are waste. A producer that returns a fresh buffer forces the caller to copy it into the outbound buffer; writing straight into the destination removes that copy.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Redis or memcached proxies passing values as `[]byte`/`Buffer` untouched; `io.WriterTo`/`ReaderFrom` in Go. • Data / databases: wire protocols that copy column bytes straight into the network buffer without materialising strings. • Embedded: gateways that forward payloads between links without parsing them into structures.
Watch out: byte-typed fields push charset handling onto callers who do need text; document the encoding.
Seen in Netty: 2b32c3fb59 2012-03-11 "Eliminate some unnecessary byte copy / convertion": Redis status and error replies hold a buffer instead of a String. • 12898a2ef4 2012-06-10 `ChunkedInput.readChunk(ByteBuf)` fills the given buffer instead of returning a chunk, which "safe[s] a copy on most cases".

### netty-163 — Size per-connection protocol buffers from the exact protocol overhead
Lens: networking · Source: Netty
Principle: Compute buffer sizes from the specification's exact bound for the data at hand (pending bytes plus maximum record overhead) instead of a generous constant or the maximum record size.
Why it is faster: Per-connection buffers multiply by the connection count. Over-estimating TLS overhead at about 2 KB per record, or always allocating a full 16 KB record, inflates memory and cache footprint for every connection, while the true bound is under 100 bytes.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: WebSocket frame buffers sized from payload length plus the 2-14 byte header. • Embedded: protocol stacks with static buffers sized from the RFC maximum, not a rounded-up guess. • Data / databases: per-connection sort and network buffers sized to the actual query.
Watch out: an exact bound that is wrong by one byte causes buffer-overflow errors from the engine; derive it from the spec and test the edge cases.
Seen in Netty: ae61b12b9e 2014-05-18 with `OpenSslEngine`, `SslHandler` sizes output as `min(pending + MAX_ENCRYPTION_OVERHEAD, maxPacketBufferSize)` instead of always the maximum record size, lowering peak memory. • ed61ec0f83 2017-01-19 restores precise wrap-buffer sizing; 8066af386a uses an 86-byte maximum OpenSSL record overhead instead of about 2 KB.

### netty-164 — Inside codecs: read bits in wide chunks, decode with multi-bit tables, hash well
Lens: networking · Source: Netty
Principle: In bit-level codecs, keep a 64-bit bit buffer refilled in large units, decode several bits per table lookup instead of walking a tree bit by bit, and use a well-dispersing hash in match finders.
Why it is faster: A bit reader refilled one byte at a time does a buffer access every 8 bits; a 64-bit accumulator does one per 32 or more. Huffman decoding one bit per step costs a branch per bit; a state table indexed by 4-bit nibbles does a quarter of the steps with no data-dependent branches. In LZ compressors a weak hash (addition instead of multiplication) clusters entries, which costs both speed and compression ratio.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: deflate, brotli and video bitstream readers with 64-bit bit buffers and multi-bit decode tables. • Web / backend: HPACK/QPACK implementations in any language. • Data / databases: LZ-family page compressors and hash joins benefit from a multiplicative hash; varint/bit-packed column decoders from wide refills. • Embedded: nibble-table Huffman decoders that fit in a few hundred bytes of flash.
Watch out: wide refills must not read past the end of the input; larger decode tables can fall out of cache on small cores.
Seen in Netty: c13419750d 2014-07-25 bzip2 bit reader and writer use a long buffer and read up to 32 bits at a time: 13% faster. • 287e147395 2017-07-31 Snappy uses a multiplicative hash and actually applies the computed table size; one test output shrank from 180 to 175 bytes. • 65d8ecc3a0 2019-07-02 `HpackHuffmanDecoder` decodes 4 bits at a time from an int-packed state table: small ASCII inputs 426k to 473k ops/s, medium 57.8k to 66.7k; 760bd4ab90 folds the flags into the table entry.

### netty-165 — Resolver hygiene: share in-flight queries, batch sends, allocate ids in O(1)
Lens: networking · Source: Netty
Principle: Attach concurrent lookups for the same name to the query already in flight, send related queries together so they can share a syscall, and allocate query ids from a free-id structure rather than by random probing.
Why it is faster: Under load many callers ask for the same hostname at once; issuing a query each multiplies upstream traffic and latency. Separate flushes for A and AAAA queries prevent them from sharing one `sendmmsg`. Picking a random id and probing until it is free degrades as the id space fills and can stall the event loop.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Go `singleflight` or request collapsing in a CDN or cache layer for any expensive lookup. • Native / systems: NAT port and transaction-id allocators using free lists instead of random probing. • Data / databases: coalesce identical concurrent cache-miss loads into one backend query.
Watch out: sharing an in-flight query shares its failure and its timeout too; DNS ids must stay unpredictable, so keep randomness in the free-id scheme.
Seen in Netty: 99aa51b74a 2018-11-21 `DnsNameResolver` flushes once for A and AAAA queries so they go out in one `sendmmsg`. • ad6b3f61e0 2023-02-08 optionally shares an in-flight query for the same hostname among callers. • af33de629f 2024-01-23 `DnsQueryIdSpace`: 4 lazily created buckets of 16k ids with randomised push-back replace a random-probe loop that "block[ed] the eventloop" with many queries in flight. • 4021eadab2 2026-09-10 search-domain deduplication with a `LinkedHashSet`: 4,096 domains went from 12,196 to 147 us.

### netty-166 — Distribute stream bandwidth with a fair-queuing scheduler, not tree walks
Lens: networking · Source: Netty
Principle: For multiplexed protocols with stream priorities, allocate send capacity with a weighted fair queue (priority queues and virtual time per node) and integer math, instead of traversing and sorting the whole priority tree on each write.
Why it is faster: Walking and sorting every stream in the dependency tree per allocation is O(streams log streams) and gets slow with many streams. A weighted fair queue touches only the streams that have data, and dropping unnecessary sorts and floating-point rounding removes further per-write work.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: fq_codel-style packet schedulers; QUIC stream schedulers. • Data / databases: I/O schedulers sharing disk bandwidth between tenants or query classes. • Web / backend: per-tenant fair queues in API gateways.
Watch out: fair-queuing state per stream costs memory; idle streams should drop out of the queues.
Seen in Netty: cb63e34bda 2015-03-30 removes a child sort from HTTP/2 priority handling; ba9f214303 removes a `Math.ceil`. • 79ab756fa3 2015-11-20 `UniformStreamByteDistributor` uses a single queue. • 904e70a4d4 2015-12-02 `WeightedFairQueueByteDistributor` replaces tree traversal, fixing its performance problems.

### netty-167 — Use constant-time comparison only where secrecy needs it
Lens: networking · Source: Netty
Principle: Compare secret values (tokens, sensitive headers) in constant time, and use fast early-exit comparison for everything else.
Why it is faster: Constant-time equality always reads every byte so timing reveals nothing; ordinary equality stops at the first difference, which is usually the first byte for non-matching keys. Applying constant-time comparison to every table lookup pays the full cost on every miss.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: constant-time compare for session ids, API keys and HMACs; plain compare for route names and cache keys. • Embedded: constant-time PIN and MAC checks, fast compare for command parsing.
Watch out: misclassifying a secret as non-sensitive opens a timing side channel; default to constant time when unsure.
Seen in Netty: 03ad809a3b 2019-10-23 `HpackEncoder` checks whether a header is sensitive first, then uses the fast comparison for the rest. • 613cfcb600 2020-11-30 QPACK static-table lookup skips a redundant constant-time equals (along with indexing names): single-match name lookup 4.86M to 36.3M ops/s.

### netty-168 — Measure every optimisation and revert the ones that do not pay
Lens: observability · Source: Netty
Principle: Treat each optimisation as a hypothesis: measure it against the baseline, keep it only if it wins on the workload that matters, and remove it (or narrow it) when it does not, including changes that were already merged.
Why it is faster: Plausible optimisations often do nothing or cost more than they save: a buffer pool that uses memory without speeding anything up, packing that the CPU does not care about, a busy-spin that helps only tiny connection counts. Removing them recovers memory and simplicity. Treating measured regressions as bugs stops the slow accumulation of small losses.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: require a benchmark or load-test number in every performance PR; add a CI perf gate that flags regressions. • Native / systems: inspect the generated assembly (Compiler Explorer, `perf annotate`) before hand-optimising what the compiler may already do. • Data / databases: A/B new caching or pooling layers on production-like data and remove them if hit rates do not justify the memory. • Embedded: measure cycles with a hardware timer on target, not on the desktop simulator.
Watch out: a change can lose on a microbenchmark and win in production (or the reverse); decide on the workload you care about and record why.
Seen in Netty: 4d17db6eb1 2008-09-28 found a plain `LinkedList` "same or better" than a custom queue. • aa4b8a2a6c 2010-02-23 kept `FileRegion` after measuring that a mapped buffer "does not outperform transferTo()". • a9c90d0e4a 2010-02-18 reverted a direct-buffer pool: it "doesn't seem to improve the performance as much as additional memory consumption... often led to OOME"; 263e1ed601 found pooling on reads did not help at all; 545acfdf42 later brought back only the read-side pool. • 83f1b5fa35 2012-05-20 spun on `selectNow()` before blocking ("boost performance dramatically when the number of connections is very small"); 06a6b9b3a9 removed it a few days later: "Needs more experiments". • c0462c0c3b 2014-06-26 went back from packed `short[]` to two `byte[]` arrays because packing gave no gain; ea3dac0753 dropped a random tree-branch choice after measuring the same speed without an RNG. • 93c306602a 2014-07-14 removed redundant range checks that had caused a measured regression; ba6ce5449e restored the older header map design to recover a regression from a memory-saving redesign. • 81fee66c78 2015-05-19 accepted a slightly slower same-thread path for faster cross-thread frees, stating the trade-off in the commit. • 46cff5399f 2019-12-11 reverted batched `epoll_ctl` after correctness problems; cb084854a6 reverted an allocation-removal change. • 31c033a1d9 2025-05-14 added a fast-path override for `setCharSequence` and recorded that the sibling `getCharSequence` was not worth overriding; 16835704b4 confirmed with PrintAssembly that the JIT did not fuse two loops before fusing them by hand. • a7fbb6f846 2026-03-24 batching zlib decoder output recovered a 30-35% throughput regression.

### netty-169 — Let profiles of real workloads pick the targets, and profile allocations too
Lens: observability · Source: Netty
Principle: Choose what to optimise from CPU and allocation profiles of real or realistic workloads, express findings as a share of samples or bytes, and keep native code profilable.
Why it is faster: Profiles show where time and garbage actually go, which is often somewhere nobody suspected: logging boxing, a reference count, a view object allocated per write. Allocation profiles matter as much as CPU in garbage-collected runtimes because allocation cost is paid later in GC pauses. Native code compiled without frame pointers shows up as unattributed time in flame graphs.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: continuous profiling (async-profiler, pprof, Pyroscope) with allocation mode on; Chrome DevTools allocation sampling for Node. • Native / systems: build release binaries with frame pointers so `perf` flame graphs stay accurate. • Data / databases: profile with production query mixes, not synthetic single-query loops. • Embedded: sample the program counter from a timer interrupt or use the debug probe's trace to find hot functions on target.
Watch out: sampling profilers have safepoint and inlining biases; confirm a suspected hotspot with a targeted benchmark before rewriting it.
Seen in Netty: 35b9aa9302 2015-04-02 HTTP/2 listener `LinkedHashSet` iterators were about 1.6% of heap in a stream-churn test, so the set became an `ArrayList`. • 8d664fa0fd 2016-12-06 compiles the native transport with `-fno-omit-frame-pointer` so flame graphs walk native stacks. • 83a19d5650 2017-09-25 reference counting was about 1% of CPU in gRPC, which motivated switching to `getAndAdd`. • 30a02b441a 2018-05-15 autoboxing in HTTP/2 frame logging showed up in gRPC profiles. • 0bea8ecf5d 2018-08-07 a Spark profile showed `put()` hot in composite `nioBuffer()`. • fedc291450 2021-02-23 a swapped-byte-order view was about 9% of all allocations in HTTP/3 profiling. • 627c3b7392 2026-01-08 TechEmpower flame graphs led to submitting `SEND` instead of `WRITE`. • 61ab453cc5 2026-09-10 removed about 79% of sampled promise allocations in `SslHandler.wrap()`; a0faa5e3c7 JFR showed 84 lambda events (about 78 MB) on the event loop drop to 0 (about 17 MB) at about 11k req/s. • b23c19dfc6 2026-10-15 address-validity checks were 3.3% of I/O-thread CPU in a Pulsar broker; ee7ab44061 NIO views had been 13% of that broker's allocations. • dfcf1f9f0a 2026-11-13 a renegotiation check was about 2% of CPU and 5fe1f1fd65 `SSL_pending` about 1.7% of samples; 3636289c53 `String.format` took 37 of the startup CPU samples, reduced to 1.

### netty-170 — Commit a microbenchmark with the change, and make it honest
Lens: observability · Source: Netty
Principle: Ship a reproducible microbenchmark next to each performance change, and design it so the runtime cannot cheat: varied inputs drawn at random, the method under test not inlined into the harness, the same threads and setup as production.
Why it is faster: A committed benchmark lets reviewers reproduce the claim and later changes detect regressions. A benchmark that feeds one input lets the branch predictor learn it and the JIT specialise for it, so results look better than reality. Running thread-local code on the wrong thread type, or with assertions or leak detection set differently from production, measures a different code path entirely.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Benchmark.js or Vitest bench files next to the parser they measure, fed real request samples. • Native / systems: Google Benchmark or Criterion with `DoNotOptimize`/`black_box` and randomised inputs. • Data / databases: keep a benchmark corpus of realistic rows and queries in the repo. • Embedded: on-target cycle-count tests in the test suite with inputs that exercise every branch.
Watch out: microbenchmarks miss cache pressure, GC and contention from the rest of the system; pair them with end-to-end runs.
Seen in Netty: b47fc77522 2012-12-06 the pooled allocator landed with a Caliper microbenchmark module and `ByteBufAllocatorBenchmark`; 510692d7e7 added `NioGatheringWriteBenchmark`. • cff98fff51 2014-11-07 `HttpRequestDecoderBenchmark` backed the 177% decoder rewrite. • c9364616c8 2015-10-29 fixed the FastThreadLocal benchmark to run on `FastThreadLocalThread`; only then did it show the real gap: 55,452 vs 35,482 ops/s (+56%). • 517da28740 2021-02-05 `DecodeHexBenchmark` draws from a large input set at random per iteration and marks the benchmarked method not inlinable; 414c0f2e50 adjusted header benchmarks for JMH's changed default iteration time. • 142027220a 2023-09-22 `HttpStatusValueOfBenchmark`, 5a58f0431e `ByteBufZeroingBenchmark` and 09b4ea6a1f `VarintDecodingBenchmark` shipped with the changes they justify.

### netty-171 — Confirm micro-wins with end-to-end load and tail-latency numbers
Lens: observability · Source: Netty
Principle: Re-measure each change with a full-stack load generator (requests per second, bandwidth, p50/p99 latency) or a workload simulator, because component speedups do not always reach the system.
Why it is faster: End-to-end numbers include syscalls, cache contention and the interactions a microbenchmark leaves out. They show which micro-wins matter (a 6.7% request-rate gain from a cheaper iovec build) and which do not move the needle. For memory work, a simulator that replays allocation patterns shows footprint directly.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: wrk2, k6 or vegeta runs with latency percentiles in each performance PR. • Data / databases: TPC-style or replayed production workloads, not only operator microbenchmarks. • Native / systems: iperf/tcpkali for bandwidth, and simulator replays for allocator footprint. • Embedded: measure end-to-end message latency and power on hardware, not just function timings.
Watch out: load generators can saturate before the server does (coordinated omission, client CPU limits); verify the generator is not the bottleneck.
Seen in Netty: 88bd6e7a93 2014-07-22 wrk, 256 pipelined connections: 2,887,885 to 3,080,169 req/s from building iovecs off-heap. • 50af9b916c 2014-11-21 wrk plaintext 3,846,702 to 4,032,584 req/s with composite cumulation; 3e42292d8b 4,449,510 to 4,508,150 req/s from LIFO caches; ed98ce27e1 4,553,438 to 4,634,343 req/s from a shared native epoll event array. • 1dff107de1 2018-09-11 gRPC p50 round trip 64.2 to about 62 us from skipping timer re-arms. • e177b74874 2019-07-04 h2load 620k to 642k req/s from a byte-processor Huffman decoder. • a4acd66fee 2025-02-19 tcpkali about 37 to 70 Gbps from eager buffer-ring refill. • 67a46b102f 2022-11-16 Reactor-Netty H2C 132k to 380k req/s from per-event-loop Cleaners. • 07bf4ad46a 2025-10-20 an allocator simulator showed 5,185,536 to 4,399,104 bytes at 128 live buffers for the small-heap policy. • b6fad79c6a 2026-10-27 startup measured with CDS and AOT: 83.7 to 69.3 ms and 37.2 to 24.3 ms.

### netty-172 — Build log and debug output only when it will be emitted
Lens: observability · Source: Netty
Principle: Check the log level before formatting messages, boxing arguments, hex-dumping buffers or matching exception text, so disabled diagnostics cost one branch.
Why it is faster: Even with logging off, string building, varargs arrays, autoboxing and hex dumps run if the call site does not check first. On per-frame paths that work can dominate.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: parameterised logging (SLF4J `{}`), lazy suppliers, or `if (debug.enabled)` in Node. • Native / systems: logging macros that skip argument evaluation when disabled (Rust `tracing`, glog `VLOG`). • Embedded: compile out debug prints in release builds; avoid `printf` formatting in ISRs.
Watch out: guards duplicated by hand drift from the actual log call; prefer APIs that evaluate arguments lazily.
Seen in Netty: f0a96e7417 2009-05-21 "no need to create a string when there's nothing to log". • 44615f6cb2 2015-03-12 `Http2FrameLogger` skips hex dumps and varargs when disabled ("MUCH faster"). • fe8ecea366 2017-07-26 skips hex dumps when logging is disabled; 54a6b0c542 matches `SSLException` messages only when debug logging is on. • 30a02b441a 2018-05-15 wraps HTTP/2 frame-logger calls in `isEnabled` checks after boxing showed up in gRPC profiles.

### netty-173 — Sample diagnostics and keep their bookkeeping lock-free
Lens: observability · Source: Netty
Principle: Track only a sample of resources (1 in N, or with exponentially decreasing probability), with uncontended, approximate counters and concurrent structures, so diagnostics can stay enabled in production.
Why it is faster: Tracking every allocation with a stack trace in a shared, locked structure serialises all threads and multiplies the cost of allocation. Sampling cuts the work by N; a power-of-two interval makes the check a mask; a per-instance, non-volatile counter avoids cache-line ping-pong; recording with probability 2^-n keeps O(log N) records per N accesses while always keeping the first and last.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: sampled distributed tracing; sampled heap profiling (tcmalloc, Go `MemProfileRate`). • Native / systems: per-thread sampling counters in profilers and lock-contention trackers. • Data / databases: sample slow-query or lock-wait statistics rather than recording every event. • Embedded: record every Nth event into a trace buffer to bound overhead.
Watch out: sampling can miss rare leaks; keep a full-tracking mode for debugging sessions.
Seen in Netty: 337f5bbb8e 2013-01-11 `ResourceLeakDetector` samples with an interval of 113; 09b022e926 switches to `PhantomReference`. • cedcee3f42 2013-02-09 the sampling counter becomes per-instance and non-volatile to reduce contention; f4e128b807 makes the `disabled` flag non-volatile. • a47685b243 2015-10-28 forces the sampling interval to a power of two so the check is `(counter & mask) == 0`. • 42fba015ce 2016-10-21 replaces a shared counter and a synchronised linked list with `ThreadLocalRandom` sampling and a `ConcurrentHashMap`: before, the detector used about 3 CPU-seconds per wall-second in a 12-thread gRPC benchmark; fd0ae840b6 uses a singleton map value with identity equality. • 74adbcd25d 2017-09-18 removes all locks: access records go onto a lock-free stack with exponentially decreasing probability (about 20 records per 1M accesses). • a7d1dc362a 2015-04-18 `StringUtil.simpleClassName` avoids `Class.getPackage()`, which synchronises internally.

### netty-174 — Capture expensive diagnostic detail only when it will be reported
Lens: observability · Source: Netty
Principle: Store cheap handles (a Throwable without resolving its frames, a flag) on the hot path and do the expensive work (stack resolution, cleaner registration, debug-only checks) only when a problem is reported or when diagnostics are switched on.
Why it is faster: Resolving a stack trace into frame objects costs far more than capturing it, and most records are never reported. Debug-only checks such as duplicate-recycle detection, and cleaner or phantom registration for leak tracking, add synchronisation and allocation to every operation if they are always on.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: capture `new Error()` for async stack context only in debug mode; symbolise stacks lazily in error reporters. • Native / systems: store raw return addresses and symbolise offline; enable allocator debug checks with an environment variable. • Data / databases: keep lock-tracking detail only for transactions that end up in a deadlock report.
Watch out: the cheap handle must still hold enough to reconstruct the report later; capturing too little makes leaks undiagnosable.
Seen in Netty: 6732c6761b 2013-06-10 pooled buffers are recycled only when leak detection is disabled. • e57cf9d201 2014-03-12 the Recycler's `IdentityHashMap` duplicate-recycle check runs only with assertions enabled. • 4560197923 2017-09-17 `ResourceLeakDetector` calls `getStackTrace()` only when a leak is reported: recording went from 4.4k to 71.4k ops/s; 9df8082c39 allocates the record deque only at the ADVANCED level. • dc46a3918a 2022-12-08 netty5 attaches no Cleaner by default, only when leak detection needs it; allocator throughput roughly doubled on an M1.

### netty-175 — Design hot paths to stay fast when instrumentation is on
Lens: observability · Source: Netty
Principle: Prefer bulk operations over per-element calls on instrumented objects, because diagnostic wrappers (leak trackers, checked accessors) add cost per call, not per byte.
Why it is faster: A leak-tracking wrapper records or checks on every method call. Code that reads one byte per call multiplies that overhead by the payload size, so a debugging mode becomes unusably slow and hides real problems. Bulk searches and internal iteration call the wrapper once per operation.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: code that stays usable under Node's async hooks or OpenTelemetry auto-instrumentation by avoiding per-item instrumented calls. • Native / systems: keep ASan/Valgrind CI runs fast by using `memchr`/`memcpy` instead of byte loops through wrapped accessors. • Data / databases: batch calls into instrumented storage APIs so tracing overhead scales with operations, not rows.
Watch out: optimising only for the instrumented mode can distort the normal path; check both.
Seen in Netty: c13419750d 2014-07-25 the wider bzip2 bit reader was 13% faster normally and 55% faster with paranoid leak detection. • 6db59cc97e 2017-02-17 Base64 with a byte processor and packed reads made the handler module's leak-paranoid test build run 4x faster. • 1c230405fd 2020-11-19 multipart decoding with bulk `bytesBefore`: about 99% faster in PARANOID mode, which went from about 1000x slower than normal to at most about 10x.

### netty-176 — Keep metrics off the contended path
Lens: observability · Source: Netty
Principle: Derive metrics from events you already handle (chunk creation and destruction) instead of updating extra counters per operation, use striped counters for totals, and read estimates without locks.
Why it is faster: A single atomic counter updated by every thread is a cache line bouncing between cores. Striped counters (LongAdder) spread updates and sum on read, which suits counters that are written often and read rarely. Counting at coarse events (per chunk rather than per allocation) removes most updates, and a metric that is only an estimate does not need a lock to read.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: per-thread or per-core request counters aggregated at scrape time (Prometheus client sharding). • Native / systems: per-CPU statistics merged on read, as in kernel counters. • Data / databases: account buffer-pool memory per extent or segment, not per page access. • Embedded: increment counters in the ISR's local struct and aggregate in the background task.
Watch out: striped counters cost more memory and their sum is not an atomic snapshot; do not use them where an exact instantaneous value is needed.
Seen in Netty: 6a11330061 2022-01-12 the pinned-memory counter moves from `AtomicInteger` to a LongAdder-based counter. • d9850cd032 2024-03-15 `PoolArena#numPinnedBytes()` reads without a lock, since the result is an estimate anyway. • 80c9a41c25 2025-08-21 a LongAdder-based `ChunkRegistry` accounts memory at chunk creation and destruction; ae6017d555 then removes the per-magazine `AtomicLong usedMemory`.
